Focus on these areas, in this order:
1. EC2
   * What EC2 is
   * Instance, AMI, security group, key pair
   * Public vs private IP
   * Vertical vs horizontal scaling
   * Auto Scaling Group
   * Load Balancer + EC2 architecture
   * Basic interview question: “How would you deploy a Spring Boot app on AWS?”


2. S3
   * Object storage
   * Bucket/object
   * Static files, images, backups, logs
   * Versioning
   * Lifecycle policies
   * Presigned URLs
   * Public vs private bucket
   * S3 is not a filesystem/database
   * Very common question: “How would you upload/download files securely?”


3. RDS
   * Managed relational database
   * MySQL/PostgreSQL
   * Automated backups
   * Read replicas
   * Multi-AZ
   * Difference between Read Replica vs Multi-AZ
   * RDS vs running MySQL yourself on EC2


4. VPC — very important
You don’t need networking depth, but you absolutely should understand:
   * VPC
   * Public subnet
   * Private subnet
   * Internet Gateway
   * NAT Gateway
   * Route table
   * Security Group
   * Basic architecture:


   5. Internet → ALB → EC2/ECS in private subnet → RDS
Be able to explain why database servers should not normally be publicly accessible.


      6. IAM
      * User
      * Role
      * Policy
      * Principle of least privilege
      * IAM role vs access keys
      * Example: EC2 needs access to S3 → attach an IAM role instead of hardcoding AWS credentials.


      7. Load Balancer + Auto Scaling
      * ALB
      * Target groups
      * Health checks
      * Auto Scaling Group
      * Why applications should ideally be stateless
      * Session storage in Redis/database instead of one EC2 instance


      8. CloudWatch
      * Logs
      * Metrics
      * Alarms
      * CPU monitoring
      * Application logs
      * Triggering Auto Scaling/alarm notifications


      9. Lambda + API Gateway
Learn only basics:
         * Serverless functions
         * Event-driven
         * Pay-per-use
         * Good for short/event-based workloads
         * API Gateway → Lambda
         * Lambda limitations: cold starts, execution duration, statelessness


         10. SQS and SNS
This is especially useful because system-design interviews often mix these with AWS.
SQS
            * Queue
            * Producer → Queue → Consumer
            * Async processing
            * Retry
            * Dead Letter Queue


            11. SNS
            * Pub/sub
            * One message → multiple subscribers


            12. Know:
SNS = fan-out
SQS = queue/buffering


               13. DynamoDB
 Only learn basics unless the JD explicitly mentions it:
                  * NoSQL key-value/document database
                  * Partition key
                  * Sort key
                  * Very scalable
                  * DynamoDB vs RDS at a high level

AWS EC2 :
AWS EC2 : 






EC2 (Elastic Compute Cloud)


• Amazon EC2 is a web service that provides resizable compute capacity in the cloud.  
You can scale the compute capacity up and down as per the computing requirement changes.  
Amazon EC2 changes the economics of computing by allowing you to pay only for the resources that you actually use.  
• EC2 is an example of IAAS  
• We can scale the compute capacity up and down as per the computing requirement changes.


Features of EC2
• Provides scalable computing capacity  
• Launch as many or as few virtual servers as you need  
• Configure security and networking  
• Manage storage  


Amazon Machine Image (AMI)
• Its gives the basic information about the instance created also shown running states of instance.


Instance Type
• It store the information about instance such as ram, cpu etc.


Key Pairs
a) Used secure system to store the private key  
b) Public key is store by AWS  
c) Private key is store in secure place instead of password we can used a key pair to access the ec2  
d) Private key never be recovered  


Amazon EBS (Elastic Block Store) Volumes
• EC2 is a virtual server in a cloud while EBS is a virtual disc in a cloud.  
• Amazon EBS allows you to create storage volume and attach them in to the EC2 instances.


Security Group
• Used for handle the incoming and outgoing traffic.  
• We can modify the security groups according to our requirement.


Elastic IP Address
• This feature provides static public IPv4 addresses for dynamic cloud computing.  
• This ip's can be attached to EC2 instances.  
• Elastic IP can be quickly re-map to new instances, if your current EC2 instance fails


Tags
• It's a meta data that you can define for your amazon EC2 resources.  
• Tags helps you to classify our AWS resources in different ways If you have number of resources then tag help us to find the specific resource


Virtual Private Cloud
• It helps you to define in which amazon VPC you want to launch the instance.


Amazon EC2 Pricing
Amazon EC2 instances are priced per second according to five different options:


• On-demand (you can pay as per your demand like if you want service for 1hr then you can only able to pay for 1hr and then you can stop)  
• Spot instances (Spot Instances are useful for those applications that have flexible start and end time, discounted short-term capacity if available)  
• Savings Plans (commitment to a certain amount of usage)  
• Reserved Instances (discounted reserved long-term capacity, if your application is ready and you have long term plane then you can used this pricing)  
• Dedicated Hosts (A dedicated host is a physical server with EC2 instance capacity which is fully dedicated to your use., on-demand hourly or by reserved instances)


EC2 Interview Questions
EC2 Interview Questions


🔹 Basic EC2 Concepts


1. What is Amazon EC2 and how does it work?
   Amazon EC2 (Elastic Compute Cloud) is a web service that provides resizable compute capacity in the cloud. It allows users to run virtual machines (instances) with different configurations. Users can scale up/down based on demand.


2. What are the different instance types in EC2? How do you choose the right one?
   Instance types include General Purpose (t2, t3), Compute Optimized (c5), Memory Optimized (r5), Storage Optimized (i3), etc. The choice depends on workload requirements such as CPU, memory, and IOPS.


3. What is an AMI and what does it contain?
   Amazon Machine Image (AMI) is a template that contains the software configuration (OS, application server, applications) required to launch an instance.


4. How do you launch an EC2 instance using the AWS Management Console and CLI?
   - Console: Go to EC2 Dashboard > Launch Instance > Select AMI > Choose Instance Type > Configure > Launch.  
   - CLI: Use `aws ec2 run-instances` with required parameters.


5. What is the difference between EC2 and EBS?
   EC2 is a virtual server; EBS (Elastic Block Store) is virtual disk storage attached to EC2 instances.




🔹 Security and Access


6. What are security groups in EC2? How do they differ from NACLs?
   Security groups act as virtual firewalls for instances to control inbound/outbound traffic. NACLs are stateless and operate at the subnet level.


7. What is a key pair in EC2, and why is it important?
   Key pairs (public/private) are used to securely SSH into EC2 instances. AWS stores the public key, and the user downloads the private key.


8. How would you SSH into an EC2 instance?
   Use the command `ssh -i <key.pem> ec2-user@<public-ip>` with the private key file and the instance’s public IP.


9. What is an Elastic IP? Why and when would you use it?
   An Elastic IP is a static public IPv4 address that can be associated with EC2 instances. It is used to maintain a fixed IP address despite instance stops/restarts.


10. How do you securely connect to your EC2 instance without exposing the private key?
    Use EC2 Instance Connect (browser-based), bastion host, or Systems Manager Session Manager.




🔹 Storage and Volumes


11. What is the difference between EBS and Instance Store?
    EBS is persistent and survives instance stop/start. Instance Store is temporary and data is lost if the instance is stopped/terminated.


12. Can you resize or modify an EBS volume attached to an EC2 instance? How?
    Yes, use the AWS Console or CLI to modify the volume, then use OS-level tools (like `resize2fs` for Linux) to resize the filesystem.


13. What are the steps to back up an EC2 instance?
    - Create an AMI  
    - Take EBS snapshots  
    - Use AWS Backup service


14. How can you automate the creation of AMIs from EC2?
    Use AWS CLI/SDK with automation tools like Lambda or scheduled tasks using AWS EventBridge.






🔹 Networking


15. What is a VPC and how does EC2 fit into it?
    A Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) is a virtual network. EC2 instances are launched inside subnets of a VPC, allowing control over networking.


16. How do you configure EC2 instances in public and private subnets?
    - Public subnet: Attach Internet Gateway and public IP  
    - Private subnet: Use NAT Gateway for internet access without exposing public IP


17. How does EC2 handle inbound and outbound traffic?
    Traffic is controlled using security groups (instance level) and NACLs (subnet level).






🔹 Scaling and Availability


18. What is an Auto Scaling Group (ASG) in EC2?
    ASG automatically scales EC2 instances in or out based on defined metrics (CPU, traffic, etc.) to maintain availability and performance.


19. How do you implement High Availability (HA) with EC2?
    Deploy EC2 instances across multiple Availability Zones (AZs), use Elastic Load Balancers (ELB), and Auto Scaling.


20. What is the role of Load Balancer in EC2 setup?
    Distributes incoming application traffic across multiple EC2 instances in different AZs for fault tolerance.


21. How does EC2 work with Route 53 for DNS routing?
    Route 53 routes user requests to EC2 instances using domain names, enabling geo-based routing, failover, and weighted load balancing.




🔹 Pricing and Cost Optimization


22. What are the different pricing models for EC2?
    - On-demand  
    - Spot Instances  
    - Reserved Instances  
    - Savings Plans  
    - Dedicated Hosts


23. When should you use Spot Instances over On-Demand?
    For fault-tolerant and flexible applications that can handle interruptions, such as big data and CI/CD jobs.


24. What are Reserved Instances and when would you use them?
    They offer significant discounts for long-term (1-3 years) workloads with predictable usage.


25. How do you monitor and optimize EC2 costs in DevOps projects?
    Use Cost Explorer, Budgets, CloudWatch metrics, Rightsizing recommendations, and automation for start/stop unused resources.


🔹 Automation & DevOps Integration


26. How do you provision EC2 instances using Terraform/CloudFormation?
    Define infrastructure as code using `.tf` or YAML/JSON templates, specifying instance type, AMI, and key pairs.


27. How do you install software or run scripts automatically on EC2?
    Use user data scripts during instance launch to install packages and configure the environment.


28. How do you integrate EC2 into a CI/CD pipeline (e.g., CodeDeploy)?
    Use AWS CodeDeploy with deployment groups tied to EC2 instances to automatically push application updates.


29. What are user data scripts and how are they used in EC2?
    User data scripts run at the first boot of an EC2 instance, often used for bootstrapping software.


30. How can Jenkins deploy code to EC2 instances?
    Use SSH or CodeDeploy plugins, configure credentials, and trigger deployment jobs from Jenkins pipelines.


AWS EC2 - Interview Study Notes
AWS EC2 - Interview Study Notes
Beginner to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus


Goal of these notes
Understand EC2 well enough to explain how a production application is launched, secured, stored, scaled, monitored, and cost-optimized in AWS.
	1. What is Amazon EC2?
Amazon EC2 (Elastic Compute Cloud) provides resizable virtual compute capacity in AWS. An EC2 instance behaves like a virtual server where you choose the operating system, CPU/RAM class, networking, storage, security rules, and software you want to run.
                  * Typical workloads: Spring Boot/Node.js APIs, web servers, background workers, batch jobs, CI/CD runners, and custom software.
                  * You control the operating system and application. AWS manages the underlying physical infrastructure.
                  * EC2 is an Infrastructure-as-a-Service (IaaS) compute service.
  

Typical highly available EC2 web architecture across multiple Availability Zones.
2. Core EC2 Launch Building Blocks
When launching an EC2 instance, think in terms of these building blocks:
  

The main choices involved when launching an EC2 instance.
Concept
	What to Remember
	AMI
	The operating system / machine image used to boot the instance.
	Instance Type
	Defines CPU, memory, networking capability, and other hardware characteristics.
	VPC + Subnet
	Defines where the instance is placed in the AWS network.
	Security Group
	Stateful virtual firewall attached to the instance/network interface.
	Key Pair / Session Manager
	Ways to securely access the instance.
	IAM Role
	Temporary AWS permissions for applications running on the instance.
	User Data
	Bootstrap script or cloud-init configuration used at launch.
	EBS
	Persistent block storage commonly used for the root disk and data volumes.
	3. AMI (Amazon Machine Image)
An AMI is the image used to boot an EC2 instance. It contains the software configuration needed to start the machine.
                  * Examples: Amazon Linux, Ubuntu, Windows Server, or a custom company image.
                  * A custom AMI can contain your preinstalled runtime, agents, configuration, and application dependencies.
                  * AMIs are Region-specific, but they can be copied to another Region.
                  * A single AMI can be used to launch many identical instances.
Interview answer
AMI is the template/image; EC2 instance is the running virtual machine created from that image.
	4. EC2 Instance Types
An instance type determines the hardware resources available to your instance. You choose a family based on workload characteristics.
Category
	Designed For
	Examples of Workloads
	General Purpose
	Balanced CPU, memory, networking
	Web/API servers, dev/test, small-medium apps
	Compute Optimized
	Higher compute performance
	CPU-heavy services, encoding, batch compute
	Memory Optimized
	Large memory footprint
	In-memory databases, caches, analytics
	Storage Optimized
	High local storage throughput / IOPS
	Search, data processing, storage-heavy workloads
	Accelerated Computing
	GPU / specialized accelerators
	ML training/inference, graphics, HPC
	How to read an instance name
Example: m7g.large. The family letter indicates the workload family, the generation number indicates the generation, the suffix can indicate processor/platform characteristics, and the size (.large, .xlarge, etc.) indicates relative capacity.
Interview tip
You do not need to memorize every EC2 family. Know the major categories and explain how you would choose based on CPU, memory, network, and cost requirements.
	5. EC2 Storage: EBS vs Instance Store
EC2 can use persistent EBS storage and, for some instance types, temporary local instance-store storage.
Feature
	EBS
	Instance Store
	Persistence
	Persists independently of normal instance stop/start
	Data is tied to the physical host and is ephemeral
	Common use
	Root volume, application data, databases
	Temporary caches, scratch data, buffers
	Snapshot support
	Yes
	Not like EBS snapshots
	Attach model
	Network-attached block storage in an AZ
	Local storage physically attached to host
	After stop/start
	EBS data persists
	Instance-store data is lost
	Common EBS volume types to know:
                  * gp3 - general-purpose SSD; a common default choice.
                  * io2 - provisioned IOPS SSD for demanding, latency-sensitive workloads.
                  * st1 - throughput-optimized HDD for large sequential workloads.
                  * sc1 - cold HDD for infrequently accessed, throughput-oriented data.
6. EC2 Networking Basics
Every EC2 instance is launched into a VPC and a subnet.
                  * Private IPv4 address: used for communication inside the VPC.
                  * Public IPv4 address: can provide internet reachability when routing and security are configured.
                  * Elastic IP: a static public IPv4 address that can be associated with supported AWS resources.
                  * ENI (Elastic Network Interface): a virtual network interface containing IP addresses, MAC address, and security groups.
                  * Public subnet: route table typically has a route to an Internet Gateway.
                  * Private subnet: no direct route to an Internet Gateway for inbound internet access; outbound internet access commonly uses NAT.
7. Security Groups
A security group acts as a stateful virtual firewall for EC2. Inbound rules control allowed incoming traffic, and outbound rules control allowed outgoing traffic.
                  * Stateful: response traffic for an allowed connection is automatically permitted.
                  * Rules are allow rules; security groups do not use explicit deny rules.
                  * An instance can have multiple security groups.
                  * Prefer security-group references between application tiers instead of broad CIDR ranges where practical.
Purpose
	Protocol
	Port
	Recommended Source
	SSH to Linux
	TCP
	22
	Your office/VPN IP or controlled access path
	RDP to Windows
	TCP
	3389
	Your office/VPN IP or controlled access path
	HTTP
	TCP
	80
	ALB security group or required clients
	HTTPS
	TCP
	443
	ALB security group or required clients
	App Port
	TCP
	e.g. 8080
	Load balancer / upstream 
service security group
	Custom tcp : any port number : for custom app ports
8. Connecting to EC2
  

Common secure access patterns for EC2.
                  * Linux: SSH commonly uses a key pair. AWS stores the public key on the instance; you protect the private key.
                  * Windows: the key pair can be used to decrypt the Administrator password, then connect by RDP.
                  * Systems Manager Session Manager can provide interactive shell access without opening inbound SSH/RDP, if the instance is configured correctly.
Security best practice
Avoid opening SSH (22) or RDP (3389) to 0.0.0.0/0 in production. Restrict the source, use a VPN/bastion where needed, or prefer Systems Manager Session Manager.
	9. IAM Role / Instance Profile
Applications on EC2 often need to access services such as S3, CloudWatch, SQS, DynamoDB, or Secrets Manager. Use an IAM role associated with the EC2 instance through an instance profile.
                  * The application receives temporary AWS credentials automatically.
                  * Do not hardcode long-lived AWS access keys inside the server or source code.
                  * Apply least privilege: give the role only the permissions the application actually requires.
Common interview question
How should an EC2 application access S3? Attach an IAM role/instance profile with the required S3 permissions instead of storing access keys on the server.
	10. EC2 User Data
User data lets you provide launch-time instructions to an EC2 instance. On Linux, this is commonly a shell script or cloud-init configuration.
                  * Install packages at startup.
                  * Configure the application.
                  * Download artifacts or configuration.
                  * Start application services.
                  * Useful with launch templates and Auto Scaling so replacement instances configure themselves consistently.
Simple example:
#!/bin/bash
dnf install -y java-21-amazon-corretto
systemctl enable myapp
systemctl start myapp
Important
Do not place passwords or long-lived secrets in user data. Instance metadata and user data can be visible to software or users that already have access to the instance.
	11. Instance Metadata / IMDS
EC2 Instance Metadata Service (IMDS) provides information about the running instance from inside the instance, such as instance identity and temporary credentials associated with an IAM role.
                  * IMDSv2 uses session-oriented requests and is the recommended secure configuration.
                  * Applications and agents can use metadata without hardcoding instance-specific values.
                  * Treat metadata access as security-sensitive; restrict unnecessary access from applications/containers.
12. EC2 Instance Lifecycle
  

The core EC2 instance states and common transitions.
Action
	Compute Billing
	EBS Data
	What Changes
	Reboot
	Continues
	Persists
	Same instance; equivalent to OS reboot
	Stop
	Instance compute not billed while stopped
	Persists
	Can start again; public IPv4 may change unless using Elastic IP
	Hibernate
	Compute stops after hibernation completes
	Persists, including saved RAM to EBS
	Processes resume from saved memory when supported
	Terminate
	Stops
	Root EBS is deleted by default unless configured otherwise
	Instance is permanently deleted
	

Very common interview distinction
Stop is temporary and you can start the same instance again. Terminate permanently deletes the instance.
	13. EC2 Pricing / Purchasing Options
  

Major EC2 purchasing options and when they fit.
Option
	Core Idea
	Best Fit
	On-Demand
	No long-term commitment; pay for usage
	Short-term, unpredictable, dev/test, burst workloads
	Savings Plans
	Commit to a consistent $/hour usage level for 1 or 3 years
	Steady baseline compute usage
	Spot
	Uses spare EC2 capacity at large discount; can be interrupted
	Fault-tolerant batch, CI/CD, analytics, stateless workloads
	Reserved Instances
	Commitment-based EC2 pricing tied more closely to instance configuration
	Existing workloads and some specific reservation needs
	Dedicated Hosts / Instances
	Single-tenant hardware options
	Compliance, isolation, or software licensing requirements
	Capacity Reservations
	Reserve EC2 capacity in a specific Availability Zone
	Workloads that must have capacity available when needed
	

2026 AWS guidance to remember
Savings Plans are generally the preferred flexible commitment-based discount model for many new EC2 workloads. Reserved Instances still exist and remain an interview topic, but do not confuse a pricing discount with a Capacity Reservation.
	14. Spot Instances
Spot is cheap because AWS can reclaim the capacity when needed.
                  * Use only for interruption-tolerant workloads.
                  * Applications should checkpoint work, retry, and tolerate instance replacement.
                  * Good examples: batch processing, CI runners, analytics, rendering, and stateless workers.
                  * Do not place irreplaceable state only on the local instance.
15. EC2 Auto Scaling + Load Balancer
  

EC2 Auto Scaling adjusts instance count while a load balancer distributes traffic.
                  * Launch Template defines how new EC2 instances should be launched.
                  * Auto Scaling Group (ASG) manages desired, minimum, and maximum capacity.
                  * Scale out: add instances when demand increases.
                  * Scale in: remove instances when demand decreases.
                  * ALB/ELB health checks and EC2 health checks help determine which instances should receive traffic or be replaced.
                  * Distribute instances across multiple Availability Zones for higher availability.
16. Launch Templates
A Launch Template stores reusable EC2 launch configuration such as AMI, instance type, security groups, key pair, storage, IAM role, and user data.
                  * Commonly used by Auto Scaling Groups.
                  * Versioned, so configuration can evolve safely.
                  * Helps create consistent replacement instances.
17. Monitoring EC2
Use CloudWatch and EC2 status checks to understand instance health and performance.
                  * Common metrics: CPUUtilization, NetworkIn/Out, disk/EBS metrics, status-check metrics.
                  * By default EC2 sends CloudWatch metrics at 5-minute intervals; detailed monitoring provides 1-minute intervals.
                  * Memory and filesystem utilization usually require the CloudWatch Agent or another monitoring agent.
                  * Use CloudWatch alarms for high CPU, status-check failures, or application thresholds.
EC2 Status Checks
Check
	What It Detects
	System status check
	Underlying AWS host, power, or network infrastructure issues.
	Instance status check
	Problems inside the individual VM such as OS/network configuration.
	Attached EBS status check
	Issues with attached EBS reachability/health.
	Application status check
	Optional HTTP/HTTPS application-level health monitoring.
	18. EC2 + EBS Snapshots / AMIs
                  * EBS snapshot = point-in-time backup of an EBS volume.
                  * AMI = bootable machine image that can reference EBS snapshots for its volumes.
                  * Use snapshots for backup and restore; use AMIs to launch similarly configured EC2 instances.
                  * For consistent application-level backups, coordinate with the application/database as needed.
19. High Availability Pattern
Avoid designing a production application around one EC2 instance.
                  1. Deploy multiple EC2 instances.
                  2. Spread them across at least two Availability Zones where appropriate.
                  3. Place them behind an Application Load Balancer.
                  4. Use an Auto Scaling Group to maintain and scale capacity.
                  5. Keep application state external: RDS, ElastiCache, S3, EFS, etc., as appropriate.
                  6. Monitor with CloudWatch and alarms.
Key design principle
An EC2 instance should be replaceable. Production architectures become more resilient when servers are treated as disposable compute rather than a unique machine that cannot fail.
	20. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: I cannot SSH to a Linux EC2 instance.
Check instance state -> correct IP/DNS -> route/Internet Gateway or private access path -> security group port 22 -> NACL -> key/user -> OS firewall/sshd. Consider Session Manager.
Scenario: The website on EC2 is not reachable.
Check ALB/target health if used -> security groups -> correct listener/port -> application process -> OS firewall -> subnet/route -> health-check path.
Scenario: CPU is constantly at 95%.
Use CloudWatch to confirm -> inspect application/process -> optimize -> choose a larger/more suitable instance type or scale horizontally with an Auto Scaling Group.
Scenario: Disk is full.
Identify filesystem usage -> increase EBS volume if needed -> extend partition/filesystem -> remove/rotate logs -> add monitoring and alarms.
Scenario: A Spot instance disappeared.
Spot is interruptible. Use fault-tolerant design, checkpoints, multiple capacity pools, Auto Scaling, and persistent/external storage.
Scenario: Need zero/low downtime during traffic spikes.
Use ALB + Auto Scaling across multiple AZs, health checks, stateless app servers, externalized session/data, and appropriate scaling policies.
Scenario: Application needs S3 access.
Attach an IAM role/instance profile with least-privilege S3 permissions; do not hardcode access keys.
21. EC2 vs Other AWS Compute Services
Service
	Abstraction
	You Manage
	Typical Use
	EC2
	Virtual machine
	OS + runtime + app
	Maximum VM-level control
	ECS on EC2
	Containers on EC2
	Cluster capacity + containers
	Containerized services
	Fargate
	Serverless containers
	Containers/app
	Containers without managing EC2 hosts
	Lambda
	Functions
	Function code/config
	Event-driven/serverless workloads

                  * Run commands when you launch an EC2 instance with user data - AWS EC2 User Guide
                  * Use instance profiles - AWS IAM User Guide
                  * Monitor your instances using CloudWatch - AWS EC2 User Guide
                  * Status checks for Amazon EC2 instances - AWS EC2 User Guide


AWS EC2 Auto Scaling - Interview Study Notes
AWS EC2 Auto Scaling - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand EC2 Auto Scaling from first principles: ASGs, launch templates, Min/Desired/Max, scale out/in, load balancers, health replacement, target tracking, step/scheduled/predictive scaling, warmup/cooldown, lifecycle hooks, instance refresh, mixed instances, Spot, and interview scenarios.
	1. What is Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling?
Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling automatically maintains and adjusts the number of EC2 instances in an Auto Scaling group (ASG). It can replace unhealthy instances and increase/decrease capacity as demand changes.
                  * Maintains application availability by replacing failed/unhealthy EC2 instances.
                  * Scales out when more compute is needed.
                  * Scales in when demand falls to reduce cost.
                  * Distributes instances across multiple Availability Zones.
                  * Integrates with Elastic Load Balancing, CloudWatch, Spot, launch templates, lifecycle hooks, and deployment features.
  

An Auto Scaling group tries to keep desired capacity between minimum and maximum limits.
2. Min, Desired, and Max Capacity
Setting
	Meaning
	Minimum capacity
	Lowest capacity the group should scale down to.
	Desired capacity
	Capacity the ASG currently tries to maintain.
	Maximum capacity
	Highest capacity scaling actions can normally reach.
	                  * Desired must be between Min and Max.
                  * Scaling policies normally change Desired, not the Launch Template.
                  * If an instance unexpectedly terminates, ASG launches a replacement to return to Desired.
Example
Min = 2, Desired = 4, Max = 10. ASG runs 4 instances now; scaling policies may move desired anywhere from 2 to 10.
	3. Launch Template
  

Launch Template defines instance configuration; ASG defines capacity and placement.
                  * AMI
                  * Instance type or instance requirements
                  * Security groups
                  * IAM instance profile/role
                  * EBS volumes
                  * Key pair
                  * User data/bootstrap script
                  * Network-interface settings and other EC2 launch parameters
Current AWS guidance
Use Launch Templates for new Auto Scaling designs. Launch Configurations are legacy and lack many modern EC2/ASG features.
	4. Auto Scaling Group Placement
                  * Choose a VPC and one or more subnets/Availability Zones.
                  * For production high availability, use multiple AZs where possible.
                  * ASG tries to maintain balanced capacity across enabled Availability Zones.
                  * Application instances are commonly placed in private subnets behind an internet-facing ALB.
Production pattern
Public ALB across multiple AZs -> private EC2 Auto Scaling group across multiple AZs -> RDS/other backend services.
	5. Scale Out vs Scale In
Term
	What Happens
	Example
	Scale out
	Increase desired capacity / launch instances
	2 -> 4 instances
	Scale in
	Decrease desired capacity / terminate instances
	6 -> 3 instances
	Replace unhealthy
	Terminate bad instance and launch another
	Desired remains 4
	Interview distinction
Replacing an unhealthy instance is not necessarily 'scaling out'. ASG may simply replace one instance to maintain the same desired capacity.
	6. Auto Scaling + Load Balancer
  

ELB distributes traffic; Auto Scaling changes the amount of compute.
                  * Attach an ALB/NLB/GWLB/Classic LB target group/load balancer to an ASG.
                  * New instances are automatically registered.
                  * Terminated instances are automatically deregistered.
                  * ALB request count per target can be used as a scaling metric.
                  * Deregistration delay lets in-flight requests finish before terminated targets disappear.
Most important line
ELB does not create EC2 instances. Auto Scaling changes capacity; ELB distributes traffic across available healthy capacity.
	7. Health Checks and Automatic Replacement
  

Health signals can trigger replacement so the group maintains desired capacity.
                  * EC2 status checks are enabled by default.
                  * You can additionally use Elastic Load Balancing health checks.
                  * Current ASG health options also include VPC Lattice, EBS, and custom/application health checks.
                  * When an InService instance is considered unhealthy, ASG replaces it.
                  * If using ALB and application-level health matters, enable ELB health checks on the ASG.
Common mistake
Attaching an ALB does not automatically make ASG replace instances that fail ALB health checks. Enable ELB health checks for the group.
	8. Health Check Grace Period
The health check grace period gives a newly launched instance time to initialize before certain health failures are used to replace it.
                  * Useful when application startup/bootstrap takes time.
                  * If too short, healthy-but-still-starting instances may be repeatedly replaced.
                  * If too long, genuinely broken instances remain longer before replacement.
Interview phrase
Grace period protects startup from premature health replacement; it is not the same as instance warmup used by scaling policies.
	9. Scaling Methods
  

Target tracking is usually the easiest and preferred dynamic scaling method for standard utilization metrics.
Method
	How It Works
	Example
	Manual
	Change desired capacity yourself
	Set desired from 2 to 5
	Target Tracking
	Maintain metric near target
	Average CPU around 50%
	Step Scaling
	Different adjustments based on alarm severity
	CPU 70% -> +2; 90% -> +5
	Simple Scaling
	One adjustment then cooldown
	CPU alarm -> add 2
	Scheduled
	Scale at known time
	Weekdays 8 AM desired=10
	Predictive
	Forecast recurring future demand
	Scale before daily traffic peak
	10. Target Tracking Scaling
                  * Choose a metric and target value.
                  * Auto Scaling creates/manages the needed CloudWatch alarms.
                  * Scale out when load rises above what the target capacity can handle.
                  * Scale in more conservatively when load falls.
                  * AWS strongly recommends target tracking for common proportional metrics.
Good metrics
Average CPU utilization or ALB request count per target. The best target-tracking metric should decrease roughly proportionally as you add capacity.
	11. Step Scaling
Step scaling gives more control over how aggressively capacity changes.
                  * Create CloudWatch alarm(s).
                  * Define different scaling adjustments for different breach ranges.
                  * Example: CPU 60-70% -> +1; 70-85% -> +3; >85% -> +6.
                  * Useful when you know how much capacity different load levels need.
Target tracking vs step
Target tracking is simpler and self-adjusting. Step scaling is useful when you want explicit scaling increments for different alarm severities.
	12. Scheduled Scaling
                  * Changes Desired/Min/Max at a known time or recurring schedule.
                  * Good when traffic pattern is known in advance.
                  * Example: increase Min/Desired before office hours, reduce at night.
                  * Scheduled actions still obey the capacity values you define for those actions.
Scheduled vs predictive
Scheduled scaling follows a schedule you specify. Predictive scaling learns recurring patterns from historical metrics and forecasts capacity.
	13. Predictive Scaling
                  * Analyzes historical load to find daily/weekly recurring patterns.
                  * Forecasts future load and capacity needs.
                  * Can launch capacity before a predictable peak arrives.
                  * Useful when instances take significant time to initialize.
                  * Works best for recurring/cyclical workloads, not random one-off traffic spikes.
Common production approach
Predictive scaling can prepare baseline capacity before the peak; target tracking can still react to real-time deviations.
	14. Warmup vs Grace Period vs Cooldown vs Lifecycle Hook
  

These four concepts are frequently confused in interviews.
Concept
	Purpose
	Default instance warmup
	Wait for new instance metrics/resource use to stabilize before normal scaling evaluation.
	Health check grace period
	Delay health-check-based replacement after instance enters InService.
	Cooldown
	Pause after simple/manual scaling to avoid repeated actions before effects settle.
	Lifecycle hook
	Pause launch/terminate transition so custom automation can run.
	Current AWS recommendation
Configure default instance warmup for target-tracking/step-scaling groups instead of relying on unrelated cooldown behavior.
	15. Lifecycle Hooks
                  * Launch hook can pause an instance before it fully enters service.
                  * Termination hook can pause an instance before final termination.
                  * Use cases: download configuration, register with an external system, drain custom work, upload logs, graceful shutdown.
                  * Hooks can publish events/notifications or trigger automation such as Lambda/EventBridge workflows.
                  * Complete/abandon the lifecycle action before the hook timeout.
Example
On termination, lifecycle hook pauses instance -> worker stops taking jobs -> finishes/returns in-flight work -> logs uploaded -> lifecycle action completes -> EC2 terminates.
	16. Instance Refresh
  

Instance Refresh is a managed rolling replacement mechanism for an existing ASG.
                  * Update Launch Template/AMI or provide desired configuration.
                  * Start an instance refresh.
                  * Instances are replaced in batches while health/warmup rules are respected.
                  * Configure minimum/maximum healthy percentages.
                  * Optional checkpoints and bake time help validate deployment.
                  * Skip matching can avoid replacing instances already on the desired configuration.
                  * Auto rollback can revert supported failed refreshes.
Use case
Deploy a new AMI version across 100 EC2 instances without manually terminating them one by one.
	17. Termination Policies and Scale-In Protection
                  * When scaling in, Auto Scaling uses termination policy logic to choose which instance(s) to terminate.
                  * Default behavior considers Availability Zone balance and instance age/configuration factors.
                  * Custom termination policies can influence the choice.
                  * Instance scale-in protection prevents selected instances from being terminated by normal scale-in.
                  * Scale-in protection does not protect against every possible termination source, such as manual termination or certain health replacement events.
Interview use
Use scale-in protection for temporary important instances/jobs, but do not make stateful business data depend on one EC2 instance surviving forever.
	18. Mixed Instances: On-Demand + Spot
  

A mixed instances policy can improve both cost and capacity flexibility.
                  * Use multiple compatible EC2 instance types.
                  * Configure an On-Demand base capacity.
                  * Choose what percentage of additional capacity should be On-Demand vs Spot.
                  * Spot lowers cost but can be interrupted.
                  * Use allocation strategies that balance price and capacity availability.
                  * Applications using Spot should tolerate instance interruption and replacement.
19. Spot Capacity Rebalancing
                  * EC2 can send a rebalance recommendation when a Spot instance is at elevated interruption risk.
                  * With Capacity Rebalancing enabled, ASG attempts to launch replacement Spot capacity proactively.
                  * New replacement instance is normally made healthy before old at-risk capacity is terminated.
                  * ASG can temporarily exceed Max by up to 10% of desired capacity for rebalancing.
                  * Lifecycle hooks can help gracefully drain the at-risk instance.
Interview line
Capacity Rebalancing gives the ASG a head start before a Spot interruption rather than waiting until the instance is already lost.
	20. Warm Pools
                  * Warm pool keeps pre-initialized instances ready outside normal InService capacity.
                  * Instances can be kept stopped, hibernated, or running depending on setup.
                  * When scale-out happens, ASG can move a warm instance into service faster than a cold launch.
                  * Useful for applications with long startup times.
                  * Not necessary for fast-starting applications; warm pools add operational/resource cost.
Example
If an application needs 8 minutes to initialize, keeping pre-initialized stopped instances can dramatically reduce scale-out latency.
	21. Auto Scaling Across Availability Zones
                  * Choose subnets in multiple Availability Zones.
                  * ASG attempts to balance instances across enabled AZs.
                  * If an AZ becomes constrained/unavailable, ASG can launch in another enabled AZ when possible.
                  * Combine with a load balancer spanning the same AZ architecture.
High availability
One ASG across multiple AZs is generally better than manually maintaining separate independent fleets for the same stateless application.
	22. What Metric Should You Scale On?
Metric
	When It Makes Sense
	Average CPUUtilization
	CPU-bound application where CPU tracks demand.
	ALBRequestCountPerTarget
	HTTP app where requests per instance track load.
	NetworkIn/Out
	Network-bound workloads when throughput reflects demand.
	Custom queue backlog per instance
	Workers consuming SQS jobs.
	Custom business metric
	When infrastructure metrics do not represent workload pressure.
	Bad metric warning
Do not scale on a metric that does not improve when instances are added. Example: total queue depth alone can keep growing even after capacity doubles; backlog per instance is often a better target metric.
	23. Common Production Architecture
                  * Route 53 / CloudFront / WAF as needed.
                  * Application Load Balancer across public subnets.
                  * EC2 Auto Scaling group across private subnets in multiple AZs.
                  * Launch Template with immutable AMI, SGs, IAM role, EBS, and minimal user data.
                  * Target tracking on CPU or ALB requests per target.
                  * ELB health checks enabled for application-level replacement.
                  * RDS/ElastiCache/S3 outside the EC2 instance for persistent state.
                  * CloudWatch metrics/alarms/logs.
                  * Instance Refresh for controlled deployments.
Key design principle
Instances in an ASG should be replaceable. Do not store irreplaceable application state only on one local EC2 instance.
	24. EC2 Auto Scaling vs Application Auto Scaling
Service
	Scales
	Examples
	EC2 Auto Scaling
	EC2 instances in Auto Scaling groups
	Web/app EC2 fleet
	Application Auto Scaling
	Scalable dimensions of other AWS services
	ECS desired tasks, DynamoDB provisioned capacity, Aurora replicas, etc.
	Interview point
'AWS Auto Scaling' is a broad concept. If the question is about EC2 instances and Auto Scaling Groups, they usually mean Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling.
	25. Auto Scaling vs Elastic Load Balancing
Area
	Auto Scaling
	ELB
	Job
	Changes compute capacity
	Distributes traffic
	Launch/terminate EC2
	Yes
	No
	Health checks
	Can replace unhealthy instances
	Stops routing to unhealthy targets
	Scaling metric
	Uses CloudWatch/ALB metrics
	Provides metrics such as request count/target
	Used together
	Very common
	Very common
	26. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: One EC2 instance crashes in a 4-instance ASG.
ASG marks it unhealthy/terminated and launches a replacement to return to Desired=4.
Scenario: Traffic suddenly doubles.
Target tracking or step scaling raises desired capacity, ASG launches new instances, and ALB begins sending traffic after they become healthy.
Scenario: CPU is always low at night.
Dynamic scaling can scale in; scheduled scaling can also reduce baseline capacity when the timing is known.
Scenario: Daily 9 AM traffic spike causes slow startup.
Use predictive/scheduled scaling so capacity is ready before 9 AM, and consider warm pools if instances initialize slowly.
Scenario: New EC2 instances keep getting terminated immediately.
Check launch/user-data failures, ELB health path, health-check grace period, security groups, and application startup time.
Scenario: Need to deploy a new AMI to all instances.
Create/update Launch Template version and use Instance Refresh.
Scenario: Need cheapest fault-tolerant capacity.
Use a mixed instances policy with Spot plus On-Demand baseline and enable Capacity Rebalancing.
Scenario: One worker job must not be killed during scale-in.
Use lifecycle hooks and/or temporary scale-in protection, but externalize job state/checkpoints.
Scenario: ALB says instance unhealthy but ASG does nothing.
Enable ELB health checks for the Auto Scaling group.
27. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Instances not launching: Launch Template AMI/type/SG/IAM/EBS/subnet capacity/quotas.
2.  Instance launches then terminates: user data/app startup, health checks, grace period, ALB target health.
3.  No scale-out: metric, CloudWatch alarm/policy, target value, Max capacity, suspended processes.
4.  No scale-in: Min capacity, scale-in disabled, instance warmup, multiple target-tracking policies, scale-in protection.
5.  Uneven AZs: subnet/AZ capacity, Spot availability, balancing activity, instance constraints.
6.  ALB traffic fails: target group registration, SGs, listener/rules, health path/port.
7.  Scaling oscillates: metric choice, warmup, thresholds/target, app startup time, too-aggressive step policies.
8.  Instance Refresh stuck: unhealthy new instances, protected/standby instances, launch failures, warmup/maintenance policy.
9.  Spot interruptions: multiple instance types/AZs, allocation strategy, Capacity Rebalancing, graceful shutdown hooks.
28. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is EC2 Auto Scaling?
Answer: It is an AWS service that maintains and automatically adjusts the number of EC2 instances in an Auto Scaling group based on desired capacity, health, schedules, or scaling policies.
2. What is an Auto Scaling Group?
Answer: A logical group of EC2 instances managed together for health replacement, scaling, and placement across configured subnets/Availability Zones.
3. What are Min, Desired, and Max?
Answer: Min is the lower scaling boundary, Desired is the capacity ASG currently tries to maintain, and Max is the upper scaling boundary.
4. What is a Launch Template?
Answer: A reusable EC2 launch definition containing AMI, instance type/requirements, security groups, IAM role, storage, user data, key pair, and other instance launch settings.
5. Launch Template vs Launch Configuration?
Answer: Launch Templates are the modern recommended option and support newer EC2/ASG capabilities and versioning. Launch Configurations are legacy.
6. Scale out vs scale in?
Answer: Scale out adds capacity/instances. Scale in removes capacity/instances.
7. If an instance crashes, does that count as scale out?
Answer: Not necessarily. The ASG can launch a replacement simply to restore the same desired capacity.
8. How does ASG work with ALB?
Answer: ASG automatically registers new instances and deregisters terminating instances. ALB routes requests; ASG changes capacity.
9. Does ALB create new EC2 instances?
Answer: No. Auto Scaling launches/terminates instances; ALB distributes traffic.
10. What health checks does ASG use by default?
Answer: EC2 status checks. Optional health sources can include ELB, VPC Lattice, EBS, and custom/application checks.
11. If ALB marks an instance unhealthy, will ASG always replace it?
Answer: Only if ELB health checks are enabled for the Auto Scaling group. Attaching an ALB alone is not enough.
12. What is health check grace period?
Answer: A startup window after an instance enters InService during which health failures are not immediately used to replace the instance.
13. What is target tracking scaling?
Answer: A dynamic policy that automatically adjusts capacity to keep a metric near a target value, such as average CPU around 50%.
14. Why is target tracking popular?
Answer: It is simple, proportional, and AWS creates/manages the needed CloudWatch alarms automatically.
15. What is step scaling?
Answer: A dynamic policy where different alarm breach levels trigger different scaling adjustments, such as +2 for moderate load and +5 for high load.
16. What is simple scaling?
Answer: An older/simple policy that performs one scaling adjustment and then waits for a cooldown before another simple-scaling activity.
17. What is scheduled scaling?
Answer: Changing Min/Desired/Max at known times or recurring schedules.
18. What is predictive scaling?
Answer: Using historical load patterns to forecast future capacity needs and scale before predictable demand arrives.
19. Scheduled vs predictive scaling?
Answer: Scheduled scaling follows times you explicitly define. Predictive scaling learns recurring demand patterns from historical metrics.
20. What is instance warmup?
Answer: The time for a new instance's resource usage/application behavior to stabilize before it fully influences scaling metrics/decisions.
21. Warmup vs health-check grace period?
Answer: Warmup is mainly for scaling behavior/metrics. Grace period is for delaying health-check-based replacement during startup.
22. What is cooldown?
Answer: A pause used mainly by simple scaling/manual behavior to allow a scaling action to take effect before another similar action.
23. What is a lifecycle hook?
Answer: It pauses an instance during launch or termination so custom automation can run, such as initialization, draining, cleanup, or log upload.
24. What is Instance Refresh?
Answer: A managed rolling replacement process for updating ASG instances to a new AMI/Launch Template configuration while maintaining health/capacity preferences.
25. How do you deploy a new AMI to an ASG?
Answer: Create a new Launch Template version with the AMI and start an Instance Refresh, or use another controlled replacement/deployment method.
26. What is instance scale-in protection?
Answer: A setting that prevents selected instances from being terminated by ordinary ASG scale-in actions.
27. What is a mixed instances policy?
Answer: An ASG configuration using multiple instance types and purchase options such as On-Demand and Spot.
28. What is Spot Capacity Rebalancing?
Answer: A feature that proactively attempts to replace Spot instances that receive rebalance recommendations before they are interrupted.
29. What is a warm pool?
Answer: A pool of pre-initialized instances kept ready so scale-out can move capacity into service faster than launching from scratch.
30. How do you choose a scaling metric?
Answer: Choose a metric that reflects workload per instance and improves when capacity is added, such as average CPU, ALB request count per target, or queue backlog per worker.
31. Why can scaling on total queue depth be poor?
Answer: Total depth may stay high even after adding instances. A normalized metric such as backlog per consumer better reflects required capacity.
32. Can an ASG span multiple AZs?
Answer: Yes, and that is a common high-availability pattern. Configure subnets in multiple AZs.
33. What happens when ASG scales in?
Answer: Desired capacity decreases and the ASG selects one or more instances to terminate according to termination policy and protection rules.
34. EC2 Auto Scaling vs Application Auto Scaling?
Answer: EC2 Auto Scaling manages EC2 instances in ASGs. Application Auto Scaling scales supported dimensions of services such as ECS tasks or DynamoDB capacity.
35. Does Auto Scaling itself cost extra?
Answer: Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling has no separate service charge; you pay for the EC2/CloudWatch/other AWS resources you use.
29. Final Auto Scaling Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	ASG
	Logical EC2 fleet managed for capacity and health
	Min
	Lowest normal capacity
	Desired
	Capacity ASG tries to maintain now
	Max
	Highest normal capacity
	Launch Template
	How new EC2 instances are configured
	Scale Out
	Add capacity
	Scale In
	Remove capacity
	Target Tracking
	Keep metric near a target
	Step Scaling
	Different adjustments by alarm severity
	Scheduled Scaling
	Scale at known times
	Predictive Scaling
	Forecast recurring future load
	Health Check
	Signal used to replace unhealthy instances
	Grace Period
	Protect new instance startup from health replacement
	Warmup
	Delay influence of new instance on scaling metrics
	Cooldown
	Pause between simple scaling actions
	Lifecycle Hook
	Pause launch/termination for custom work
	Instance Refresh
	Rolling ASG instance replacement/update
	Scale-In Protection

	Mixed Instances
	Multiple types + On-Demand/Spot
	Capacity Rebalancing

	Warm Pool

	ALB + ASG

If you are short on time, revise these first
ASG basics -> Min/Desired/Max -> Launch Template -> scale out/in -> ALB integration -> health checks -> target tracking -> step/scheduled/predictive -> warmup vs grace period -> lifecycle hooks -> Instance Refresh -> Spot/mixed instances -> interview Q&A.
	30. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * Launch Templates are the modern standard; Launch Configurations are legacy.
                  * AWS strongly recommends target tracking for common proportional scaling metrics.
                  * Default instance warmup is recommended for target-tracking/step-scaling groups but is not automatically configured by default.
                  * ASG health checks now support EC2 plus optional ELB, VPC Lattice, EBS, and custom/application health signals.
                  * Instance Refresh supports rolling replacement and newer specialized root-volume replacement in supported mixed-instance scenarios.
                  * Capacity Rebalancing can proactively replace at-risk Spot Instances and may temporarily exceed Max by up to 10% of desired capacity.
                  * EC2 Auto Scaling itself has no additional service charge beyond the AWS resources and CloudWatch usage involved.

AWS Lambda
AWS Lambda


Introduction to Serverless Computing


Today, we're going to embark on an exciting journey into the world of serverless computing and explore AWS Lambda, a powerful service offered by Amazon Web Services.


So, what exactly is "serverless computing"? Don't worry; it's not about eliminating servers altogether. Instead, serverless computing is a cloud computing execution model where you, as a developer, don't have to manage servers directly. You focus solely on writing and deploying your code, while the cloud provider takes care of all the underlying infrastructure.


Understanding AWS Lambda


In this serverless landscape, AWS Lambda shines as a leading service. AWS Lambda is a compute service that lets you run your code in response to events without the need to provision or manage servers. It automatically scales your applications based on incoming requests, so you don't have to worry about capacity planning or dealing with server maintenance.


How Lambda Functions Fit into the Serverless World


At the heart of AWS Lambda are "Lambda functions." These are individual units of code that perform specific tasks. Think of them as small, single-purpose applications that run independently.


Here's how Lambda functions fit into the serverless world:


1. Event-Driven Execution: Lambda functions are triggered by events. An event could be anything, like a new file being uploaded to Amazon S3, a request hitting an API, or a specific time on the clock. When an event occurs, Lambda executes the corresponding function.


2. No Server Management: As a developer, you don't need to worry about managing servers. AWS handles everything behind the scenes. You just upload your code, configure the trigger, and Lambda takes care of the rest.


3. Automatic Scaling: Whether you have one user or one million users, Lambda scales automatically. Each function instance runs independently, ensuring that your application can handle any level of incoming traffic without manual intervention.


4. Pay-per-Use: One of the most attractive features of serverless computing is cost efficiency. With Lambda, you pay only for the compute time your code consumes. When your code isn't running, you're not charged.


5. Supported Languages: Lambda supports multiple programming languages like Node.js, Python, Java, Go, and more. You can choose the language you are comfortable with or that best fits your application's needs.


Real-World Use Cases


Now, let's explore some real-world use cases to better understand how AWS Lambda can be applied:


1. Automated Image Processing: Imagine you have a photo-sharing app, and users upload images every day. You can use Lambda to automatically resize or compress these images as soon as they are uploaded to S3.


2. Chatbots and Virtual Assistants: Build interactive chatbots or voice-controlled virtual assistants using Lambda. These assistants can perform tasks like answering questions, fetching data, or even controlling smart home devices.


3. Scheduled Data Backups: Use Lambda to create scheduled tasks for backing up data from one storage location to another, ensuring data resilience and disaster recovery.


4. Real-Time Analytics: Lambda can process streaming data from IoT devices, social media, or other sources, allowing you to perform real-time analytics and gain insights instantly.


5. API Backends: Develop scalable API backends for web and mobile applications using Lambda. It automatically handles the incoming API requests and executes the corresponding functions.


AWS Lambda - Interview Study Notes
AWS Lambda - Interview Study Notes
Beginner to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Be able to explain what Lambda is, how it is invoked, how it scales, how permissions/networking work, how errors are retried, and when Lambda is or is not a good choice.
	1. What is AWS Lambda?
AWS Lambda is a serverless compute service that runs your code in response to events without you provisioning or managing servers. You upload code, configure the function, and AWS handles the execution environments and scaling.
                  * You pay mainly for requests and compute duration rather than keeping a server running continuously.
                  * Common workloads: APIs, file processing, queue consumers, scheduled jobs, event-driven automation, webhooks, and lightweight background processing.
                  * Lambda is designed primarily for stateless, event-driven, short-lived compute.
  

A common serverless web/API architecture using API Gateway, Lambda, and managed AWS services.
Interview definition
Lambda is event-driven serverless compute: AWS runs and automatically scales the execution environments while I focus on the function code and configuration.
	2. Lambda Function - Main Components
Component
	What to Remember
	Function code
	Your application logic.
	Handler
	Entry point Lambda calls for each event.
	Runtime
	Language/runtime such as Java, Node.js, Python, .NET, Ruby, or custom runtime.
	Execution role
	IAM role used by the function to call AWS services.
	Memory
	128 MB to 10,240 MB for standard Lambda; CPU scales with memory.
	Timeout
	Maximum execution time; standard Lambda supports up to 900 seconds (15 minutes).
	Ephemeral storage
	/tmp temporary storage, configurable from 512 MB to 10,240 MB.
	Environment variables
	Configuration values available to function code; total configured environment-variable size is limited.
	Layers
	Reusable dependencies/content for ZIP-based functions; up to 5 layers.
	Trigger / event source
	What causes the function to run.
	Memory affects more than RAM
Lambda allocates CPU in proportion to configured memory. Increasing memory can reduce duration for CPU-bound functions, so the cheapest setting is not always the smallest memory setting.
	3. Handler and Event
The handler is the function entry point. Lambda passes an event payload and runtime context to it. The event shape depends on the invoking service.
                  * API Gateway event -> HTTP method, path, headers, body, request context.
                  * S3 event -> bucket name, object key, event type, etc.
                  * SQS event -> batch of queue messages.
                  * EventBridge event -> source, detail-type, detail, time, and other routing data.
4. Execution Environment, Cold Start, Warm Start
  

Lambda creates isolated execution environments. New environments perform initialization; later invocations may reuse them.
                  * Cold start: Lambda creates/initializes an execution environment before the handler runs.
                  * Warm invocation: an existing environment is reused, avoiding most initialization work.
                  * Place reusable SDK clients/database connection pools outside the handler when appropriate so warm invocations can reuse them.
                  * Do not depend on in-memory state or /tmp being present on the next invocation; environment reuse is an optimization, not a guarantee.
                  * Cold-start impact varies by runtime, package size, initialization code, VPC/configuration, and traffic pattern.
How to reduce cold starts
Keep startup work small, avoid loading unnecessary dependencies, initialize lazily where sensible, choose suitable memory/runtime, and use Provisioned Concurrency for latency-sensitive workloads when justified.
	5. Lambda Triggers / Event Sources
Source
	Typical Pattern
	Example
	API Gateway / Function URL
	HTTP request
	REST/HTTP API or webhook
	S3
	Object event
	Process image after upload
	EventBridge
	Event / schedule
	Cron job or application event
	SNS
	Pub/sub event
	React to a published notification
	SQS
	Polled batch
	Background queue worker
	Kinesis / DynamoDB Streams
	Polled stream batch
	Process ordered stream records
	ALB
	HTTP request
	Invoke Lambda behind an Application Load Balancer
	6. Invocation Types
  

Know who owns retries and whether the caller waits for a response.
Model
	Caller Waits?
	Typical Sources
	Error Handling
	Synchronous
	Yes
	API Gateway, Function URL, direct Invoke
	Caller/service generally handles retries.
	Asynchronous
	No
	S3, SNS, EventBridge
	Lambda queues event and can retry / use destinations or DLQ.
	Event Source Mapping
	No direct caller waiting
	SQS, Kinesis, DynamoDB Streams
	Lambda polls source; retry/batch behavior depends on source.
	7. API Gateway vs Lambda Function URL
Feature
	Function URL
	API Gateway
	Main idea
	Simple direct HTTPS endpoint for one Lambda
	Full API management layer
	Authentication
	NONE or AWS_IAM
	IAM, Cognito/JWT, Lambda authorizer and more
	Routing / transformation
	Basic
	Advanced routing, validation, transformation
	Throttling / API management
	Limited/basic controls
	Rich throttling, stages, usage plans/features depending on API type
	Best for
	Simple webhook/prototype/simple endpoint
	Production APIs needing API-management capabilities
	Interview shortcut
Function URL = simplest way to expose Lambda over HTTP. API Gateway = use when you need a real API management layer with richer auth, routing, throttling, validation, stages, or transformations.
	8. Lambda IAM - Two Permission Directions
                  * Execution role: what the Lambda function is allowed to do (for example read S3, write DynamoDB, publish SNS, write logs).
                  * Resource-based policy on Lambda: who or which AWS service is allowed to invoke the Lambda function.
Example
S3 invokes Lambda because the Lambda resource policy permits S3. Lambda then reads the S3 object because its execution role permits s3:GetObject.
	9. Execution Role
                  * Each Lambda function uses an IAM execution role.
                  * Use least privilege; do not attach broad AdministratorAccess to application functions.
                  * CloudWatch logging permissions are commonly supplied using AWSLambdaBasicExecutionRole or an equivalent custom policy.
                  * If Lambda needs Secrets Manager, S3, DynamoDB, SQS, etc., grant only the needed actions/resources.
10. Concurrency
  

Reserved and Provisioned Concurrency solve different problems.
                  * Concurrency = number of function invocations executing at the same time.
                  * A common default account-level concurrency quota is 1,000 per Region and is adjustable; new accounts can begin with lower quotas.
                  * Per-function concurrency scaling rate is up to 1,000 new execution environments every 10 seconds.
                  * If concurrency is exhausted, additional invocations can be throttled.
Reserved vs Provisioned
Reserved Concurrency reserves capacity for a function and also caps it; it does not pre-initialize environments. Provisioned Concurrency pre-initializes environments to reduce cold-start latency and has extra cost.
	11. Why Limit Concurrency?
                  * Protect a database from too many simultaneous connections.
                  * Prevent one function from consuming all account concurrency.
                  * Control calls to rate-limited downstream APIs.
                  * Create predictable backpressure for event-driven workloads.
12. Important Standard Lambda Limits to Know
Setting
	Interview-Level Value
	Memory
	128 MB to 10,240 MB
	Timeout
	1 to 900 seconds (15 minutes)
	/tmp ephemeral storage
	512 MB to 10,240 MB
	Synchronous invocation payload
	6 MB request and 6 MB response; response streaming supports larger responses
	Asynchronous invocation payload
	1 MB
	ZIP direct upload
	50 MB compressed; 250 MB uncompressed package contents including layers
	Container image
	Up to 10 GB uncompressed image size
	Environment variables
	4 KB aggregate
	Layers
	Up to 5
	Current-product note
Standard Lambda interview questions still use the 15-minute limit. AWS now also has newer Lambda Managed Instances with separate higher limits for some invocation modes; treat that as an advanced/current-product topic, not the core Lambda model.
	13. Deployment Package: ZIP vs Container Image
Option
	Good For
	Key Point
	ZIP package
	Most normal functions
	Code and dependencies packaged as ZIP; can use Lambda layers.
	Container image
	Large/custom dependency stacks
	Package runtime + app as an OCI-compatible image; Lambda image size can be much larger than ZIP.
	Custom runtime
	Languages/runtime behavior not built in
	Implement Lambda runtime API contract.
	14. Lambda Layers
                  * A layer is a versioned ZIP archive containing libraries, custom runtime files, or shared configuration/content.
                  * Useful to share common dependencies across ZIP-based Lambda functions.
                  * A function can use up to 5 layers.
                  * For container-image functions, package dependencies into the image instead of attaching traditional Lambda layers.
15. Versions and Aliases
  

Publish immutable versions and use aliases such as dev, staging, or prod.
                  * $LATEST is the mutable working version.
                  * Published versions are immutable snapshots of code + most configuration.
                  * Alias is a named pointer to a version, such as prod -> version 12.
                  * Aliases can support weighted traffic shifting between two versions for safer deployments.
16. Environment Variables and Secrets
                  * Environment variables are useful for non-secret configuration such as table names, feature flags, or environment names.
                  * Do not hardcode credentials in code or source control.
                  * For sensitive values, prefer services such as AWS Secrets Manager or SSM Parameter Store with IAM-controlled access.
                  * The function execution role needs permission to read those secrets/parameters.
17. Lambda and VPC
  

A VPC-connected Lambda can access private resources; NAT/VPC endpoints determine external/AWS-service reachability.
                  * By default, normal Lambda functions run in a Lambda-managed networking environment and can access the public internet.
                  * Attach Lambda to your VPC when it needs private resources such as RDS or private services.
                  * Select private subnets across multiple Availability Zones and suitable security groups.
                  * A public subnet does not by itself give a VPC-connected Lambda public internet access.
                  * For IPv4 internet egress from private subnets, use a NAT gateway; for supported AWS services, VPC endpoints can provide private connectivity.
Common interview trap
"Put Lambda in a public subnet for internet access" is incorrect for normal VPC-attached Lambda. Use proper egress routing such as NAT from private subnets.
	18. Lambda + SQS
                  * Lambda uses an event source mapping to poll SQS.
                  * Messages are delivered in batches.
                  * On batch failure, messages can become visible again after the queue visibility timeout.
                  * Use SQS redrive policy / DLQ for poison messages.
                  * Use partial batch responses (ReportBatchItemFailures) so successfully processed messages are not unnecessarily retried.
                  * Make the consumer idempotent because the same message can be processed more than once.
19. Retries, Dead-Letter Queues, and Destinations
  

Async Lambda retries and SQS-source retries are different mechanisms.
                  * Asynchronous Lambda invocation: function errors are retried automatically; by default Lambda can make up to two additional attempts.
                  * Async on-failure destinations can send invocation records to services such as SQS, SNS, S3 (failure), Lambda, or EventBridge depending on configuration.
                  * Lambda asynchronous DLQ supports SQS standard queues or SNS standard topics.
                  * SQS-triggered Lambda should generally use the SQS queue redrive DLQ, not confuse it with Lambda async DLQ.
                  * At-least-once delivery means duplicate events are possible; design idempotent handlers.
20. Lambda Monitoring
                  * CloudWatch Metrics are emitted automatically, including Invocations, Errors, Duration, Throttles, and concurrency-related metrics.
                  * CloudWatch Logs is the standard/default log destination when the execution role has the required logging permissions.
                  * Use structured logs and correlation/request IDs for easier debugging.
                  * CloudWatch alarms can alert on error rate, throttling, duration, or concurrency.
                  * AWS X-Ray can trace requests across services and help find latency bottlenecks.
                  * Lambda Insights provides deeper system/performance telemetry for supported runtimes.
21. Lambda Pricing - What to Know
For interview purposes, understand the cost model rather than memorizing region-specific prices.
                  * Request charges: based on number of function invocations.
                  * Compute charges: based mainly on execution duration and allocated memory (GB-seconds style metering).
                  * Provisioned Concurrency has separate charges because environments are kept ready.
                  * Additional /tmp ephemeral storage above the included amount can have additional charges.
                  * CloudWatch, NAT Gateway, API Gateway, data transfer, and other integrated services can add separate costs.
Cost optimization
Reduce unnecessary invocations, choose memory based on cost/performance testing, avoid idle Provisioned Concurrency, reduce log volume, and watch NAT Gateway/API Gateway/downstream-service costs.
	22. Lambda vs EC2 vs Containers
Feature
	Lambda
	EC2
	ECS/Fargate
	Server management
	No server management
	You manage VM/OS
	Container-focused; Fargate removes host management
	Runtime length
	Standard functions up to 15 min
	Long-running
	Long-running
	Scaling
	Automatic per event/concurrency
	ASG/manual
	Service/task scaling
	Best for
	Event-driven, bursty, short compute
	Full server control, persistent processes
	Containerized services/jobs
	Billing style
	Requests + execution
	Instance uptime/capacity
	Task/container resources
	23. Good Lambda Use Cases
                  * REST/HTTP endpoints with API Gateway for suitable workloads.
                  * S3 image/document processing.
                  * Queue workers using SQS.
                  * EventBridge scheduled jobs.
                  * Glue code between AWS services.
                  * Notifications and webhooks.
                  * Small ETL/event-processing steps.
24. When Lambda May Not Be the Best Choice
                  * Long-running processes beyond standard Lambda execution limits.
                  * Applications requiring persistent local state or a permanently running server process.
                  * Very high, steady utilization where another compute model may be cheaper or operationally simpler.
                  * Workloads needing complete OS/kernel control.
                  * Extremely latency-sensitive workloads where cold-start/managed-runtime characteristics are unacceptable and Provisioned Concurrency is not suitable.
25. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: API has random traffic spikes.
API Gateway + Lambda is a strong option; Lambda scales with concurrency. Watch downstream capacity and concurrency limits.
Scenario: Lambda is opening too many RDS connections.
Use connection reuse/pooling where appropriate, cap concurrency, consider RDS Proxy, and avoid creating a new connection per invocation.
Scenario: Function occasionally has slow first requests.
Investigate cold starts and initialization. Optimize startup or use Provisioned Concurrency for latency-sensitive traffic.
Scenario: Lambda in VPC cannot reach the internet.
Check private-subnet route to NAT Gateway (IPv4), security-group egress, NACLs, DNS, or use VPC endpoints for AWS services.
Scenario: SQS messages are processed twice.
This can happen with at-least-once delivery. Make processing idempotent and configure visibility timeout/redrive/partial batch response correctly.
Scenario: One Lambda consumes all account concurrency.
Configure reserved concurrency to cap it and protect capacity for other functions.
Scenario: Need an HTTP webhook quickly with minimal API features.
Lambda Function URL can be simplest. Use API Gateway when richer API management/auth/routing is required.
Scenario: Async events fail repeatedly.
Configure retry/maximum event age as appropriate plus an on-failure destination or DLQ for recovery.
26. Must-Know Lambda Interview Questions
                  * What is AWS Lambda and why is it called serverless?
                  * What is a Lambda handler?
                  * What is a cold start vs warm start?
                  * How does Lambda scale?
                  * What is concurrency?
                  * Reserved vs Provisioned Concurrency?
                  * What happens when concurrency is exhausted?
                  * Synchronous vs asynchronous invocation?
                  * What is an event source mapping?
                  * How does Lambda consume SQS?
                  * How do retries and DLQs work?
                  * Why should Lambda handlers be idempotent?
                  * What is the Lambda execution role?
                  * Execution role vs Lambda resource-based policy?
                  * What is the maximum standard Lambda timeout?
                  * How does memory affect CPU?
                  * What is /tmp in Lambda?
                  * ZIP vs container-image deployment?
                  * What are Lambda layers?
                  * Versions vs aliases?
                  * How does Lambda access RDS in a VPC?
                  * Why does putting Lambda in a public subnet not give internet access?
                  * Function URL vs API Gateway?
                  * How do you monitor Lambda?
                  * Lambda vs EC2 - when would you choose each?

	28. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * Standard Lambda functions run up to 15 minutes and can allocate 128 MB to 10,240 MB memory.
                  * Lambda now also has newer advanced offerings such as Lambda Managed Instances with different quotas; standard Lambda remains the core interview model.
                  * Per-function concurrency scaling rate is up to 1,000 new execution environments every 10 seconds.
                  * Function URLs are simple public HTTP(S) endpoints and support AWS_IAM or NONE authentication; API Gateway provides richer API-management features.
                  * Lambda logs default to CloudWatch Logs when permissions are present, and Lambda metrics are automatically published to CloudWatch.
