AWS Application Load Balancer (ALB) - Interview S…
AWS Application Load Balancer (ALB) - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand ALB from first principles: listeners, rules, target groups, health checks, path/host routing, HTTPS, Auto Scaling integration, stickiness, deregistration delay, weighted target groups, authentication, monitoring, and common interview scenarios.
	1. What is an Application Load Balancer?
An Application Load Balancer is an AWS Elastic Load Balancing service designed for Layer 7 HTTP and HTTPS traffic. It accepts client requests, evaluates application-level routing rules, and forwards requests to healthy targets.
                  * Works at OSI Layer 7: understands HTTP requests.
                  * Supports HTTP and HTTPS listeners.
                  * Supports WebSocket connections.
                  * Supports HTTP/2 to viewers on HTTPS listeners and HTTP/2/gRPC to supported target groups.
                  * Can route by host, URL path, HTTP headers, query strings, HTTP method, and source IP.
                  * Common targets: EC2 instances, ECS tasks/IPs, private IPs, and Lambda functions.
  

ALB listener and rules determine what happens to each HTTP request.
2. Why Use an ALB?
                  * Distribute HTTP/HTTPS traffic across multiple healthy application instances.
                  * Provide one stable DNS endpoint in front of changing backend targets.
                  * Route different applications/services using host- or path-based rules.
                  * Terminate TLS/HTTPS at the load balancer.
                  * Integrate with EC2 Auto Scaling and ECS.
                  * Perform authentication with Cognito/OIDC for supported HTTPS listener rules.
                  * Attach AWS WAF for web-request protection.
                  * Perform canary/blue-green traffic splitting with weighted target groups.
Interview one-liner
ALB is AWS's Layer-7 load balancer for HTTP/HTTPS applications with content-aware routing and health-based traffic distribution.
	3. ALB vs NLB
Area
	ALB
	NLB
	Layer
	Layer 7
	Layer 4
	Protocols
	HTTP/HTTPS, WebSocket, HTTP/2, gRPC
	TCP/TLS/UDP/QUIC-family
	Routing
	Host/path/header/query/method/source IP
	Connection/network-level routing
	Static IP
	Not typical ALB feature
	Supports static IP/EIP scenarios
	WAF
	Yes
	Not the normal WAF integration
	Best fit
	Web/API/microservices
	Very high-performance or non-HTTP network traffic
	Interview shortcut
ALB when request-aware HTTP routing matters; NLB when transport-layer performance/protocol/static-IP requirements dominate.
	4. Internet-Facing vs Internal ALB
Type
	Who Can Reach It
	Typical Use
	Internet-facing
	Public internet through public-facing ALB nodes
	Public websites/APIs
	Internal
	Only clients with network reachability to the VPC/private address space
	Internal microservices/admin/business apps
	                  * Internet-facing ALBs need subnets with appropriate internet connectivity.
                  * Backend targets can still remain in private subnets.
                  * Internal ALBs are common for service-to-service/private application traffic.
5. Listeners
A listener is the protocol + port where the ALB accepts client connections.
Listener
	Typical Use
	Example
	HTTP :80
	Plain HTTP or redirect
	Redirect all requests to HTTPS
	HTTPS :443
	Encrypted public web traffic
	Forward to application target group
	Custom HTTP/HTTPS port
	Special application requirement
	HTTPS :8443
	                  * Every listener has a default rule.
                  * HTTPS listeners require one or more certificates and a TLS security policy.
6. Listener Rules
  

ALB rules provide application-aware request routing.
                  * Rules have priorities; lower priority number is evaluated first.
                  * If no custom rule matches, the default rule runs last.
                  * Conditions can use host-header, path-pattern, HTTP header, HTTP method, query string, and source IP.
                  * Current ALB rules also support host-header and URL rewrite transforms.
                  * Each rule ultimately ends with forward, redirect, or fixed-response routing behavior.
Example
api.example.com/orders/* -> Orders Target Group; admin.example.com/* -> Admin Target Group; default -> Web Target Group.
	7. Rule Actions
Action
	Meaning
	Forward
	Send request to one or more target groups.
	Redirect
	Return an HTTP redirect, commonly HTTP -> HTTPS.
	Fixed response
	ALB directly returns a configured status/body.
	Authenticate with Cognito
	HTTPS pre-routing user authentication.
	Authenticate with OIDC
	HTTPS pre-routing external identity-provider authentication.
	JWT validation
	Newer HTTPS pre-routing token validation capability.
	8. Target Groups
A target group is a logical collection of destinations to which ALB forwards matching requests.
                  * Target group defines protocol, port, target type, protocol version, and health check.
                  * Target group types for ALB: instance, IP, or Lambda.
                  * A target can belong to multiple target groups.
                  * ALB listener rules point to target groups, not directly to EC2 instances.
                  * For ECS awsvpc/Fargate, IP target type is commonly used.
Interview point
Listener decides which target group; target group contains the actual backend targets.
	9. Target Types
Target Type
	What You Register
	Example
	instance
	EC2 instance IDs
	EC2 Auto Scaling group
	ip
	Private IP addresses
	ECS tasks, on-prem/private connected resources
	lambda
	Lambda function
	Serverless ALB integration
	                  * Publicly routable IPs cannot be registered as ALB IP targets.
                  * Target type cannot be changed after the target group is created.
10. Health Checks
  

Health checks control which targets receive normal traffic.
                  * Health check is configured per target group.
                  * Common HTTP health path: /health or /actuator/health.
                  * Configure protocol, port, path, interval, timeout, success codes, and healthy/unhealthy thresholds.
                  * A target must pass enough checks to become healthy.
                  * Unhealthy targets stop receiving normal new requests.
Fail-open behavior
If all registered targets in all enabled AZs are unhealthy, ALB can route requests to them anyway ('fail open'). Design the app to handle this and monitor target health.
	11. Good Health-Check Endpoint
                  * Fast and lightweight.
                  * Returns success only when the application can actually serve meaningful traffic.
                  * Should not perform an extremely expensive full dependency test every few seconds.
                  * Separate liveness/readiness concepts in the application if necessary.
                  * Keep security rules allowing health-check traffic from ALB to targets.
Common Spring Boot choice
/actuator/health or a dedicated /health endpoint, with careful control over which dependency checks determine readiness.
	12. ALB + Auto Scaling
  

ALB and Auto Scaling are complementary services.
                  * ASG launches/terminates EC2 instances.
                  * ASG automatically registers/deregisters instances with the target group.
                  * ALB sends traffic only to healthy registered targets.
                  * ALBRequestCountPerTarget can be used by Auto Scaling target tracking.
                  * Use multiple Availability Zones for high availability.
Most important distinction
ALB distributes traffic. Auto Scaling changes the number of instances.
	13. Cross-Zone Load Balancing
                  * At the ALB load-balancer level, cross-zone load balancing is always enabled.
                  * This lets ALB nodes route to healthy registered targets across enabled Availability Zones.
                  * At target-group level, cross-zone behavior can be overridden/disabled for supported target types.
                  * If disabled for a target group, ensure each enabled AZ has enough healthy target capacity.
                  * Target stickiness and Lambda targets have restrictions when target-group cross-zone is off.
Interview-safe answer
ALB is cross-zone by default/at the load-balancer level; current ALB target groups can explicitly override that setting in supported cases.
	14. HTTPS / TLS Termination
  

ALB can handle viewer TLS and then forward HTTP or HTTPS to the application.
                  * Attach ACM or IAM certificate to HTTPS listener.
                  * SNI allows multiple domain certificates on one HTTPS listener.
                  * Choose a TLS security policy.
                  * Common pattern: port 80 listener redirects permanently to HTTPS 443.
                  * Backend target group can use HTTP or HTTPS independently.
                  * ALB supports mutual TLS (mTLS) passthrough or verification modes.
Certificate scope
The browser validates the ALB's viewer certificate. If ALB uses HTTPS to targets, target-side TLS is a separate connection.
	15. Security Groups
                  * ALB has its own security group.
                  * Internet-facing ALB SG commonly allows 443 from intended client ranges; 80 only if redirecting HTTP.
                  * Application target SG should allow app port only from the ALB SG.
                  * Avoid opening private application ports directly to the internet.
Classic interview architecture
Internet -> ALB SG :443 -> App SG :8080 -> RDS SG :5432/3306.
	16. AWS WAF
                  * AWS WAF web ACL can be associated with an ALB.
                  * Use managed/custom rules to block or challenge malicious/unwanted HTTP requests.
                  * Common protections: IP reputation, SQLi/XSS patterns, rate-based rules, path/header rules.
                  * WAF operates at application-request level; security groups operate at network connection level.
17. Authentication with Cognito / OIDC
  

ALB can offload some authentication/security responsibilities before requests reach the app.
                  * HTTPS listener rules can authenticate users with Amazon Cognito.
                  * HTTPS listener rules can authenticate with an OpenID Connect compliant identity provider.
                  * ALB maintains authentication session cookies.
                  * After successful authentication, ALB forwards identity-related information to the target.
When useful
Internal dashboards or web applications can use ALB authentication to avoid rebuilding basic OIDC login handling in every service.
	18. mTLS and JWT Validation - Advanced/Current
                  * ALB mutual TLS can verify client X.509 certificates at the listener or pass the certificate chain through to the backend.
                  * mTLS verify mode uses an ALB trust store.
                  * ALB can pass certificate information to targets in X-Amzn-Mtls headers.
                  * Current listener rules also support pre-routing JWT/token validation configuration.
Interview priority
Know HTTPS termination and Cognito/OIDC first. mTLS/JWT validation are useful current capabilities but usually secondary in a standard full-stack interview.
	19. Sticky Sessions
                  * Sticky sessions keep a client associated with the same target for a configured duration.
                  * ALB supports load-balancer-generated duration cookies and application-based cookies.
                  * Useful when application session state is stored locally on a target.
                  * Prefer stateless application design with shared state in ElastiCache/database when possible.
                  * WebSocket connections are naturally pinned to the target that accepted the upgrade.
Why avoid unnecessary stickiness
Stickiness can make traffic uneven and hides state-management problems. Stateless backends scale more cleanly.
	20. Slow Start
                  * Slow start gradually increases traffic sent to newly registered/healthy targets.
                  * Range: 30 to 900 seconds.
                  * Useful when a newly started application needs warm-up time, JIT compilation, cache loading, or connection establishment.
                  * Different from Auto Scaling instance warmup: slow start controls ALB traffic share.
Example
New JVM instance is healthy but its caches/JIT are cold. Slow start prevents it from immediately receiving its full share of traffic.
	21. Deregistration Delay / Connection Draining
                  * When a target is deregistering, ALB stops sending new requests to it.
                  * Existing in-flight requests can continue during the deregistration delay.
                  * Default deregistration delay: 300 seconds.
                  * Valid range: 0 to 3600 seconds.
                  * If no in-flight requests/connections remain, deregistration can complete early.
Deployment use
During scale-in or a rolling deployment, deregistration delay helps prevent active user requests from being cut off abruptly.
	22. Target Group Routing Algorithms
Algorithm
	Meaning
	Round robin
	Default-style even request distribution across healthy targets.
	Least outstanding requests
	Prefer target with fewer outstanding requests.
	Weighted random
	Random distribution with target weight behavior; can use anomaly mitigation/Automatic Target Weights.
	Beginner priority
For interviews, know round-robin-like balancing and health checks first. Routing algorithm tuning is usually an intermediate/advanced topic.
	23. Weighted Target Groups
  

Weighted forwarding is useful for controlled deployments.
                  * One listener rule can forward to multiple target groups with weights.
                  * Weight range supports proportional traffic distribution.
                  * Example: v1=90, v2=10 for canary rollout.
                  * Gradually change weights to shift more traffic to the new version.
                  * A weighted target group becoming unhealthy does not automatically transfer its weight to another target group.
Blue-green pattern
Blue TG = current version; Green TG = new version. Shift 100/0 -> 90/10 -> 50/50 -> 0/100 after validation.
	24. HTTP/2, gRPC, and WebSockets
                  * HTTPS listeners support HTTP/2 from viewers.
                  * Target group protocol version can be HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2, or gRPC for supported instance/IP target groups.
                  * ALB understands gRPC package/service/method routing and gRPC health status codes.
                  * WebSockets work over HTTP/HTTPS listeners and remain connected to the target that accepted the upgrade.
Interview point
ALB is not limited to traditional HTTP/1.1 web pages; it is commonly used for APIs, WebSockets, HTTP/2, and gRPC microservices.
	25. URL and Host Rewrites - Current Feature
                  * Listener rules can apply a host-header rewrite transform.
                  * Listener rules can apply a URL rewrite transform.
                  * Transforms use regex matching plus replacement strings.
                  * Useful for migrations, service normalization, or routing without forcing backend changes.
Example
Incoming /v1/users/123 can be rewritten to /users/123 before forwarding to a newer backend.
	26. Monitoring and Access Logs
Metric / Signal
	What It Helps Diagnose
	RequestCount
	Overall request volume
	TargetResponseTime
	Backend latency
	HTTPCode_ELB_4XX/5XX
	Errors generated by load balancer
	HTTPCode_Target_4XX/5XX
	Errors returned by application targets
	HealthyHostCount / UnHealthyHostCount
	Target availability
	RejectedConnectionCount
	Connection rejection conditions
	                  * Use CloudWatch metrics and alarms.
                  * Enable ALB access logs for request-level investigation when needed.
                  * Connection logs can help with TLS/mTLS troubleshooting where configured.
Very common interview distinction
ELB 5xx vs Target 5xx: one points more toward load-balancer/proxy problems; the other points toward your application targets.
	27. Common HTTP Error Troubleshooting
Status
	Possible Cause
	Check
	400
	Malformed client request
	Headers/request format
	401/403
	Authentication/WAF/app authorization
	Auth action, WAF, target app
	404
	Rule/path/app route
	Listener rule + app route
	502
	Target closed/reset/bad protocol response
	Target logs, protocol, networking
	503
	No usable healthy registered targets / capacity issue
	Target health/count
	504
	Target did not respond before timeout
	App latency, dependencies, idle timeout
	28. ALB vs API Gateway
Area
	ALB
	API Gateway
	Primary purpose
	Layer-7 load balancing/routing
	API management
	Backends
	EC2/ECS/IP/Lambda
	Lambda, HTTP, AWS services, VPC integrations
	Auth
	Cognito/OIDC/JWT/mTLS options
	IAM/JWT/Cognito/Lambda authorizers
	API keys/usage plans
	No
	REST API supports
	Caching
	No response cache
	REST API optional cache
	Best fit
	Web apps/microservices behind load balancer
	Managed public APIs/API products
	29. ALB vs CloudFront
Area
	ALB
	CloudFront
	Scope
	Regional
	Global edge network
	Primary job
	Load balance/routing to targets
	CDN/caching/edge delivery
	Caching
	No CDN response cache
	Yes
	Backend
	Targets in VPC/Lambda
	Origins such as ALB/S3/API
	Use together
	ALB can be CloudFront origin
	CloudFront sits in front of ALB
	30. Common Production Architecture
                  * Route 53 -> CloudFront/WAF if global CDN/edge features are needed.
                  * Internet-facing ALB in public subnets across multiple AZs.
                  * HTTPS :443 listener; HTTP :80 redirects to HTTPS.
                  * Path/host rules forward to separate target groups.
                  * EC2/ECS application targets in private subnets.
                  * Separate security groups: ALB SG -> App SG -> DB SG.
                  * Auto Scaling/ECS handles backend capacity.
                  * CloudWatch metrics + ALB access logs.
Design principle
Expose the ALB, not the EC2 instances. Keep application targets private whenever the architecture allows it.
	31. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Route /api to backend service and /admin to another.
Create path-based listener rules pointing to separate target groups.
Scenario: api.example.com and shop.example.com use the same ALB.
Use host-header routing rules.
Scenario: One EC2 instance crashes.
ALB health check marks it unhealthy and stops normal routing to it; Auto Scaling can replace the instance.
Scenario: Deploy v2 to 10% of users.
Create a second target group and use weighted forwarding 90/10.
Scenario: New instances are healthy but slow for the first minute.
Enable target-group slow start.
Scenario: Requests are cut off during scale-in.
Check deregistration delay and graceful application shutdown.
Scenario: Application only works if the same user stays on one server.
Use sticky sessions temporarily, but prefer external/shared session state such as ElastiCache.
Scenario: Need to redirect HTTP to HTTPS.
Create HTTP :80 listener with redirect action to HTTPS :443.
Scenario: ALB is healthy but target returns 500.
Look at Target 5xx/application logs; ALB is only forwarding the request.
Scenario: Need users authenticated before app receives traffic.
Use Cognito/OIDC authentication on an HTTPS listener rule where appropriate.
32. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Confirm ALB DNS resolves and listener is on the expected protocol/port.
2.  Check ALB security group inbound/outbound rules.
3.  Check listener rule priority and matching host/path/header/query/method conditions.
4.  Check target group has registered targets.
5.  Check target health status and health-check path/port/success codes.
6.  Check target security group allows the app port from the ALB security group.
7.  Check application process is listening on the expected target port/interface.
8.  Compare ELB-generated 5xx metrics with Target 5xx metrics.
9.  Check deregistration/draining/slow-start/stickiness behavior during deployments.
10.  Use ALB access logs and application logs for request correlation.
33. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is an Application Load Balancer?
Answer: ALB is AWS's Layer-7 load balancer for HTTP/HTTPS applications. It evaluates request-level routing rules and forwards traffic to healthy target groups.
2. Which OSI layer does ALB work at?
Answer: Layer 7, the application layer.
3. What protocols does ALB support?
Answer: HTTP and HTTPS listeners, plus WebSockets. HTTPS listeners support HTTP/2 from viewers, and supported target groups can use HTTP/2 or gRPC.
4. What is a listener?
Answer: The protocol and port where the ALB accepts connections, such as HTTP:80 or HTTPS:443.
5. What is a listener rule?
Answer: A prioritized rule containing conditions and actions that determines how matching requests are handled.
6. What is the default rule?
Answer: The rule that runs last when no higher-priority custom rule matches. It has no conditions.
7. What routing conditions can ALB use?
Answer: Host header, path pattern, HTTP header, HTTP method, query string, and source IP.
8. What actions can ALB rules perform?
Answer: Forward, redirect, fixed response, and supported pre-routing authentication/token actions on HTTPS listeners.
9. What is a target group?
Answer: A logical group of registered backend destinations plus protocol/port/health-check and traffic-management settings.
10. What target types can ALB use?
Answer: Instance, IP, and Lambda.
11. Why use IP target type?
Answer: It is common for ECS tasks/Fargate and for private IP targets that are not represented by an EC2 instance ID in the target group.
12. What is a health check?
Answer: A periodic request from ALB to a target used to decide whether that target should receive normal traffic.
13. What happens when a target is unhealthy?
Answer: ALB stops sending it normal new traffic until it becomes healthy again.
14. What happens if all targets are unhealthy?
Answer: ALB can fail open and route to unhealthy targets, so health alarms and resilient applications are still important.
15. ALB vs Auto Scaling?
Answer: ALB distributes traffic; Auto Scaling launches and terminates EC2 instances.
16. Does ALB create EC2 instances?
Answer: No. EC2 Auto Scaling manages instance count.
17. What is cross-zone load balancing?
Answer: Routing requests from ALB nodes to targets in multiple enabled Availability Zones. It is always on at the ALB load-balancer level, with current target-group-level overrides supported in some cases.
18. How do you enable HTTPS on ALB?
Answer: Create an HTTPS listener, attach an ACM/IAM certificate, choose a TLS security policy, and optionally redirect HTTP to HTTPS.
19. Can one HTTPS listener serve multiple domain certificates?
Answer: Yes. ALB supports SNI and multiple certificates.
20. What is mutual TLS?
Answer: mTLS authenticates both server and client certificates. ALB supports passthrough and verify modes for client certificates.
21. What are sticky sessions?
Answer: Cookie-based affinity that keeps a client's requests on the same target for a configured duration.
22. Why avoid sticky sessions when possible?
Answer: They can create uneven load and couple session state to one server. Stateless apps with shared session storage scale more cleanly.
23. What is slow start?
Answer: A target-group feature that gradually increases traffic to a newly healthy target over 30-900 seconds.
24. What is deregistration delay?
Answer: The draining period after a target is removed, during which ALB stops new requests but allows existing requests/connections to finish. Default is 300 seconds.
25. What are weighted target groups?
Answer: A forward action can split traffic among multiple target groups using weights, useful for canary and blue-green deployments.
26. Does ALB automatically fail over weighted traffic to another target group if one group is unhealthy?
Answer: No. Weighted forwarding does not automatically move the unhealthy group's share to another group.
27. Can ALB authenticate users?
Answer: Yes. HTTPS listener rules support authentication with Amazon Cognito or OIDC identity providers, and current ALB also has newer token-validation capabilities.
28. Can ALB work with AWS WAF?
Answer: Yes. A WAF web ACL can be associated with an ALB to filter application-layer requests.
29. ALB vs NLB?
Answer: ALB is Layer 7 and request-aware; NLB is Layer 4 and is better for raw TCP/UDP/TLS-style high-performance network traffic and static-IP requirements.
30. ALB vs API Gateway?
Answer: ALB is primarily regional Layer-7 load balancing for application targets. API Gateway is an API-management service with API lifecycle, authorizers, usage plans, and integrations.
31. ALB vs CloudFront?
Answer: ALB is regional load balancing; CloudFront is global CDN/edge caching. CloudFront often uses ALB as an origin.
32. Can ALB route gRPC?
Answer: Yes. Target groups can use the gRPC protocol version for supported instance/IP targets behind an HTTPS listener.
33. What is host-based routing?
Answer: Routing by the HTTP Host header, such as api.example.com to one target group and shop.example.com to another.
34. What is path-based routing?
Answer: Routing by URL path, such as /api/* to one target group and /images/* to another.
35. What metrics should you remember?
Answer: RequestCount, TargetResponseTime, HealthyHostCount/UnHealthyHostCount, ELB 4xx/5xx, Target 4xx/5xx, and access logs for request-level troubleshooting.
34. Final ALB Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	ALB
	Layer-7 HTTP/HTTPS load balancer
	Listener
	Protocol + port accepting client traffic
	Rule
	Conditions + actions evaluated by priority
	Target Group
	Logical backend group + health settings
	Target Type
	instance / ip / lambda
	Health Check
	Determines target eligibility for traffic
	Host Routing
	Route by domain/Host header
	Path Routing
	Route by URL path
	Redirect
	Common HTTP -> HTTPS action
	Fixed Response
	ALB returns response without target
	Cross-Zone
	ALB routes across enabled AZs
	TLS Termination
	ALB decrypts viewer HTTPS
	SNI
	Multiple certificates/domains on listener
	mTLS
	Client certificate authentication
	Cognito/OIDC
	ALB user authentication
	Sticky Session
	Cookie-based target affinity
	Slow Start
	Gradually ramp traffic to new target
	Deregistration Delay
	Graceful draining on removal
	Weighted TG
	Canary/blue-green traffic splitting
	HTTP/2 / gRPC
	Modern application protocols
	WAF
	Application-layer request filtering
	ALB + ASG
	ALB routes; ASG scales compute
	

If you are short on time, revise these first
ALB basics -> listener -> rules -> target groups -> health checks -> host/path routing -> ALB + Auto Scaling -> HTTPS/TLS -> security groups -> sticky/slow-start/draining -> weighted target groups -> ALB vs NLB/API Gateway/CloudFront -> interview Q&A.
	35. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * ALB listener rules currently support host, path, HTTP header, method, query string, and source-IP conditions.
                  * Rules now support host-header and URL rewrite transforms.
                  * ALB load-balancer-level cross-zone routing is always enabled; supported target groups can override it.
                  * ALB target groups can use instance, IP, or Lambda targets.
                  * Supported target-group protocol versions include HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2, and gRPC.
                  * ALB supports Cognito/OIDC authentication, mutual TLS, and newer pre-routing token validation on supported HTTPS rules.
                  * Weighted target groups are useful for canary/blue-green, but ALB does not automatically redistribute weight away from an unhealthy target group.
                  * Target-group deregistration delay defaults to 300 seconds; slow-start range is 30-900 seconds.
36. AWS Documentation References
                  * Elastic Load Balancing - Application Load Balancers
                  * Listeners for your Application Load Balancers
                  * Listener rules and condition types
                  * Action types for listener rules
                  * Target groups for Application Load Balancers
                  * Health checks for Application Load Balancer target groups
                  * Target group attributes: stickiness, slow start, deregistration delay, cross-zone
                  * Authenticate users using an Application Load Balancer
                  * Mutual TLS authentication for Application Load Balancers
                  * HTTP/2 and gRPC target protocol versions


AWS ELB
AWS ELB
Interview Study Notes
Beginner to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Focus | Visual Edition
Study tip: focus first on ALB vs NLB, listeners, target groups, health checks, Auto Scaling, Multi-AZ, and HTTPS termination.


Study goal
Be able to explain what ELB does, ALB vs NLB, target groups, listeners, health checks, Auto Scaling integration, HTTPS/TLS, security, and common failure/scaling scenarios.
	

Fast Revision Map
Request flow at a glance:
1. Client sends a request to the load balancer DNS name.
2. A listener receives the request on a configured port/protocol such as HTTP 80 or HTTPS 443.
3. For ALB, listener rules choose the correct target group using conditions such as host or path.
4. Health checks determine which registered targets are healthy.
5. ELB forwards the request to a healthy target and continues distributing traffic across available capacity.
________________


1. What is ELB?
ELB stands for Elastic Load Balancing. It distributes incoming traffic across multiple healthy targets, such as EC2 instances, containers, IP addresses, and other supported targets. This improves availability and helps applications scale.
  

Interview line
ELB acts as a single entry point and distributes traffic only to healthy registered targets.
	

2. Why do we use a Load Balancer?
                  * Distribute traffic across multiple application servers.
                  * Avoid one server becoming the only bottleneck or single point of failure.
                  * Route new traffic away from unhealthy targets.
                  * Support high availability across multiple Availability Zones.
                  * Work with Auto Scaling so new application instances can receive traffic.
                  * Centralize HTTPS/TLS certificate handling.
                  * Perform intelligent request routing with an Application Load Balancer.
3. ELB Types You Must Know
Type
	Layer
	Common Use
	Remember
	Application Load Balancer (ALB)
	Layer 7
	HTTP/HTTPS web apps, APIs, microservices
	Content-aware routing
	Network Load Balancer (NLB)
	Layer 4
	TCP/UDP/TLS and very high-performance network traffic
	Low latency + network-level routing
	Gateway Load Balancer (GWLB)
	Layer 3
	Firewalls, IDS/IPS, virtual appliances
	Security appliance traffic
	Classic Load Balancer (CLB)
	Previous generation
	Legacy workloads
	Know it exists; prefer current generation for new designs
	

Memory trick
ALB = application-aware HTTP routing. NLB = network/transport traffic. GWLB = virtual network appliances.
	

4. Application Load Balancer (ALB)
ALB operates at Layer 7. It understands HTTP/HTTPS requests and can route based on request properties.
                  * Best fit for most web applications and REST APIs.
                  * Supports HTTP and HTTPS.
                  * Supports host-based and path-based routing.
                  * Can forward to multiple target groups.
                  * Works well for microservices and containerized applications.
                  * Can integrate with AWS WAF for web application filtering.
                  * Supports features such as WebSockets and HTTP/2.
Path-Based and Host-Based Routing
  

5. Network Load Balancer (NLB)
NLB operates at Layer 4 and is designed for high-performance network traffic. It handles connections using transport/network information rather than HTTP path or host rules.
                  * Common protocols include TCP, TLS, and UDP. Current AWS documentation also includes QUIC support.
                  * Designed for very high throughput and low latency.
                  * Can use static IP addresses / Elastic IPs in supported configurations.
                  * Useful when a workload is not HTTP-based or needs network-level behavior.
                  * Can preserve client source IP in supported target configurations.
                  * Modern NLBs can be created with security groups; this should not be confused with older material saying NLB never has security groups.
Typical NLB flow: a client opens a TCP, UDP, or TLS connection. The NLB selects a healthy target and forwards the connection using Layer 4 information such as IP address, port, and protocol.
6. ALB vs NLB
Feature
	ALB
	NLB
	OSI layer
	Layer 7
	Layer 4
	Typical protocols
	HTTP, HTTPS
	TCP, TLS, UDP (and newer QUIC support)
	Path routing
	Yes
	Not the normal use case
	Host routing
	Yes
	Not the normal use case
	Static IP requirement
	Usually use DNS name
	Supported configurations can use static IPs
	Typical use
	Web apps, APIs, microservices
	High-throughput / low-latency network traffic
	Decision basis
	HTTP request information
	Connection / network information
	

Most common interview question
If the interviewer says HTTP API with /users and /orders routing, think ALB. If they say very high-performance TCP/UDP traffic or static IP requirement, think NLB.
	

7. Gateway Load Balancer (GWLB)
GWLB is used to deploy, scale, and manage fleets of virtual network appliances such as firewalls, intrusion detection/prevention systems, and deep packet inspection systems.
Typical GWLB flow: application or network traffic is transparently steered through a Gateway Load Balancer to a fleet of virtual security appliances such as firewalls, IDS, or IPS systems.
For a normal full-stack interview, knowing its purpose is usually enough.
8. Target Groups
A target group is a logical group of destinations that receive traffic from a load balancer. Health checks are configured at the target-group level.
Example target-group design:
Request / Service
	Target Group
	Example Targets
	/api/*
	API Target Group
	EC2-1, EC2-2
	/admin/*
	Admin Target Group
	EC2-3, EC2-4
	                  * Targets can include EC2 instances and IP addresses; ALB also supports Lambda targets.
                  * A target can participate in routing only when registered with the relevant target group.
                  * Different target groups can represent different services in a microservices architecture.
9. Listener and Listener Rules
A listener waits for client connection requests on a configured protocol and port. Listener rules decide what action to take.
Common listener setup: port 80 (HTTP) redirects to HTTPS, while port 443 (HTTPS) evaluates listener rules and forwards matching requests to the appropriate target group.
Remember
Listener = where traffic enters. Target group = where traffic goes.
	

10. Health Checks
ELB continuously evaluates target health. New requests are routed to healthy targets instead of unhealthy ones.
  

                  * Typical HTTP health endpoint: /health or /actuator/health.
                  * Health check settings include protocol, port, path (for HTTP/HTTPS), interval, timeout, and healthy/unhealthy thresholds.
                  * An application may be running but still be considered unhealthy if the health endpoint fails.
11. ELB + Auto Scaling
Elastic Load Balancing and EC2 Auto Scaling are commonly used together.
  

                  * When traffic rises, Auto Scaling can launch more instances.
                  * New instances register with the target group and begin receiving traffic after becoming healthy.
                  * When traffic falls, instances can be removed while ELB keeps routing to the remaining healthy targets.
12. High Availability Across Availability Zones
  

Using healthy targets in multiple Availability Zones helps the application continue serving traffic when an instance or even an Availability Zone has problems.
13. Internet-Facing vs Internal Load Balancer
Type
	Who can reach it?
	Typical Use
	Internet-facing
	Public clients through the internet
	Public website / public API
	Internal
	Private clients within connected private networks/VPC
	Internal APIs / service-to-service traffic
	

Internet-facing load balancer: accepts traffic from public clients.
Internal load balancer: reachable only inside the VPC and commonly used for service-to-service traffic.
14. HTTPS / TLS Termination
A load balancer can terminate encrypted client connections, so certificates can be managed centrally rather than on every application server.
  

                  * AWS Certificate Manager (ACM) is commonly used to manage certificates for an HTTPS ALB listener.
                  * A common setup redirects HTTP port 80 to HTTPS port 443.
                  * Backend traffic can also be encrypted when required.
15. Sticky Sessions (Session Affinity)
By default, requests can be distributed independently. Stickiness can bind a client session to a particular target for a period.
With stickiness enabled, repeated requests from the same client can be associated with the same target for a configured period. For highly scalable systems, keeping session state outside individual instances is often preferable.
ALB stickiness is commonly cookie-based and configured at the target group level.
Design note
Sticky sessions can be useful for stateful applications, but stateless application servers with session state stored externally usually scale more cleanly.
	

16. Cross-Zone Load Balancing
Cross-zone load balancing concerns how traffic is distributed among registered targets across enabled Availability Zones. Exact defaults and configuration behavior differ by load balancer type, so focus on the concept in interviews rather than memorizing old defaults.
Cross-zone load balancing determines whether a load balancer node can distribute traffic to healthy registered targets across other enabled Availability Zones. Exact defaults and behavior depend on the load balancer type.
17. Security Groups
A common secure ALB pattern is to expose only the load balancer and allow the application instances to accept traffic only from the load balancer security group.
Security model: expose the load balancer on required client ports, and configure application instances to accept their application port only from the load balancer security group.
                  * ALB SG: allow required inbound client ports, commonly 443.
                  * EC2 SG: allow application port from the ALB security group instead of from the entire internet.
                  * Current NLB supports associated security groups when configured that way at creation, so avoid outdated blanket statements that NLB never uses security groups.
18. DNS Name and Route 53
AWS provides a DNS name for a load balancer. Applications and DNS records should reference the load balancer rather than hardcoding its changing infrastructure IP addresses.
Typical DNS flow: Route 53 maps your application domain to the load balancer DNS name, and the load balancer then routes traffic to healthy registered targets.
19. Deregistration Delay / Connection Draining
When a target is removed or being replaced, the load balancer can allow in-flight requests or connections to finish for a configured deregistration delay instead of cutting them off immediately.
Deregistration sequence: ELB stops sending new requests to the target, allows in-flight work to finish during the deregistration delay, and then fully removes the target from traffic rotation.
Interview use
This matters during deployments, scale-in events, and instance replacement.
	

20. Monitoring and Logs
Monitor load balancers with CloudWatch metrics and, when needed, access logs.
                  * Request / connection counts
                  * Target response time or latency-related metrics
                  * Healthy and unhealthy host counts
                  * HTTP 4xx / 5xx error metrics for ALB
                  * Processed bytes / network metrics
                  * Access logs for request-level investigation
21. ALB + AWS WAF
AWS WAF can be associated with an Application Load Balancer to filter malicious or unwanted web requests using web ACL rules.
Request path with WAF: AWS WAF evaluates incoming web requests against web ACL rules before the ALB forwards allowed traffic to the application.
Examples include blocking known bad IP ranges, filtering common attack patterns, or enforcing application-layer rules.
22. ELB vs Auto Scaling
ELB
	Auto Scaling
	Distributes traffic
	Changes the number of compute instances
	Checks target health for routing
	Launches / terminates instances based on policy
	Provides a traffic entry point
	Provides compute capacity management
	Often used together
	Often used together
	

Interview line
ELB does not create more EC2 instances by itself. Auto Scaling changes capacity; ELB distributes traffic to the available healthy capacity.
	

23. ELB vs Route 53
ELB
	Route 53
	Distributes application/network traffic to targets
	DNS service
	Works at load-balancing/data-plane level
	Maps domain names to endpoints/IPs using DNS
	Health-aware target routing inside the load balancer
	Can use DNS routing policies and health checks
	Example: ALB -> EC2 instances
	Example: example.com -> ALB
	

24. Typical Production Architecture
Typical production ELB architecture: Route 53 resolves the application domain to an internet-facing ALB. The ALB distributes traffic to healthy application instances in an Auto Scaling group spread across multiple Availability Zones. The application layer can then access private services such as RDS.
Possible additions: CloudFront in front, WAF at the application edge, private subnets for application servers, and Multi-AZ RDS behind the application tier.
25. Scenario: One EC2 Instance Fails
Question: One backend EC2 instance crashes. What happens?
Failure handling: the health check begins failing, the target is marked unhealthy after the configured threshold, and new requests are routed to the remaining healthy targets. Auto Scaling can replace lost capacity if configured.
If Auto Scaling is configured, the failed capacity can also be replaced according to the Auto Scaling health and scaling configuration.
26. Scenario: Traffic Suddenly Increases
Question: Your application receives a sudden traffic spike. How do ELB and Auto Scaling help?
Traffic spike handling: a scaling metric crosses its threshold, Auto Scaling launches additional instances, the new instances register with the target group, and ELB starts sending traffic to them after they pass health checks.
27. Scenario: Route /api and /admin Differently
Use an Application Load Balancer with listener rules and separate target groups.
Example listener rules: /api/* goes to the API target group, /admin/* goes to the Admin target group, and the default rule goes to the main web target group.
28. Scenario: Very High-Performance TCP Traffic
Use a Network Load Balancer when the requirement is transport-layer routing, very high throughput/low latency, or a non-HTTP protocol where ALB request-aware routing is not needed.
29. Common Interview Questions
Question
	Short Interview Answer
	What is ELB?
	A managed AWS service that distributes incoming traffic across healthy registered targets.
	ALB vs NLB?
	ALB is Layer 7 and HTTP/HTTPS-aware; NLB is Layer 4 and designed for high-performance TCP/UDP/TLS-style traffic.
	What is a target group?
	A logical group of registered destinations to which a load balancer forwards traffic.
	What is a listener?
	The protocol and port on which the load balancer accepts client connections, together with routing actions/rules.
	What is a health check?
	A periodic check used to decide whether a target should receive new traffic.
	How do ELB and Auto Scaling work together?
	Auto Scaling changes compute capacity; ELB distributes traffic among healthy registered instances.
	What are sticky sessions?
	A feature that keeps a client session associated with the same target for a period.
	How do you enable HTTPS?
	Create an HTTPS listener and attach a suitable TLS certificate, commonly from ACM.
	How do you make the app tier safer?
	Expose the ALB, keep app servers private where appropriate, and allow app inbound traffic from the ALB security group.
	What if one target becomes unhealthy?
	The load balancer stops routing new traffic to that target and continues using healthy targets.
	

30. What You Do NOT Need to Go Deep Into Yet
                  * Internal ELB implementation details.
                  * Every CloudWatch metric name and billing dimension.
                  * Advanced GWLB / GENEVE packet-level internals.
                  * All NLB listener-rule edge cases.
                  * Detailed load-balancing algorithm tuning unless the role is cloud/network focused.
                  * Every historical Classic Load Balancer behavior.
Final ELB Cheat Sheet

	For ALB, decides what action to take and which target group receives traffic.

	CloudWatch / Logs

Top 8 topics to revise before the interview

AWS DynamoDB - Interview Study Notes
AWS DynamoDB - Interview Study Notes
Goal of these notes
Understand DynamoDB from first principles: NoSQL data model, partition/sort keys, Query vs Scan, indexes, consistency, capacity modes, transactions, TTL, Streams, DAX, global tables, and how to answer practical system-design questions.
	1. What is Amazon DynamoDB?
Amazon DynamoDB is AWS's fully managed, serverless NoSQL database. It supports key-value and document data models and is designed for predictable single-digit millisecond performance at large scale.
                  * No servers, database instances, patching, or storage provisioning to manage.
                  * Automatically partitions data and scales storage.
                  * Supports on-demand and provisioned throughput modes.
                  * Integrates naturally with Lambda, API Gateway, IAM, Streams, Global Tables, and DAX.
                  * Unlike an RDBMS, DynamoDB does not provide SQL-style JOIN operations.
  

A DynamoDB table contains items, and each item contains attributes.
2. Table, Item, and Attribute
Concept
	Relational Analogy
	Meaning
	Table
	Table
	Collection of items
	Item
	Row
	One record/document
	Attribute
	Column/value
	Named field inside an item
	                  * Items in the same table may have different non-key attributes.
                  * Only primary-key and secondary-index key attributes need to follow the declared key schema.
                  * Maximum item size is 400 KB, including attribute names and values.
Large object pattern
Do not store large images/videos in DynamoDB. Store the object in S3 and keep the S3 key/URL plus metadata in DynamoDB.
	3. Primary Keys
  

The primary key uniquely identifies an item. Composite keys also let related items be sorted together.
Key Type
	Structure
	Example
	Simple
	Partition key only
	userId = U101
	Composite
	Partition key + sort key
	customerId = C101, orderDate#orderId = 2026-09-01#O1
	4. What Does the Partition Key Do?
                  * DynamoDB hashes the partition-key value to distribute items across physical storage partitions.
                  * A good partition key has many distinct values and spreads reads/writes evenly.
                  * Examples: userId, orderId, deviceId, customerId.
                  * Poor example for a huge workload: status = ACTIVE/INACTIVE, because most traffic can concentrate on very few values.
Hot partition
A hot partition happens when too much traffic concentrates on a small set of partition-key values. Good key design is the first defense; adaptive capacity helps but does not remove all per-partition constraints.
	5. Sort Key
                  * Items with the same partition-key value can have different sort-key values.
                  * Within a partition key, DynamoDB stores results in sort-key order.
                  * Useful for one-to-many data such as customer -> orders, device -> readings, user -> posts.
                  * Useful operators include equality, comparison/range conditions, and begins_with.
Example
PK = CUSTOMER#123, SK = ORDER#2026-09-29#9001. Query the customer partition and use sort-key conditions to fetch only a time range or item type.
	6. Design from Access Patterns
DynamoDB modeling normally starts with the questions your application must answer.
                  * Get user by userId.
                  * Get all orders for customer X.
                  * Get orders for customer X during a date range.
                  * Find all orders with a given status.
                  * Get products by category and price.
Interview mindset
In an RDBMS you often normalize first and query later. In DynamoDB you identify access patterns first, then design keys and indexes so those access patterns are efficient.
	7. Query vs Scan
  

Query is targeted. Scan reads the entire table or index before optional filtering.
Area
	Query
	Scan
	Partition key
	Equality required
	Not required
	Sort key
	Optional key condition
	Not a key requirement
	Data read
	Targeted partition/index
	All items/index entries
	Efficiency
	Usually efficient
	Can be expensive at scale
	Use
	Normal application access
	Admin jobs, migrations, rare broad reads
	Important
FilterExpression does not make a Scan cheap. DynamoDB reads items first, then filters the returned results.
	8. GetItem, PutItem, UpdateItem, DeleteItem
Operation
	Purpose
	GetItem
	Read one item by its complete primary key
	PutItem
	Create or fully replace an item
	UpdateItem
	Modify selected attributes; supports atomic counters and expressions
	DeleteItem
	Delete one item by primary key
	Query
	Read items sharing a partition-key value
	Scan
	Read all items/entries
	9. Condition Expressions
Conditional writes make a write succeed only if a condition is true.
                  * Create only if an item does not already exist.
                  * Update only if version = 7.
                  * Decrease stock only if stock >= requested quantity.
                  * Delete only if status = CANCELLED.
Why this matters
Condition expressions let DynamoDB enforce concurrency/business preconditions atomically, preventing many read-then-write race conditions.
	10. Optimistic Locking
                  * Store a version attribute in the item.
                  * Client reads item with version N.
                  * Client updates using condition version = N and writes version N+1.
                  * If another writer changed it first, the condition fails and the application can retry/reconcile.
Interview use
Optimistic locking is useful when concurrent writers might overwrite each other but pessimistic database locking is not available or desirable.
	11. Global Secondary Index (GSI) vs Local Secondary Index (LSI)
  

GSIs are much more common because they allow a completely different query key and can be added later.
Feature
	GSI
	LSI
	Partition key
	Can be different
	Must be same as base table
	Sort key
	Optional / can differ
	Required and differs from table sort key
	Create later
	Yes
	No - create with table
	Delete later
	Yes
	No
	Read consistency
	Eventually consistent only
	Eventual or strong
	Throughput
	Separate from table for provisioned mode
	Shares table throughput
	Per-PK item collection limit
	No LSI-style 10 GB limit
	10 GB
	Default maximum count
	20
	5
	12. GSI Example
Base table:
                  * PK = orderId
                  * Attributes: customerId, status, createdAt, total
Requirement: 'Show all PENDING orders for a customer.'
                  * Create GSI with PK = customerId and SK = status#createdAt.
                  * Query customerId and use a sort-key prefix/range.
Index design
Create an index because you have a real access pattern. Too many indexes increase storage and write cost because writes must maintain index entries.
	13. Sparse Index
A GSI only contains items that have the GSI key attributes.
                  * This can intentionally create a sparse index.
                  * Example: only failed orders contain escalationStatus = FAILED.
                  * GSI PK = escalationStatus gives a small index containing only items needing attention.
Why useful
Sparse indexes can make rare-condition queries cheaper and faster than indexing every item.
	14. Read Consistency
  

Choose strong consistency only where stale reads are unacceptable.
                  * Eventually consistent reads are the default.
                  * Strongly consistent reads return the latest committed value.
                  * Strong reads are supported for tables and LSIs.
                  * GSI reads are always eventually consistent.
                  * Eventually consistent reads consume half the read capacity of strong reads for the same item size.
Common interview scenario
A profile page can often tolerate eventual consistency. A just-updated critical balance/state may require stronger concurrency design or a strongly consistent table read.
	15. Capacity Modes
  

Most new/interview projects can start with on-demand unless traffic is predictable and provisioned economics matter.
Area
	On-Demand
	Provisioned
	Capacity planning
	Not required
	Configure RCU/WCU
	Billing
	Pay per request
	Pay for provisioned throughput
	Traffic
	Variable/unpredictable
	Steady/predictable
	Scaling
	Automatic
	Manual or Auto Scaling
	Current AWS guidance
	Default/recommended for most workloads
	Use when predictability/cost control justifies it
	16. RCU and WCU
                  * 1 RCU = one strongly consistent read/sec for an item up to 4 KB.
                  * 1 RCU = two eventually consistent reads/sec for an item up to 4 KB.
                  * 1 WCU = one write/sec for an item up to 1 KB.
                  * Larger items round up to the next capacity-unit size.
                  * Transactional reads/writes consume twice the corresponding units.
Example
A 6 KB strongly consistent read requires 2 RCUs. A 1.5 KB write requires 2 WCUs.
	17. Throttling and Hot Partitions
                  * Provisioned requests can throttle when available capacity is exceeded.
                  * On-demand can also throttle when table/account/request scaling limits or configured maximum throughput are exceeded.
                  * Poor partition-key distribution can create hot keys/partitions.
                  * DynamoDB provides burst and adaptive capacity to help uneven workloads, but key design still matters.
                  * Use exponential backoff/retry behavior provided by AWS SDKs and monitor throttling metrics.
Per-partition design point
A physical partition is designed for up to 3,000 read units/sec and 1,000 write units/sec. Avoid concentrating very high traffic on one logical key.
	18. Transactions
DynamoDB supports ACID transactions for multi-item all-or-nothing operations.
                  * TransactWriteItems supports Put, Update, Delete, and ConditionCheck actions.
                  * Up to 100 unique items can participate in a transaction.
                  * Aggregate transaction item size cannot exceed 4 MB.
                  * All items/tables in a transaction must be in the same AWS account and Region.
                  * Transactions cost more capacity than non-transactional operations.
When to use
Use transactions when several related changes truly must commit or fail together, such as order state + inventory reservation + ledger item. Do not use them by default for every write.
	19. Batch Operations vs Transactions
Area
	BatchWriteItem
	TransactWriteItems
	Purpose
	Efficient bulk Put/Delete
	All-or-nothing business transaction
	Atomic as whole
	No
	Yes
	Max actions
	25 Put/Delete operations
	Up to 100 actions
	UpdateItem
	No
	Yes
	Conditions
	No condition expressions in BatchWriteItem
	Yes
	Failure handling
	Returns unprocessed items
	Entire transaction fails
	20. Time to Live (TTL)
                  * TTL lets each item contain an expiration timestamp in Unix epoch seconds.
                  * DynamoDB automatically deletes expired items, typically within a few days after expiry.
                  * TTL deletion does not consume write throughput in the Region where the expiry deletion occurs.
                  * Expired items may still appear in reads until physically deleted, so applications can filter them when necessary.
                  * Good use cases: sessions, temporary tokens, old events, transient cache-like records.
Important
TTL is asynchronous cleanup, not an exact scheduler. Do not rely on an item disappearing at the exact expiry second.
	21. DynamoDB Streams
  

Streams turn table changes into near-real-time events.
                  * Records INSERT, MODIFY, and REMOVE events.
                  * Can include keys, old image, new image, or both depending on stream view type.
                  * Stream records are retained for up to 24 hours.
                  * Commonly consumed using AWS Lambda.
                  * Use cases: audit workflows, notifications, search indexing, materialized views, downstream synchronization.
Ordering
DynamoDB Streams preserves modification order for each individual item. Do not assume a single total ordering across all table items.
	22. DAX (DynamoDB Accelerator)
                  * DAX is an in-memory cache designed specifically for DynamoDB.
                  * A cache hit can provide microsecond response times.
                  * Best for read-heavy, eventually consistent workloads with repeated reads.
                  * On a cache miss, DAX reads from DynamoDB and caches the result.
                  * DAX is not useful if most reads are unique or if the workload requires strongly consistent reads.
DAX vs ElastiCache
DAX is DynamoDB-specific and API-compatible for supported operations. ElastiCache is a general-purpose Redis/Valkey/Memcached cache that your application manages explicitly.
	23. Global Tables
  

TTL, DAX, and Global Tables each address a different problem.
                  * Global Tables replicate DynamoDB data across multiple AWS Regions.
                  * Multi-active: applications can read and write in replica Regions.
                  * Useful for global low-latency applications and multi-Region availability.
                  * Traditional/default mode is Multi-Region Eventual Consistency (MREC).
                  * AWS added Multi-Region Strong Consistency (MRSC) as a newer option for eligible designs.
MREC vs MRSC
MREC favors lower write latency and asynchronously replicates changes. MRSC synchronously replicates before successful writes and supports strong reads across replicas, with different restrictions and higher latency tradeoffs.
	24. DynamoDB vs RDS
Area
	DynamoDB
	RDS
	Model
	NoSQL key-value/document
	Relational SQL
	Schema
	Flexible non-key attributes
	Defined table schema
	JOINs
	No
	Yes
	Scaling
	Serverless horizontal scaling
	Instance/storage/read-replica scaling model
	Access design
	Key/access-pattern driven
	Flexible SQL/ad-hoc querying
	Transactions
	Supported, but NoSQL modeling first
	Core relational feature
	Best fit
	Known high-scale access patterns
	Relational data and query flexibility
	Interview answer
Choose DynamoDB when access patterns are known and you need massive scale, low operational overhead, and predictable key-based performance. Choose RDS when relationships, joins, ad-hoc SQL, and relational constraints are central.
	25. Single-Table Design - Beginner View
Single-table design stores multiple entity types in one DynamoDB table using carefully designed partition and sort keys.
                  * Example PK = CUSTOMER#123, with SK values PROFILE, ORDER#001, ORDER#002.
                  * This can retrieve related entities with one Query.
                  * It reduces joins by colocating data needed by an access pattern.
                  * It is powerful but adds modeling complexity; you do not need to force every small project into one table.
Interview advice
Understand the idea and why it exists. For a 3-YOE full-stack interview, explaining partition/sort keys, access patterns, GSIs, and Query vs Scan clearly is usually more important than mastering every single-table pattern.
	26. Common Architecture Example
Serverless Orders API:
                  * API Gateway receives the HTTP request.
                  * Lambda validates the request and uses an IAM execution role.
                  * DynamoDB stores order/customer state.
                  * GSI supports alternate lookup such as customerId + createdAt.
                  * DynamoDB Stream invokes another Lambda for notifications/search indexing.
                  * TTL removes temporary idempotency/session records where appropriate.
Why DynamoDB fits
The access patterns are known, traffic can spike, and the application benefits from serverless scaling without managing database instances.
	27. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Need to fetch a user by userId millions of times/day.
Use userId as a high-cardinality partition key; GetItem provides direct key lookup.
Scenario: Need all orders for one customer sorted by date.
Use customerId as partition key and createdAt/orderId as sort key, or design a GSI for that access pattern.
Scenario: Need to find orders by status but status is not part of base key.
Create a suitable GSI rather than scanning the full table.
Scenario: Writes are throttling on one celebrity user key.
You likely have a hot key; reconsider key distribution/write sharding/access pattern, not just total table capacity.
Scenario: A just-written value must be immediately read as latest from the base table.
Use a strongly consistent table read if the design requires it.
Scenario: Need strong read from a GSI.
Not supported; redesign to read the base table/LSI or change the access pattern.
Scenario: Need session records deleted after 24 hours.
Store an expiry attribute and enable TTL; remember deletion is asynchronous.
Scenario: Need to run logic whenever an item changes.
Enable DynamoDB Streams and consume with Lambda.
Scenario: Need atomic update of order + inventory reservation.
Use conditional writes or TransactWriteItems depending on whether one or multiple items must change atomically.
Scenario: Read-heavy product catalog needs extremely low latency.
Consider DAX if reads are repeated and eventual consistency is acceptable.
28. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Check the exact partition key and sort key used by the failing request.
2.  Check whether the code is using Query or accidentally performing Scan.
3.  Inspect ConsumedReadCapacityUnits / ConsumedWriteCapacityUnits and throttled-request metrics.
4.  Look for hot partition-key values and skewed access.
5.  Check GSI key design and whether the index is backfilling/active.
6.  Check IAM permissions for the table and index resources.
7.  Use exponential backoff/retries for throttling or unprocessed batch items.
8.  Check item size, transaction limits, condition-expression failures, and LSI item-collection limits.
9.  For Streams/Lambda, inspect event source mapping, iterator age, function failures, retries, and DLQ/destination configuration.
29. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is DynamoDB?
Answer: DynamoDB is AWS's fully managed, serverless NoSQL key-value and document database. It automatically manages storage, partitioning, availability, and scaling and is designed for predictable low-latency access at scale.
2. What is an item in DynamoDB?
Answer: An item is one record in a DynamoDB table, similar to a row. It is a collection of named attributes and can be up to 400 KB.
3. Is DynamoDB schema-less?
Answer: Non-key attributes are flexible, so different items can have different attributes. However, the primary key and secondary-index key attributes must follow their defined key schema and scalar key types.
4. What is a partition key?
Answer: It is the key whose value DynamoDB hashes to determine data placement. It is also used to target items for GetItem or Query access patterns.
5. What makes a good partition key?
Answer: High cardinality and evenly distributed access. Avoid keys where a small number of values receive most traffic, because they can create hot partitions.
6. What is a sort key?
Answer: A sort key is the second part of a composite primary key. Items with the same partition key can have different sort keys and can be queried in sort-key order or ranges.
7. Query vs Scan?
Answer: Query retrieves items for a specific partition-key value and is usually efficient. Scan reads every item or index entry before filtering and becomes expensive as data grows.
8. Does FilterExpression reduce the read cost of a Scan?
Answer: No. DynamoDB reads the items first and applies the filter afterward, so a filter reduces returned results but not the data initially read for capacity purposes.
9. GSI vs LSI?
Answer: A GSI can use a different partition key and optional sort key, can be added or removed later, scales separately, and supports eventual reads only. An LSI uses the same partition key as the table with a different sort key, must be created with the table, shares throughput, supports strong reads, and has a 10 GB per-partition-key item-collection limit.
10. Can you strongly consistently read from a GSI?
Answer: No. GSI reads are eventually consistent only.
11. What is eventual consistency?
Answer: A read may temporarily return an older committed value after a recent write. Repeating the read later returns the updated value.
12. What is a strongly consistent read?
Answer: A strong read returns the latest committed value reflecting prior successful writes. It is supported on tables and LSIs.
13. On-demand vs provisioned capacity?
Answer: On-demand automatically manages throughput and charges per request; it is the default/recommended option for many workloads. Provisioned mode configures RCUs/WCUs and is useful for steady predictable workloads and specific cost-control cases.
14. What is an RCU?
Answer: One RCU provides one strongly consistent read per second for an item up to 4 KB, or two eventually consistent reads per second for an item up to 4 KB.
15. What is a WCU?
Answer: One WCU provides one write per second for an item up to 1 KB.
16. What is a hot partition?
Answer: A partition receiving disproportionate traffic so its per-partition capacity is exhausted even if the table has unused capacity elsewhere. Better key distribution is the main solution.
17. What is adaptive capacity?
Answer: DynamoDB automatically gives more throughput to hotter partitions when possible, provided table and partition limits permit it. It helps uneven traffic but is not a replacement for good key design.
18. What is a conditional write?
Answer: A write that succeeds only if a condition expression evaluates to true, such as attribute_not_exists(id) or version = 4. It is useful for concurrency control and business invariants.
19. What is optimistic locking?
Answer: Store a version number and update only if the current version matches the one read. If another writer changed the item, the conditional update fails instead of silently overwriting it.
20. Does DynamoDB support transactions?
Answer: Yes. TransactWriteItems and TransactGetItems provide ACID transactions for up to 100 unique items and 4 MB total, within the same account and Region.
21. BatchWriteItem vs transaction?
Answer: BatchWriteItem is for efficient bulk Put/Delete and is not atomic as a whole. TransactWriteItems is all-or-nothing, supports conditions and updates, and consumes more capacity.
22. What is TTL?
Answer: Time to Live stores an expiry timestamp on each item. DynamoDB automatically removes expired items, usually within a few days. It is cleanup, not an exact-time scheduler.
23. What are DynamoDB Streams?
Answer: A 24-hour near-real-time change log of item INSERT, MODIFY, and REMOVE events. Streams are commonly connected to Lambda for downstream processing.
24. What is DAX?
Answer: DynamoDB Accelerator is a DynamoDB-specific in-memory cache that can deliver microsecond cache-hit reads. It is mainly useful for read-heavy workloads where eventual consistency is acceptable.
25. What are global tables?
Answer: Global tables replicate a DynamoDB table across multiple AWS Regions in a multi-active model, helping global latency and regional availability.
26. What is MREC vs MRSC?
Answer: MREC is the traditional multi-Region eventual-consistency mode with asynchronous replication. MRSC is the newer strong-consistency global-table mode that synchronously replicates writes to support strong reads across eligible replicas, with different latency and feature tradeoffs.
27. DynamoDB vs RDS?
Answer: Use DynamoDB for known key-based access patterns, serverless horizontal scale, and minimal operations. Use RDS when relational joins, ad-hoc SQL, relational constraints, and flexible querying are important.
28. Why does DynamoDB not support JOIN?
Answer: It is a distributed NoSQL database optimized for predictable key-based access. Relationships are usually modeled by denormalizing data, composite keys, and access-pattern-specific indexes.
29. What is single-table design?
Answer: It stores multiple entity types in one table and uses partition/sort key patterns to colocate related data so an access pattern can often be answered with one Query.
30. What is a sparse GSI?
Answer: A GSI that only includes items containing the index key attributes. This is useful for indexing rare states such as failed jobs without indexing every table item.
31. How large can one DynamoDB item be?
Answer: 400 KB including attribute names and values.
32. What is the LSI 10 GB limit?
Answer: For a table with LSIs, the total item collection for one partition-key value, including base-table items and LSI projections, cannot exceed 10 GB.
33. How many GSIs and LSIs can a table have by default?
Answer: Up to 20 GSIs by default and up to 5 LSIs.
34. How do you handle large objects?
Answer: Store the large object in S3 and store its object key, URL, status, and searchable metadata in DynamoDB.
35. How do you prevent duplicate order creation?
Answer: Use a conditional PutItem such as attribute_not_exists(orderId), or use an idempotency key item/transaction depending on the workflow.
30. Final DynamoDB Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	DynamoDB
	Serverless AWS NoSQL key-value/document database
	Item
	One record; max 400 KB
	Partition Key
	Hash/distribution key and query target
	Sort Key
	Orders/groups related items inside a partition key
	Query

	Scan
	Reads entire table/index; avoid for normal APIs
	GSI
	Alternate key; can be added later; eventual reads
	LSI
	Same PK, alternate SK; create with table; strong reads possible
	On-Demand
	Pay-per-request automatic capacity
	Provisioned
	Configure RCU/WCU; Auto Scaling optional

	WCU

	Condition Expression

	Streams

	DAX

	Global Tables

If you are short on time, revise these first
DynamoDB basics -> partition key + sort key -> access patterns -> Query vs Scan -> GSI vs LSI -> eventual vs strong consistency -> on-demand vs provisioned -> condition expressions -> transactions -> TTL -> Streams -> DynamoDB vs RDS -> interview Q&A.
	31. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * On-demand is currently the default and AWS-recommended throughput option for most DynamoDB workloads.
                  * Maximum item size remains 400 KB.
                  * GSI reads are eventually consistent only; tables and LSIs support optional strong reads.
                  * DynamoDB Streams retains change records for 24 hours.
                  * TTL deletion is asynchronous and typically happens within a few days after expiry.
                  * Global Tables now support both MREC and newer MRSC consistency modes; MRSC was added in 2025.
                  * TransactWriteItems / TransactGetItems support up to 100 unique items with an aggregate size up to 4 MB.
