AWS SNS - Interview Study Notes
AWS SNS - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand SNS from first principles: publishers, topics, subscriptions, fan-out, Standard vs FIFO, filtering, retries/DLQs, delivery protocols, security, and when to use SNS vs SQS/EventBridge. The interview section at the end includes concise answers.
	1. What is Amazon SNS?
Amazon SNS (Simple Notification Service) is a fully managed publish-subscribe messaging service. Publishers send a message to a topic, and SNS pushes copies of that message to the topic's subscribers.
                  * SNS is push-based: SNS delivers messages to subscribers rather than subscribers polling the topic.
                  * One publish can fan out to many independent subscribers.
                  * SNS supports application-to-application messaging and application-to-person notifications.
                  * Topics are logical communication channels; subscriptions connect endpoints to a topic.
  

The SNS mental model: publisher -> topic -> many subscribers.
2. Why Use SNS?
                  * Fan-out: one event can notify many systems.
                  * Loose coupling: publishers do not need to know how subscribers are implemented.
                  * Event-driven architecture: react to business events such as OrderCreated or PaymentSucceeded.
                  * Notifications: deliver to email, SMS, mobile push, HTTP/S endpoints, Lambda, SQS, and Firehose depending on topic type.
                  * Independent subscribers: failure in one subscriber does not have to block others.
Simple example
Order Service publishes OrderCreated once. SNS sends a copy to Billing, Email, and Analytics subscribers.
	3. Publisher, Topic, Subscription, Subscriber
Concept
	Meaning
	Publisher
	Application or AWS service that publishes messages.
	Topic
	Logical channel that receives published messages.
	Subscription
	Connection between a topic and an endpoint.
	Subscriber / Endpoint
	Destination that receives messages: SQS, Lambda, HTTP/S, email, SMS, mobile push, Firehose, etc.
	4. Standard vs FIFO Topics
  

Standard topics support the broadest endpoint types; FIFO topics are specialized for ordered application messaging.
Feature
	Standard Topic
	FIFO Topic
	Ordering
	Best effort
	Strict per MessageGroupId
	Delivery model
	At least once; duplicates possible
	Ordering/deduplication features for SQS delivery
	Subscribers
	All supported SNS protocols
	Amazon SQS standard/FIFO queues
	Topic suffix
	Normal name
	Must end with .fifo
	Filtering
	Yes
	Yes
	Archive/replay
	Use Firehose/custom archive patterns
	Built-in archive/replay up to 365 days
	Best fit
	General pub-sub and notifications
	Ordered/deduplicated event fan-out to SQS
	Interview shortcut
Choose Standard for most pub-sub and human notifications. Choose FIFO only when strict ordering/deduplication across SQS subscribers is a real requirement.
	5. Standard Topic Delivery Semantics
                  * Standard topics provide best-effort ordering.
                  * Subscribers should tolerate possible duplicate deliveries.
                  * Standard topics support the broadest range of protocols/endpoints.
                  * If a subscriber needs durable buffering and retry isolation, place an SQS queue between SNS and the application worker.
Design principle
Make event consumers idempotent when duplicate processing would cause incorrect business effects.
	6. FIFO Topics
                  * FIFO topic names end with .fifo.
                  * Messages use MessageGroupId for ordered groups.
                  * Messages in the same group preserve order; different groups can progress in parallel.
                  * MessageDeduplicationId or content-based deduplication helps prevent duplicate publishes.
                  * High-throughput FIFO mode can scope deduplication to MessageGroup for greater throughput.
  

FIFO ordering is maintained within each message group.
Important limitation
SNS FIFO topics do not directly deliver to email, SMS, mobile push, HTTP/S, Firehose, or Lambda. Subscribe an SQS queue, then consume from SQS.
	7. SNS + SQS Fan-Out
  

This is one of the most important AWS messaging patterns to know.
                  * Publisher publishes one event to SNS.
                  * SNS sends a copy to each subscribed SQS queue.
                  * Each downstream service processes its own queue independently.
                  * SQS adds durable buffering, consumer-controlled retries, visibility timeout, and queue backlog.
                  * A slow Analytics consumer does not block Billing or Email.
Why not subscribe all workers to one SQS queue?
One SQS queue distributes work among competing consumers. Multiple SQS queues behind SNS give each service its own copy of the event.
	8. Subscription Filter Policies
  

Filter policies let subscribers receive only relevant messages.
                  * Without a filter policy, a subscription receives every message published to the topic.
                  * Filter policies are JSON conditions configured on each subscription.
                  * FilterPolicyScope=MessageAttributes is the default.
                  * FilterPolicyScope=MessageBody can match fields inside a JSON message body.
                  * Filters reduce unnecessary downstream work and simplify publisher code.
Example
One OrderEvents topic can deliver payment events to Billing, shipment events to Shipping, while Analytics receives everything.
	9. Message Attributes
                  * Message attributes are metadata sent alongside the message body.
                  * They are commonly used for filtering, routing hints, content type, event type, tenant ID, or correlation ID.
                  * Keep core business facts in the event body; use attributes for routing/metadata when appropriate.
10. Raw Message Delivery
  

Raw delivery removes the standard SNS JSON envelope for supported endpoints.
                  * By default, SNS wraps the original message in an SNS JSON notification envelope containing metadata.
                  * Raw message delivery can send the message body directly to SQS, HTTP/S, and Firehose subscriptions.
                  * This is useful when consumers only need the payload and do not want to parse SNS metadata.
Trade-off
Raw delivery is simpler for consumers, but you lose the SNS envelope fields unless you carry needed metadata in the message itself.
	11. Delivery Retries
SNS uses protocol-specific delivery policies when a subscriber cannot receive a message.
                  * AWS-managed endpoints such as SQS and Lambda have AWS-defined retry behavior.
                  * HTTP/S subscriptions support configurable delivery policies.
                  * Retries use backoff to avoid repeatedly hammering an unavailable endpoint.
                  * If retry attempts are exhausted and a DLQ is configured, SNS sends the undeliverable message to the DLQ.
12. Dead-Letter Queues (DLQ)
  

SNS DLQs capture delivery failures after retry policy is exhausted.
                  * The DLQ must be an Amazon SQS queue.
                  * The DLQ is configured on the subscription, not on the topic.
                  * Each subscriber can have its own DLQ because each endpoint can fail independently.
                  * Monitor DLQ message count with CloudWatch alarms.
                  * Investigate and redrive/reprocess after fixing the cause.
SNS DLQ vs SQS DLQ
SNS DLQ captures SNS-to-subscriber delivery failures. SQS DLQ captures messages that a consumer repeatedly receives but fails to process.
	13. Supported Subscription Protocols
Protocol
	Endpoint
	Typical Use
	SQS
	Amazon SQS queue
	Durable fan-out to workers
	Lambda
	AWS Lambda function
	Event-driven serverless processing
	HTTP / HTTPS
	Web endpoint
	Webhook / service integration
	Email / Email-JSON
	Email address
	Human notifications
	SMS
	Phone number
	Text messages
	Application
	Mobile push endpoint
	Push notifications
	Firehose
	Amazon Data Firehose stream
	Archive/analytics to S3, Redshift, OpenSearch, etc.
	Confirmation
HTTP/S, email, and some cross-account subscriptions require confirmation before messages are delivered.
	14. SNS + Lambda
                  * A Standard SNS topic can directly invoke subscribed Lambda functions.
                  * SNS pushes an event to Lambda; Lambda does not poll SNS.
                  * Useful for short event-driven processing that does not need queue buffering.
                  * If you need backlog, visibility timeout, consumer pacing, or stronger retry isolation, put SQS between SNS and Lambda.
SNS -> Lambda vs SNS -> SQS -> Lambda
Direct SNS->Lambda is simple and low-latency. SNS->SQS->Lambda is more resilient when you need buffering, backlog, and controlled retries.
	15. SNS + HTTP/HTTPS
                  * SNS sends HTTP POST requests to confirmed endpoints.
                  * Endpoints must handle subscription confirmation messages and notification messages.
                  * Use HTTPS rather than HTTP for secure transport.
                  * Configure delivery policy/retries carefully for customer-managed endpoints.
                  * Make webhook handlers idempotent because retries can occur.
16. Application-to-Person Notifications
                  * SNS can deliver SMS text messages.
                  * SNS can send email notifications through email subscriptions.
                  * SNS mobile push can deliver notifications through supported push-notification services.
                  * These are notification channels; they are not replacements for a durable worker queue.
Interview distinction
Application-to-application messaging connects software systems. Application-to-person messaging notifies humans/devices.
	17. Message Size
                  * Historically SNS messages were limited to 256 KiB; current SNS topics can configure MaximumMessageSize up to 1 MiB.
                  * The default MaximumMessageSize remains 256 KiB.
                  * If MaximumMessageSize is set above 256 KiB, supported endpoints are restricted to SQS, Lambda, and Firehose, and the topic supports up to 100 subscriptions.
                  * For truly large payloads, store the payload in S3 and publish an object key/reference.
Interview-safe answer
For normal interview discussion, avoid sending large files through SNS. Store the object in S3 and publish metadata/reference.
	18. Topic Policies and IAM
                  * IAM policies control which principals can call actions such as sns:Publish or sns:Subscribe.
                  * SNS topic policies are resource-based policies and are common for cross-account publishing/subscribing.
                  * Use least privilege: limit actions to the specific topic ARN.
                  * For SNS -> SQS, the SQS queue policy must allow the SNS topic/service to send messages to the queue.
Common mistake
Creating the subscription is not enough if the destination SQS queue policy does not allow SNS to call sqs:SendMessage.
	19. Encryption and Network Security
                  * SNS supports server-side encryption with AWS KMS.
                  * SNS SSE encrypts message bodies at rest while stored in the topic service.
                  * Use HTTPS/TLS for transport security.
                  * AWS recommends HTTPS, and encrypted SNS topics require HTTPS and SigV4 for publishing.
                  * VPC interface endpoints (AWS PrivateLink) can let workloads call SNS APIs without using the public internet path.
20. Cross-Account Messaging
                  * An SNS topic can publish to resources or principals in another AWS account with the correct resource/IAM policies.
                  * For cross-account SNS -> SQS, configure the topic subscription and the destination queue policy correctly.
                  * External subscriptions may require confirmation depending on the endpoint type/account relationship.
Security principle
Cross-account access requires permission on both sides: the caller/source and the receiving resource policy where applicable.
	21. Monitoring and CloudWatch
                  * Monitor messages published and delivered.
                  * Monitor delivery failures by protocol.
                  * Enable delivery status logging for supported endpoints where useful.
                  * Alarm on subscription DLQs.
                  * For SMS, monitor delivery status and spend/usage controls as appropriate.
Debugging pattern
Start with SNS delivery metrics/logs, then inspect the subscriber (SQS queue, Lambda logs, HTTP endpoint, etc.).
	22. FIFO Topic Archive and Replay (Advanced)
  

Current SNS FIFO topics can archive messages in SNS and replay a selected time window to subscribers.
                  * FIFO topic owners can enable ArchivePolicy.
                  * Messages can be archived for up to 365 days.
                  * Subscribers can configure replay to redeliver archived messages for a chosen time range.
                  * Useful for recovery after an outage, rebuilding downstream state, or reprocessing corrected data.
Do not confuse with Standard topics
Standard topics do not have the same built-in in-place replay feature; Firehose can be used to archive Standard topic events externally.
	23. SNS vs SQS
Area
	SNS
	SQS
	Pattern
	Publish-subscribe / fan-out
	Queue / competing consumers
	Delivery
	Pushes to subscribers
	Consumers pull; Lambda service can poll
	One message to many services
	Yes
	Not from one queue - consumers compete
	Backlog for worker processing
	Not a worker queue
	Yes
	Ordering option
	FIFO topics to SQS
	FIFO queues
	Best use
	Broadcast event/notification
	Buffer durable work
	Easy memory trick
SNS = one-to-many notification/fan-out. SQS = one queue of work processed by consumers.
	24. SNS vs EventBridge
Area
	SNS
	EventBridge
	Core model
	Topic + subscriptions
	Event bus + rules + targets
	Routing
	Topic fan-out + subscription filters
	Rich event-pattern routing across many sources
	Human notifications
	Email/SMS/mobile supported
	Normally routes to services; not primarily A2P notification
	FIFO ordering
	FIFO topics available
	Not a FIFO message queue
	Best use
	Simple pub-sub/fan-out and notifications
	Event integration bus, many producers/sources, richer routing
	Interview answer
Use SNS when topic-based fan-out is enough. Use EventBridge when you need a broader event bus with richer routing rules and integrations. They can also be used together.
	25. SNS vs Direct SQS
                  * Direct SQS is enough when one logical worker group should process each message once.
                  * SNS + multiple SQS queues is better when several services each need their own copy.
                  * SNS does not replace SQS buffering; SNS and SQS complement each other.
26. Common Architecture Example
E-commerce order event:
1.  Order service publishes OrderCreated to SNS.
2.  Billing queue receives a copy.
3.  Email queue receives a copy.
4.  Analytics queue receives a copy.
5.  Each service processes independently.
6.  Filter policies can keep irrelevant event types away from a subscriber.
7.  If SNS cannot deliver to a subscription, that subscription can use a DLQ.
Why this scales well
The Order service does not call three downstream systems synchronously. New subscribers can be added without changing the publisher.
	27. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: One order event must reach billing, analytics, and email.
Publish once to SNS and subscribe three separate SQS queues or other endpoints.
Scenario: Only the shipping service should receive shipment events.
Use a subscription filter policy based on message attributes or JSON body fields.
Scenario: Payment events must remain ordered for each customer.
Use an SNS FIFO topic and SQS FIFO queues, with customer ID as MessageGroupId if that matches the business ordering boundary.
Scenario: Need chat/live two-way communication.
SNS is not a WebSocket service; use API Gateway WebSocket or another real-time channel.
Scenario: Subscriber HTTP endpoint is temporarily down.
SNS retries according to delivery policy; configure a subscription DLQ to preserve undeliverable messages.
Scenario: Lambda consumer cannot tolerate sudden spikes.
Put SQS between SNS and Lambda to buffer and pace consumption.
Scenario: Need to notify a user by text message.
Use SNS SMS/application-to-person messaging, subject to regional/account messaging configuration.
Scenario: Need to replay a week of ordered events.
For FIFO topics, enable archive/replay and replay the desired time range; otherwise use an external archive design.
Scenario: Message payload is several megabytes.
Store the payload in S3 and publish the S3 reference/metadata through SNS.
28. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Confirm the message was successfully published to the expected topic ARN.
2.  Check that the subscription is confirmed and not PendingConfirmation.
3.  Check the subscription filter policy - it may be filtering the message out.
4.  Check IAM/topic policy permissions.
5.  For SQS, verify the queue policy allows SNS to send messages.
6.  For Lambda, check the Lambda resource policy and CloudWatch logs.
7.  For HTTP/S, verify endpoint reachability, HTTPS certificate, and response status.
8.  Check SNS delivery status metrics/logs and subscription DLQ.
9.  For FIFO, verify MessageGroupId/DeduplicationId and compatible SQS subscriber configuration.
29. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is Amazon SNS?
Answer: Amazon SNS is a fully managed publish-subscribe messaging service. Publishers send messages to a topic, and SNS pushes copies to all matching subscriptions.
2. What is a topic?
Answer: A topic is a logical communication channel in SNS. Publishers publish to the topic; subscribers subscribe endpoints to receive messages.
3. Is SNS push-based or pull-based?
Answer: SNS is push-based. SNS pushes messages to subscriptions such as SQS, Lambda, HTTP/S, email, SMS, mobile push, and Firehose.
4. What is fan-out?
Answer: Fan-out means publishing one event once and delivering independent copies to multiple subscribers.
5. SNS vs SQS?
Answer: SNS is one-to-many publish-subscribe. SQS is a durable work queue where consumers process messages from a backlog. SNS and SQS are often used together.
6. Why use SNS + SQS together?
Answer: SNS gives each downstream service a copy of the event, while SQS gives each service its own durable buffer, retries, visibility timeout, and independent scaling.
7. What is the difference between a Standard and FIFO SNS topic?
Answer: Standard topics support all SNS endpoint protocols and best-effort ordering. FIFO topics preserve strict ordering per MessageGroupId and provide deduplication features, but delivery is for SQS subscribers.
8. Can an SNS FIFO topic invoke Lambda directly?
Answer: No. For FIFO fan-out to Lambda, subscribe an SQS standard or FIFO queue to the FIFO topic and configure Lambda to consume that queue.
9. What is MessageGroupId?
Answer: For FIFO topics, it defines an ordered message group. Messages in the same group remain ordered while different groups can be processed in parallel.
10. What is MessageDeduplicationId?
Answer: It identifies duplicate publishes for FIFO deduplication. Content-based deduplication can generate the ID from the message body.
11. What is an SNS subscription filter policy?
Answer: A JSON policy on a subscription that controls which published messages that subscriber receives.
12. What can a filter policy inspect?
Answer: It can match message attributes, or with FilterPolicyScope=MessageBody, fields in a JSON message body.
13. What happens without a filter policy?
Answer: The subscription receives every message published to the topic.
14. What is raw message delivery?
Answer: For supported endpoints such as SQS/HTTP/S/Firehose, SNS can deliver the original message body without wrapping it in the SNS JSON envelope.
15. What is an SNS DLQ?
Answer: An Amazon SQS queue attached to an SNS subscription. It stores messages SNS could not deliver to that subscriber after retries.
16. Is an SNS DLQ configured on the topic or subscription?
Answer: On the subscription, because delivery failures happen independently for each subscriber.
17. SNS DLQ vs SQS DLQ?
Answer: SNS DLQ captures SNS delivery failures. SQS DLQ captures messages a queue consumer repeatedly fails to process.
18. How does SNS retry failed deliveries?
Answer: SNS uses delivery policies that vary by protocol. AWS-managed endpoints use AWS-defined retry behavior; HTTP/S delivery policies can be customized.
19. Can SNS deliver email and SMS?
Answer: Yes, Standard SNS topics support application-to-person email, SMS, and mobile push notification endpoints.
20. Does email/SMS make SNS a durable work queue?
Answer: No. Human notification channels are for notification delivery, not for worker backlog/processing semantics like SQS.
21. What is the current SNS message-size limit?
Answer: Topics default to 256 KiB maximum message size and can now be configured up to 1 MiB. Larger-than-256-KiB configuration restricts supported endpoints, so large payloads are still commonly stored in S3 with a reference sent through SNS.
22. How do you secure an SNS topic?
Answer: Use least-privilege IAM/topic policies, HTTPS, KMS server-side encryption when needed, and VPC endpoints for private API access where appropriate.
23. How does SNS publish to an SQS queue securely?
Answer: The subscription connects SNS to the queue, and the SQS queue resource policy must allow the SNS topic/service to call sqs:SendMessage.
24. Can SNS work across AWS accounts?
Answer: Yes. Cross-account publishing/subscription requires the correct IAM and resource-based policies, and some subscriptions require confirmation.
25. What is the benefit of filter policies?
Answer: They offload event routing from publishers and consumers, so each subscription receives only relevant events.
26. SNS vs EventBridge?
Answer: SNS is simpler topic-based pub-sub and supports human notification channels. EventBridge is a broader event bus with richer event-pattern routing and many service/SaaS integrations.
27. When should I use SNS instead of direct SQS?
Answer: Use SNS when multiple independent subscribers each need their own copy. Use direct SQS when one logical worker pool should process each job.
28. What is FIFO archive and replay?
Answer: SNS FIFO topics can archive messages for up to 365 days and replay a selected time range to subscribers for recovery or reprocessing.
29. Can Standard topics replay old messages?
Answer: Not with the same built-in FIFO archive/replay feature. A common approach is to archive Standard-topic messages externally, for example through Firehose to S3.
30. How would you troubleshoot a subscriber not receiving messages?
Answer: Verify publish success, subscription confirmation, filter policies, IAM/topic/endpoint permissions, delivery status logs/metrics, and the subscription DLQ.
31. What is a common SNS microservices pattern?
Answer: Publish business events to SNS, subscribe one SQS queue per downstream service, and let each service process independently.
32. Why should HTTP SNS subscribers be idempotent?
Answer: SNS retries failed deliveries, so an endpoint can receive the same notification again. Idempotency avoids duplicate business effects.
30. Final SNS Interview Cheat Sheet

	Subscription

	Fan-out

	31. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * Standard topics support the broadest endpoint types; FIFO topics deliver to SQS standard or FIFO queues.
                  * FIFO topics support built-in message archive/replay for up to 365 days.
                  * Filter policies can evaluate either message attributes or JSON message body fields.
                  * Raw message delivery is available for SQS, HTTP/S, and Firehose subscriptions.
                  * SNS topic MaximumMessageSize can be configured above the historical 256 KiB default up to 1 MiB, with endpoint/subscription restrictions.
                  * High-throughput FIFO topics can scope deduplication to MessageGroup for higher aggregate throughput.

AWS SQS - Interview Study Notes
AWS SQS - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand SQS from first principles: why queues are used, producer/consumer flow, Standard vs FIFO, message lifecycle, visibility timeout, polling, DLQs, retries, Lambda integration, scaling, security, and the interview scenarios you are most likely to see.
	1. What is Amazon SQS?
Amazon SQS (Simple Queue Service) is a managed message queue. Producers send messages to a queue, and consumers receive and process those messages later.
                  * SQS helps decouple services so they do not have to be available or equally fast at the same time.
                  * It buffers bursts of work and lets consumers process messages at their own speed.
                  * SQS stores messages durably across multiple Availability Zones.
                  * It is pull-based for normal consumers: consumers call ReceiveMessage. With Lambda, the Lambda service polls SQS for you.
  

Basic SQS pattern: producer -> queue -> consumer.
2. Why Use a Queue?
                  * Decoupling: producer does not need to know the consumer implementation.
                  * Load leveling: sudden spikes can wait in the queue instead of overwhelming workers.
                  * Reliability: work is not lost just because a consumer is temporarily unavailable.
                  * Asynchronous processing: user-facing request can finish before background work completes.
                  * Independent scaling: add more consumers when backlog grows.
                  * Retry handling: failed work can be retried and eventually moved to a DLQ.
Simple example
Checkout API stores the order, sends an 'OrderCreated' message to SQS, and immediately responds. Background workers later send email, update inventory, or process downstream tasks.
	3. Producer, Queue, Consumer
Part
	Responsibility
	Examples
	Producer
	Creates and sends messages
	API/Lambda, EC2 app, ECS service
	Queue
	Stores messages until they are processed
	SQS Standard or FIFO
	Consumer
	Receives, processes, and deletes messages
	Lambda, ECS workers, EC2 workers
	4. SQS Message Lifecycle
                  1. Producer sends a message with SendMessage.
                  2. SQS stores the message durably.
                  3. Consumer calls ReceiveMessage and receives the message plus a receipt handle.
                  4. The message becomes invisible to other consumers for the visibility timeout.
                  5. Consumer performs the work.
                  6. If successful, consumer calls DeleteMessage using the receipt handle.
                  7. If it is not deleted before visibility expires, the message becomes visible again and can be retried.
Very important
Receiving a message does NOT remove it. DeleteMessage removes it after successful processing.
	5. Standard vs FIFO Queues
  

Standard and FIFO solve different requirements.
Feature
	Standard
	FIFO
	Delivery
	At least once
	FIFO delivery / exactly-once processing features
	Ordering
	Best effort
	Strict within each MessageGroupId
	Duplicates
	Possible
	SQS does not introduce duplicates; send deduplication supported
	Throughput
	Very high / nearly unlimited API rate
	High, with FIFO-specific throughput model
	MessageGroupId
	Not required for normal Standard use
	Required
	Queue name
	Normal name
	Must end with .fifo
	Best fit
	Most asynchronous work
	Payments/order state/workflows where order matters
	Interview rule
Use Standard unless the business requirement requires strict ordering or FIFO deduplication semantics.
	6. Standard Queue Delivery Semantics
                  * Standard queues provide at-least-once delivery.
                  * A message can occasionally be delivered more than once.
                  * Messages may occasionally be received out of order.
                  * Consumers should therefore be idempotent.
Idempotent consumer
Processing the same message twice should not create an incorrect result. Example: store a processed event ID, use database uniqueness, or make the operation naturally repeat-safe.
	7. FIFO Queues
                  * FIFO means First-In-First-Out.
                  * Ordering is guaranteed within a MessageGroupId.
                  * Every FIFO message requires a MessageGroupId.
                  * Messages from different groups can be processed in parallel.
                  * MessageDeduplicationId or content-based deduplication helps prevent duplicate sends.
                  * SQS remembers deduplication IDs for a 5-minute deduplication interval.
  

Use granular message groups to preserve business ordering while allowing parallelism.
Example
Use customer ID or order ID as MessageGroupId when messages for the same entity must stay ordered, while different customers/orders can process concurrently.
	8. Exactly-Once - Important Nuance
AWS describes FIFO queues as supporting exactly-once processing and preventing duplicates from being introduced into the queue.
                  * Producer retries with the same deduplication ID inside the deduplication window do not create another queued message.
                  * However, an application consumer can still see/process a message again if processing fails, the visibility timeout expires, or deletion does not complete.
                  * Therefore, idempotent consumers are still a good design practice even with FIFO.
Best interview phrasing
FIFO gives ordered delivery and deduplication/exactly-once queue semantics, but I would still make the consumer idempotent because application-level retries can happen.
	9. Visibility Timeout
  

Visibility timeout protects a message from being processed by multiple consumers at the same time.
                  * Default queue visibility timeout: 30 seconds.
                  * Allowed range: 0 seconds to 12 hours.
                  * Choose a timeout longer than the normal processing time.
                  * If processing can vary, use ChangeMessageVisibility to extend the timeout while work continues.
                  * If timeout is too short, another consumer may receive the same message while the first consumer is still working.
                  * If timeout is excessively long, failed messages take longer to become available for retry.
10. Receipt Handle
When a consumer receives a message, SQS returns a receipt handle. The consumer uses that receipt handle to delete or change the visibility of that specific received copy.
                  * Receipt handle is different from MessageId.
                  * If the same message is received again, it gets a new receipt handle.
                  * Use the most recent receipt handle when deleting the message.
11. Short Polling vs Long Polling
  

Long polling waits for messages and usually reduces unnecessary ReceiveMessage calls.
                  * Short polling returns immediately, even when no message is found.
                  * Long polling waits until a message is available or the wait time expires.
                  * Maximum long-poll wait: 20 seconds.
                  * Long polling reduces empty responses and can lower cost.
Interview recommendation
For custom consumers, I would normally use long polling instead of continuously short-polling an empty queue.
	12. Message Retention
                  * SQS keeps a message until it is deleted or its retention period expires.
                  * Default retention: 4 days.
                  * Minimum: 1 minute.
                  * Maximum: 14 days.
                  * Retention is not the same as visibility timeout.
Concept
	Meaning
	Retention period
	How long SQS may keep an undeleted message in the queue.
	Visibility timeout
	How long a received message is temporarily hidden from other consumers.
	13. Message Size
                  * Current SQS maximum message size: 1 MiB.
                  * Queue maximum message size is configurable from 1 KiB to 1 MiB.
                  * For larger payloads, store the data in S3 and put a reference/key in SQS.
                  * AWS provides SQS Extended Client libraries for supported languages that can use S3 for larger payloads.
Interview pattern
Do not place a 50 MB video in SQS. Store it in S3 and send metadata/object key through SQS.
	14. Delay Queues and Message Timers
                  * Delay queue hides newly sent messages for a configured delay before they become available.
                  * Maximum delay is 15 minutes.
                  * Standard queues support per-message timers that can override the queue delay.
                  * FIFO queues do not support per-message timers; use the queue-level delay.
                  * For advanced scheduling beyond 15 minutes, EventBridge Scheduler is usually a better fit.
Delay vs visibility
Delay happens before first delivery. Visibility timeout happens after a consumer receives the message.
	15. Dead-Letter Queues (DLQ)
  

DLQs isolate messages that repeatedly fail processing.
                  * Configure a redrive policy on the source queue.
                  * maxReceiveCount controls how many receives are allowed before SQS moves the message to the DLQ.
                  * Monitor DLQ depth with CloudWatch alarms.
                  * Investigate the failed message and application error.
                  * After fixing the problem, redrive/reprocess messages when safe.
Poison message
A message that always causes processing to fail. A DLQ prevents it from retrying forever and consuming worker capacity.
	16. DLQ Best Practices
                  * Use the same queue type for source and DLQ where ordering semantics matter.
                  * Give the DLQ a retention period long enough for investigation.
                  * Alarm when messages appear in the DLQ.
                  * Do not simply redrive endlessly without fixing the root cause.
                  * Include message IDs/correlation IDs and useful metadata to help debugging.
17. SQS with AWS Lambda
  

Lambda polls SQS using an event source mapping; your function does not poll SQS directly.
                  * Lambda event source mapping polls the queue and invokes the function with a batch.
                  * If the invocation succeeds, Lambda deletes successfully processed messages according to the integration behavior.
                  * By default, if one record fails and the batch fails, the whole batch can become visible again after the visibility timeout.
                  * Partial batch responses let the function report only failed records so successful records are not retried.
                  * The Lambda function timeout must be less than or equal to the SQS visibility timeout.
                  * Use a DLQ/redrive policy for messages that repeatedly fail.
Important Lambda pattern
For batch processing, make the function idempotent and enable partial batch responses when appropriate.
	18. SQS with ECS / EC2 Consumers
                  * ECS/EC2 workers normally implement their own ReceiveMessage polling loop.
                  * Use long polling.
                  * Process messages concurrently based on available CPU, memory, and downstream capacity.
                  * Delete only after successful processing.
                  * Extend visibility for long-running tasks.
                  * Scale worker count using queue backlog metrics.
Back-pressure design
Do not blindly add consumers if the database or downstream API cannot handle the extra load. Queue depth is one signal, not the only scaling constraint.
	19. Scaling Consumers
                  * ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible - backlog ready to process.
                  * ApproximateNumberOfMessagesNotVisible - in-flight messages.
                  * ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage - how long the oldest message has been waiting.
                  * NumberOfMessagesSent / Received / Deleted.
Good scaling signal
Backlog per consumer = visible messages / active consumers. Target-tracking this can scale ECS/EC2 workers more smoothly than raw queue depth alone.
	20. Batch Operations
                  * SQS supports batch APIs for sending, receiving, deleting, and changing visibility of multiple messages.
                  * Batching reduces API calls and improves throughput/cost efficiency.
                  * ReceiveMessage can return multiple messages in one request.
                  * Handle partial batch failures correctly; one operation in a batch can fail while others succeed.
21. SQS Security
                  * Use IAM identity policies to control which principals can SendMessage, ReceiveMessage, DeleteMessage, etc.
                  * SQS queue policies are resource-based policies useful for cross-account or service-to-queue access.
                  * Use least privilege.
                  * Use HTTPS/TLS for data in transit.
                  * SQS supports server-side encryption, including SQS-managed keys and AWS KMS-managed keys.
                  * Avoid putting secrets unnecessarily inside message bodies.
Example least privilege
Producer role: sqs:SendMessage only. Consumer role: sqs:ReceiveMessage, sqs:DeleteMessage, and sqs:ChangeMessageVisibility only on the target queue.
	22. SQS vs SNS
Area
	SQS
	SNS
	Pattern
	Queue / point-to-point work distribution
	Publish-subscribe fan-out
	Consumer model
	Consumers poll the queue
	SNS pushes to subscribers
	Persistence
	Messages wait until consumed/expired
	Delivery to subscribers; not a worker queue
	One message to many independent consumers
	Not by itself
	Yes
	Best use
	Background jobs/work queue
	Broadcast event to multiple subscribers
	23. SNS + SQS Fan-Out Pattern
  

Each subscriber gets its own queue, so each service processes its own independent copy.
                  * Publisher sends one event to SNS.
                  * SNS publishes copies to several SQS queues.
                  * Each service consumes at its own speed.
                  * Failure or backlog in analytics does not block email/order processing.
                  * Useful for event-driven microservices.
24. SQS vs EventBridge
Area
	SQS
	EventBridge
	Main purpose
	Durable work queue / buffering
	Event routing and integration bus
	Consumption
	Workers pull/process queue messages
	Rules route events to targets
	Backlog
	Yes, queue holds work
	Not a worker backlog in the same sense
	Routing
	Minimal queue-level filtering patterns
	Rich event-pattern routing
	Ordering
	FIFO option
	Not generally a FIFO work queue
	Typical use
	Background tasks, asynchronous jobs
	Business events, integrations, schedules/rules
	Common architecture
EventBridge/SNS routes an event to the right consumers; SQS buffers durable work in front of each consumer.
	25. SQS vs Kafka/Kinesis - Basic Interview View
Area
	SQS
	Streaming Log (Kafka/Kinesis style)
	Mental model
	Work queue
	Ordered append-only stream/log
	Message ownership
	A worker processes/deletes work
	Multiple consumers can independently read stream
	Replay
	Limited by queue lifecycle; delete removes message
	Designed for retention/replay
	Consumer position
	Queue visibility/deletion
	Offsets/checkpoints
	Use
	Async jobs, buffering
	Streaming analytics/event history
	26. Common SQS Architecture Example
Image processing example:
1.  User uploads image to S3.
2.  Application/event sends image key to an SQS queue.
3.  Image-processing workers consume messages.
4.  Worker creates thumbnail and stores output in S3.
5.  Worker deletes the message after successful processing.
6.  If processing repeatedly fails, SQS moves the message to a DLQ.
Why this is better than synchronous processing
Upload request does not wait for CPU-heavy image processing. Workers can scale independently and SQS absorbs spikes.
	27. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Order events must be processed in order for each order.
Use FIFO with order ID as MessageGroupId so each order stays ordered while different orders can process in parallel.
Scenario: Email jobs may be processed twice occasionally and order does not matter.
Use Standard queue and make the email workflow idempotent/deduplicated where necessary.
Scenario: Consumer needs 2 minutes but visibility is 30 seconds.
Increase visibility timeout or extend it while processing; otherwise the message can be delivered to another consumer.
Scenario: A message keeps crashing every worker.
Configure a DLQ with an appropriate maxReceiveCount and investigate the poison message.
Scenario: Queue is often empty and workers waste calls.
Use long polling, usually up to 20 seconds where appropriate.
Scenario: Traffic spikes from 100 to 50,000 jobs.
Let SQS buffer the backlog and scale consumers based on queue/backlog/age while respecting downstream capacity.
Scenario: Need one event to trigger billing, email, and analytics independently.
Use SNS/EventBridge fan-out to separate SQS queues for each consumer.
Scenario: Payload is 20 MB.
Store payload in S3 and send the S3 key/reference in SQS.
Scenario: Lambda retries all 10 messages because one failed.
Enable partial batch responses and return only failed message identifiers.
28. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Check ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible - is there a backlog?
2.  Check ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage - are messages waiting too long?
3.  Check consumer errors/logs.
4.  Check IAM permissions for SendMessage/ReceiveMessage/DeleteMessage.
5.  Check visibility timeout relative to processing time.
6.  Check DLQ and redrive policy.
7.  Check whether consumers actually delete messages after success.
8.  For FIFO, check MessageGroupId hot spots and deduplication IDs.
9.  For Lambda, check event source mapping, function errors, concurrency, partial batch behavior, and timeout settings.
29. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is Amazon SQS?
Answer: Amazon SQS is a fully managed message queue service. Producers send messages to a queue and consumers process them asynchronously. It is commonly used to decouple services, buffer traffic spikes, and build reliable background processing.
2. Why use SQS instead of calling another service directly?
Answer: SQS reduces coupling. The producer can finish without waiting for the consumer, and the queue buffers work if the consumer is slow or temporarily unavailable.
3. What is the difference between a producer and a consumer?
Answer: A producer sends messages to the queue. A consumer receives messages, performs the work, and deletes the message after successful processing.
4. Does ReceiveMessage remove a message?
Answer: No. It makes the message temporarily invisible. The consumer must call DeleteMessage after successful processing.
5. What is visibility timeout?
Answer: It is the period after a consumer receives a message during which that message is hidden from other consumers. If it is not deleted before the timeout expires, it becomes visible again.
6. What is the default visibility timeout?
Answer: 30 seconds. It can be configured from 0 seconds up to 12 hours.
7. What happens if visibility timeout is too short?
Answer: The message can become visible while the first consumer is still processing it, so another consumer may process the same message concurrently.
8. Standard queue vs FIFO queue?
Answer: Standard queues provide very high throughput, at-least-once delivery, and best-effort ordering. FIFO queues preserve strict order within a MessageGroupId and provide deduplication/exactly-once processing features.
9. Can Standard SQS deliver duplicates?
Answer: Yes. Standard queues use at-least-once delivery, so consumers must tolerate duplicate messages.
10. Does FIFO mean the consumer never needs idempotency?
Answer: No. FIFO prevents duplicates being introduced by SQS and provides send deduplication, but application failures or visibility expiration can still lead to reprocessing. Idempotent consumers are still recommended.
11. What is MessageGroupId?
Answer: In FIFO queues, MessageGroupId identifies an ordered group. Messages in the same group are processed one at a time in strict order, while different groups can be processed in parallel.
12. What is MessageDeduplicationId?
Answer: It is a FIFO identifier used to recognize duplicate sends. Re-sending the same deduplication ID within the 5-minute deduplication interval does not enqueue another duplicate message.
13. What is content-based deduplication?
Answer: For a FIFO queue, SQS can generate the deduplication ID from a SHA-256 hash of the message body instead of requiring the producer to supply one.
14. What is long polling?
Answer: Long polling lets ReceiveMessage wait for a message to arrive instead of returning immediately. It reduces empty responses and unnecessary polling calls.
15. What is the maximum long-poll wait time?
Answer: 20 seconds.
16. What is a dead-letter queue?
Answer: A DLQ is a separate queue where SQS moves messages that have been received unsuccessfully too many times, based on the source queue redrive policy.
17. What is maxReceiveCount?
Answer: It is the number of receives allowed before a source queue moves a repeatedly failing message to its configured DLQ.
18. What is a poison message?
Answer: A message that repeatedly causes consumer processing to fail. DLQs are commonly used to isolate poison messages.
19. What is the SQS message retention period?
Answer: By default 4 days. It can be configured from 1 minute to 14 days.
20. What is the maximum SQS message size?
Answer: 1 MiB. For larger payloads, store the data in S3 and send a reference through SQS.
21. Delay queue vs visibility timeout?
Answer: Delay hides a new message before its first delivery. Visibility timeout hides a message after a consumer has received it.
22. What is the maximum SQS delay?
Answer: 15 minutes. For longer or richer scheduling, EventBridge Scheduler is normally a better choice.
23. How does Lambda consume SQS messages?
Answer: A Lambda event source mapping polls SQS, batches messages, and invokes the Lambda function. The function itself does not need to implement ReceiveMessage polling.
24. What happens when one SQS message in a Lambda batch fails?
Answer: By default the batch can be retried. With partial batch responses, the function can report only failed message IDs so successfully processed messages do not need to be retried.
25. How should Lambda visibility timeout relate to function timeout?
Answer: The queue visibility timeout must be at least as long as the function timeout, and in practice should allow enough time for processing and retries.
26. SQS vs SNS?
Answer: SQS is a durable work queue consumed by workers. SNS is publish-subscribe fan-out that pushes a published message to multiple subscribers.
27. Why combine SNS and SQS?
Answer: SNS can fan out one event to multiple SQS queues. Each downstream service gets its own durable queue and processes independently.
28. SQS vs EventBridge?
Answer: SQS is best for buffering durable work for consumers. EventBridge is an event-routing bus with rich rules and many integrations. They are often used together.
29. How do you scale SQS consumers?
Answer: Monitor queue depth and age of oldest message, then scale consumers while respecting downstream capacity. For ECS/EC2, backlog per consumer is a useful signal.
30. How do you prevent duplicate business effects?
Answer: Design the consumer to be idempotent using techniques such as unique event IDs, database uniqueness constraints, conditional updates, or a processed-message store.
31. What IAM permissions does a basic SQS consumer need?
Answer: Typically sqs:ReceiveMessage, sqs:DeleteMessage, and sqs:ChangeMessageVisibility on the target queue. A producer normally needs sqs:SendMessage.
32. What is a receipt handle?
Answer: A temporary identifier returned when a message is received. It is used to delete or change visibility for that received copy and changes if the message is received again.
33. What metrics would you monitor?
Answer: ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible, ApproximateNumberOfMessagesNotVisible, ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage, and message sent/received/deleted counts.
34. Why might FIFO processing be slow?
Answer: A hot MessageGroupId can serialize work because only one message from that group is processed at a time. Use multiple meaningful groups when business ordering allows it.
35. When should I choose Standard over FIFO?
Answer: Choose Standard for most high-throughput asynchronous work when strict ordering is not required. Choose FIFO only when ordering/deduplication requirements justify it.
30. Final SQS Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	SQS
	Managed message queue
	Producer
	Sends messages
	Consumer
	Receives/processes/deletes messages
	Standard Queue
	At-least-once, best-effort ordering, very high throughput
	FIFO Queue
	Strict order per group + deduplication features
	MessageGroupId
	Ordering/parallelism boundary in FIFO
	Deduplication ID
	Prevents duplicate sends within FIFO dedup window
	Visibility Timeout
	Temporary hide after ReceiveMessage
	Receipt Handle
	Token used to delete/change visibility
	Long Polling
	Wait for messages; max 20 seconds
	Retention
	1 minute-14 days; default 4 days
	Max Message
	1 MiB
	Delay Queue

	DLQ
	Holds repeatedly failing messages
	maxReceiveCount
	Receives before DLQ redrive
	Idempotency

	Lambda ESM

	Partial Batch
	Retry only failed Lambda batch records
	SNS + SQS

	CloudWatch

If you are short on time, revise these first
What SQS is -> producer/consumer -> Standard vs FIFO -> at-least-once + idempotency -> visibility timeout -> Receive/Delete -> long polling -> retention -> DLQ -> Lambda integration -> SNS vs SQS -> interview Q&A.
	31. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * SQS message size is currently up to 1 MiB; many older notes still say 256 KB.
                  * Standard queues provide at-least-once delivery and best-effort ordering.
                  * FIFO queues preserve order within MessageGroupId and provide deduplication/exactly-once processing features.
                  * Long polling can wait up to 20 seconds.
                  * Visibility timeout can be configured up to 12 hours; default is 30 seconds.
                  * Message retention can be configured up to 14 days; default is 4 days.
                  * Lambda event source mappings support partial batch responses for SQS.
                  * FIFO queues can enable high-throughput mode when needed.

AWS API Gateway - Interview Study Notes
AWS API Gateway - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand API Gateway from first principles: what it does, when to use it, API types, routes/resources, integrations, Lambda proxy flow, authorization, traffic management, CORS, stages, custom domains, monitoring, and common system-design/interview scenarios.
	1. What is Amazon API Gateway?
Amazon API Gateway is a managed AWS service for creating, publishing, securing, monitoring, and managing HTTP, REST, and WebSocket APIs.
                  * It acts as a front door between API clients and backend services.
                  * Backends can include AWS Lambda, HTTP services, ECS/EC2 applications behind load balancers, and supported AWS services.
                  * API Gateway handles API-management concerns so your backend can focus on business logic.
                  * It scales as request traffic changes; you do not provision API Gateway servers.
  

The simplest mental model: client -> API Gateway -> backend service.
2. Why Do We Use API Gateway?
                  * Expose a clean public API without exposing backend implementation details.
                  * Route requests to Lambda, containers, EC2 services, or HTTP backends.
                  * Apply authentication/authorization before traffic reaches the application.
                  * Configure throttling and quotas to protect downstream services.
                  * Support CORS for browser clients.
                  * Publish separate stages such as dev, test, and prod.
                  * Use a custom domain such as api.example.com.
                  * Collect access logs and CloudWatch metrics.
                  * Build RESTful APIs or real-time WebSocket APIs.
Beginner mental model
API Gateway is to APIs what a managed front desk/security/routing layer is to a building: it receives requests, checks rules, routes them, and returns responses.
	3. API Gateway API Types
  

The first design decision is choosing HTTP API, REST API, or WebSocket API.
Type
	Communication
	Strength
	Typical Use
	HTTP API
	HTTP request/response
	Simpler, lower-cost RESTful API
	Lambda/HTTP microservices, OAuth/JWT APIs
	REST API
	HTTP request/response
	Most API-management features
	API keys, usage plans, caching, request validation, WAF, private API endpoints
	WebSocket API
	Persistent bidirectional connection
	Backend can push messages to clients
	Chat, notifications, live dashboards, games
	4. HTTP API vs REST API
Feature
	HTTP API
	REST API
	Core RESTful routing
	Yes
	Yes
	Lambda / HTTP integrations
	Yes
	Yes
	JWT/OIDC authorizer
	Built in
	Commonly Cognito/Lambda/IAM patterns
	Automatic CORS handling
	Yes
	More manual/configured
	Automatic deployments
	Supported
	Deployment workflow is more explicit
	API keys / usage plans
	No
	Yes
	Per-client throttling quotas
	No
	Yes via usage plans
	Request validation
	Limited compared with REST API
	Yes
	API caching
	No built-in stage cache
	Yes
	AWS WAF integration
	Not the same feature set
	Yes
	Private API endpoint
	No
	Yes
	Cost/complexity
	Lower / simpler
	Higher / more features
	Interview answer
Use HTTP API when you need a straightforward Lambda/HTTP API and do not need REST API's advanced API-management features. Use REST API when those extra features matter.
	5. WebSocket API
WebSocket APIs are different from normal request/response APIs. A connection stays open, so the client and backend can send messages independently.
                  * Useful when the server needs to push data to the client without polling.
                  * Common routes include $connect, $disconnect, and $default plus custom message routes.
                  * Typical use cases: chat apps, live notifications, collaboration, games, live financial/status updates.
HTTP vs WebSocket
HTTP API/REST API: client sends request, server returns response. WebSocket: persistent two-way connection allows server push.
	6. Core API Gateway Components
  

A request moves through a route/method, authorization, integration, deployment, and stage.
Concept
	Meaning
	Resource
	REST API path object, such as /users.
	Method
	REST API HTTP operation on a resource, such as GET /users.
	Route
	HTTP/WebSocket API routing key, such as GET /users.
	Integration
	Backend API Gateway invokes.
	Deployment
	Point-in-time representation/snapshot of API configuration.
	Stage
	Named environment/reference such as dev, test, prod, or $default.
	7. Routes, Resources, and Methods
Suppose you are building an Orders API:
Request
	Meaning
	Possible Backend
	GET /orders
	Fetch orders
	Lambda getOrders
	POST /orders
	Create an order
	Lambda createOrder
	GET /orders/{id}
	Fetch one order
	Lambda getOrder
	DELETE /orders/{id}
	Delete order
	Lambda deleteOrder
	                  * Path parameters: /users/{id}
                  * Query parameters: /users?page=2
                  * Headers: Authorization, Content-Type, custom headers
                  * Body: JSON payload for POST/PUT/PATCH
8. Integrations
An integration tells API Gateway where to send the request.
Integration
	Example
	When Used
	Lambda
	API Gateway -> Lambda function
	Serverless APIs
	HTTP/HTTP Proxy
	API Gateway -> public HTTP service
	Existing APIs/microservices
	Private Integration
	API Gateway -> VPC Link -> ALB/NLB/private service
	Private ECS/EC2 services
	AWS Service
	API Gateway -> supported AWS service action
	Direct service integrations in selected designs
	Mock (REST API)
	API Gateway creates response without backend
	Testing/simple fixed responses
	9. Lambda Proxy Integration
  

Proxy integration is the most common API Gateway + Lambda pattern.
                  * API Gateway passes request information to Lambda as an event.
                  * The event contains method/route information, headers, query parameters, path parameters, body, and request context.
                  * Lambda runs the business logic.
                  * Lambda returns a response that API Gateway converts to HTTP response data.
                  * HTTP APIs support Lambda proxy payload format versions 1.0 and 2.0.
Why proxy integration is popular
Less mapping configuration in API Gateway; the Lambda function receives the request context and has more control over the response.
	10. Proxy vs Non-Proxy / Custom Integration
Area
	Proxy Integration
	Non-Proxy / Custom
	Setup
	Simpler
	More configuration
	Request mapping
	Request largely passed through in defined proxy format
	Explicit mapping templates/parameters
	Response mapping
	Backend returns expected proxy response
	API Gateway maps integration response
	Use case
	Most Lambda/HTTP APIs
	Legacy/special transformations and strict mappings
	11. Authentication and Authorization
  

Protect APIs at the gateway rather than implementing every auth check independently in each backend.
Option
	Best For
	How It Works
	AWS IAM
	AWS-to-AWS/service access
	Requests are signed with AWS SigV4 credentials.
	JWT Authorizer
	HTTP APIs with OAuth 2.0/OIDC
	API Gateway validates JWT issuer/audience/scopes.
	Amazon Cognito
	User authentication in AWS-centric apps
	Cognito issues user tokens used to protect API routes/methods.
	Lambda Authorizer
	Custom auth rules
	API Gateway invokes Lambda to make authorization decision.
	NONE
	Public endpoint
	No gateway-level auth; use only intentionally.
	12. API Keys and Usage Plans
REST APIs support API keys and usage plans.
                  * API keys identify API consumers/clients.
                  * Usage plans associate API keys with deployed stages and can configure throttling and quotas.
                  * Do NOT use an API key as your only authentication/authorization mechanism.
                  * API keys can appear in headers/logs and are not equivalent to a user identity/token.
Interview trap
API key answers 'which client/consumer is this for usage-plan purposes?' It does not securely answer 'who is the user and what are they authorized to do?'
	13. Throttling
  

Throttling is protection against excessive request rate; REST caching is a separate performance feature.
                  * Throttling limits request rate and burst traffic.
                  * When limits are exceeded, API Gateway can return HTTP 429 Too Many Requests.
                  * There are service/account-level throttling considerations.
                  * REST API usage plans can add per-client throttling/quotas using API keys.
                  * Throttling protects backend capacity but should not be treated as a complete cost-control/security mechanism.
14. Caching
                  * REST APIs can use API Gateway stage/method caching.
                  * A cache hit can return a stored response without invoking the backend.
                  * This can reduce backend load and latency for cacheable GET-style requests.
                  * Caching has additional cost and requires correct cache keys/TTL/invalidation design.
                  * HTTP APIs do not provide the same built-in stage cache feature.
When not to cache
Avoid blindly caching personalized, frequently changing, or authorization-sensitive responses unless cache keys and security behavior are carefully designed.
	15. CORS
CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) is a browser security mechanism. It matters when frontend JavaScript calls an API hosted on a different origin.
                  * Example frontend: https://app.example.com
                  * Example API: https://api.example.com
                  * The browser may send an OPTIONS preflight request before the real request.
                  * HTTP APIs have built-in CORS configuration and can automatically respond to preflight requests.
                  * Configure allowed origins, methods, headers, and credentials carefully.
Common debugging issue
A backend can work perfectly in Postman/curl but fail in a browser because CORS is enforced by browsers, not by Postman.
	16. Stages and Deployments
  

Stages let the same API definition be published as dev/test/prod environments.
                  * Deployment = a publishable point-in-time API configuration.
                  * Stage = named environment/reference such as dev, test, prod.
                  * REST API changes normally must be deployed to a stage before clients see them.
                  * HTTP APIs can use automatic deployments, especially with a $default stage.
                  * Stage variables/settings can differ by environment.
17. Custom Domain Names
                  * Default API URL is generated by API Gateway and is not very friendly.
                  * Custom domain example: https://api.example.com
                  * Use an ACM TLS certificate for HTTPS.
                  * Create DNS mapping, commonly with Route 53, to the API Gateway domain.
                  * Map a base path/API mapping to a particular API and stage.
Typical architecture
Route 53 DNS -> API Gateway custom domain -> API stage -> integration.
	18. Endpoint Types and Private APIs
For REST APIs, endpoint type controls how clients reach API Gateway.
REST API Endpoint
	Meaning
	Typical Use
	Regional
	API endpoint in one AWS Region
	Most modern public API designs
	Edge-Optimized
	Traffic enters through CloudFront edge network
	Historically useful for geographically distributed clients
	Private
	Accessible privately from VPC through interface VPC endpoint
	Internal/private REST API
	Do not confuse
Private API endpoint = clients reach API Gateway privately. Private integration = API Gateway reaches a private backend through a VPC Link. These solve different sides of the connection.
	19. Private Integrations and VPC Link
  

VPC Link lets API Gateway reach private backends such as ALB/NLB or ECS services.
                  * Useful when your backend should stay in private subnets.
                  * HTTP APIs and REST APIs can use modern VPC link private integrations.
                  * A common flow is API Gateway -> VPC Link -> ALB/NLB -> ECS/EC2 service.
                  * HTTP API private integrations can also integrate through AWS Cloud Map in supported architectures.
                  * The backend itself does not need a public internet-facing endpoint.
20. Request / Response Transformation and Validation
                  * REST APIs support request validation for parameters and request bodies.
                  * API Gateway can transform headers, query strings, paths, and payloads depending on API type/integration.
                  * REST non-proxy integrations can use mapping templates for more explicit transformations.
                  * Keep transformations simple where possible; complex business logic belongs in the backend.
Design principle
API Gateway should enforce API-level concerns. Business rules such as pricing, inventory, and transaction logic belong in application services.
	21. Monitoring and Logging
                  * Use CloudWatch metrics to monitor request counts, latency, errors, and throttling.
                  * Enable access logging with useful request context fields.
                  * REST APIs can use execution logging for deeper API Gateway execution details.
                  * Use AWS X-Ray where supported/appropriate for tracing REST API requests.
                  * Monitor 4XX and 5XX error rates with CloudWatch alarms.
Metric / Signal
	What It Suggests
	4XX errors
	Client request, auth, validation, route, or throttling problems
	5XX errors
	Gateway/integration/backend failures
	Latency
	Total time client waits at API layer
	Integration latency
	Time spent waiting on backend integration
	429 responses
	Throttling limit exceeded
	22. Common HTTP Status Codes
Code
	Meaning
	Common API Gateway Context
	200
	Success
	Backend returned successful result
	400
	Bad Request
	Invalid payload/parameters
	401
	Unauthorized
	Authentication/token problem
	403
	Forbidden
	Authenticated but not allowed, IAM/policy/authorizer issue
	404
	Not Found
	Wrong route/resource or application result
	429
	Too Many Requests
	Throttling
	500
	Internal Server Error
	Backend/integration failure
	502
	Bad Gateway
	Invalid integration response / backend proxy issue
	504
	Gateway Timeout
	Integration/backend did not respond in time
	23. API Gateway + Lambda Typical Architecture
1.  Frontend calls POST /orders.
2.  API Gateway checks CORS and authorization.
3.  API Gateway routes POST /orders to the Lambda integration.
4.  Lambda validates business data and calls DynamoDB/RDS/other services.
5.  Lambda returns status and response body.
6.  API Gateway sends HTTP response to client.
Good serverless pattern
API Gateway handles API management; Lambda handles business logic; DynamoDB/RDS stores data; IAM controls service permissions; CloudWatch monitors the stack.
	24. API Gateway + ECS / EC2 Architecture
1.  Client calls API Gateway.
2.  API Gateway authenticates/throttles/routes the request.
                  1. VPC Link sends request to a private ALB/NLB.
4.  Load balancer sends traffic to ECS tasks or EC2 instances.
5.  Application returns response through the same path.
Why add API Gateway if ALB already exists?
API Gateway adds API-specific capabilities such as authorizers, API keys/usage plans, request management, stages, custom API publishing, and API-focused observability. ALB focuses primarily on load balancing Layer-7 traffic.
	25. API Gateway vs Application Load Balancer (ALB)
Area
	API Gateway
	ALB
	Primary purpose
	API management/front door
	Load balancing for applications
	Backends
	Lambda, HTTP, AWS services, private integrations
	EC2, ECS, IP, Lambda
	Auth
	IAM/JWT/Cognito/Lambda authorizers
	OIDC/Cognito authentication options, SG/network controls
	API keys/usage plans
	REST API supports
	No
	Stages/deployments
	Yes
	No API lifecycle concept
	Caching
	REST API caching
	No built-in API response cache
	WebSocket
	API Gateway WebSocket APIs
	ALB supports WebSockets as HTTP upgrade
	Best fit
	Managed APIs/serverless/API products
	Web apps, services, load balancing
	26. API Gateway vs Lambda Function URL
Area
	API Gateway
	Lambda Function URL
	Routing
	Multiple routes/services
	One Lambda function endpoint
	Authorization
	Many options
	Simpler NONE or AWS_IAM model
	API management
	Rich
	Minimal
	Custom domains
	Supported through API Gateway
	Not the same built-in API Gateway domain feature
	Use case
	Production API platform
	Simple webhook / small single-function endpoint
	Interview shortcut
Function URL is the simpler direct Lambda endpoint. API Gateway is the fuller API-management layer.
	27. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Need a cheap simple Lambda RESTful API.
Start with an HTTP API unless you need REST API-only features.
Scenario: Need API keys, usage plans and per-client quotas.
Use a REST API.
Scenario: Need OAuth/JWT authorization for an HTTP API.
Use an HTTP API JWT authorizer.
Scenario: Need custom authorization logic.
Use a Lambda authorizer.
Scenario: Frontend gets a CORS error but Postman works.
Configure CORS/OPTIONS behavior and allowed origin/method/header values.
Scenario: Backend ECS service must remain private.
Use API Gateway private integration through a VPC Link to ALB/NLB/service discovery.
Scenario: One API consumer is overloading your backend.
Use throttling; for REST APIs, usage plans can add per-client limits.
Scenario: Repeated GET endpoint is expensive.
For REST APIs, consider API caching if the response is safely cacheable.
Scenario: Need internal API reachable only from VPC.
Consider a private REST API endpoint.
Scenario: Need chat/live notifications.
Use API Gateway WebSocket API.
28. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Confirm the client is calling the correct API endpoint, stage, route/path, and HTTP method.
2.  Check authorization: token, IAM signature, authorizer configuration, and permissions.
3.  Check CORS if failure occurs only in a browser.
4.  Check API Gateway access/execution logs and CloudWatch metrics.
5.  Check integration configuration and backend permissions.
6.  For Lambda, check Lambda logs and returned proxy-response format.
7.  For private integrations, check VPC Link state, load balancer listener, target health, routing, and security groups.
8.  Check throttling if clients receive HTTP 429.
9.  Check timeout/latency if requests end in 504 or slow responses.
29. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is Amazon API Gateway?
Answer: API Gateway is a managed AWS service used to create, publish, secure, monitor, and manage REST, HTTP, and WebSocket APIs. It acts as a front door between clients and backend services such as Lambda, HTTP applications, ECS/EC2 services, and supported AWS services.
2. Why would you use API Gateway in front of Lambda?
Answer: It gives the Lambda function an HTTP API endpoint and adds routing, authorization, CORS, throttling, stages, custom domains, and monitoring. Lambda focuses on business logic while API Gateway manages the API layer.
3. What are the three major API Gateway API types?
Answer: HTTP API, REST API, and WebSocket API. HTTP and REST APIs use request/response HTTP communication, while WebSocket APIs provide persistent bidirectional communication.
4. HTTP API vs REST API - what is the main difference?
Answer: HTTP APIs are simpler and generally lower cost. REST APIs provide more API-management features such as API keys/usage plans, per-client throttling, request validation, caching, WAF integration, and private API endpoints.
5. What is a route in API Gateway?
Answer: A route maps an incoming request pattern to an integration. For an HTTP API, an example route is GET /users. In REST APIs, the similar model is resource + method.
6. What is an integration?
Answer: The integration is the backend API Gateway invokes after a request matches a route/method. Examples include Lambda, an HTTP endpoint, a VPC Link private backend, or a supported AWS service.
7. What is Lambda proxy integration?
Answer: It is an integration where API Gateway sends request information to Lambda in a defined event structure and Lambda returns a proxy-formatted result containing the HTTP status, headers, and body.
8. What is a stage?
Answer: A stage is a named environment or reference through which a deployed API is invoked, such as dev, test, or prod. A stage can have its own settings and URL mapping.
9. Deployment vs stage?
Answer: A deployment is a point-in-time publishable API configuration. A stage is the named environment/reference that points to a deployment and exposes it to clients.
10. What authorization methods can API Gateway use?
Answer: Depending on API type, common options include AWS IAM authorization, JWT/OIDC authorization, Amazon Cognito, and Lambda authorizers for custom rules.
11. Is an API key an authentication mechanism?
Answer: No. API keys are primarily used to identify API consumers for REST API usage plans and metering/throttling. Use IAM, JWT/Cognito, or a Lambda authorizer for real authentication/authorization.
12. What is a Lambda authorizer?
Answer: A Lambda authorizer is a Lambda function invoked by API Gateway to run custom authorization logic and decide whether a request should be allowed.
13. What is throttling?
Answer: Throttling limits request rate/burst traffic to protect API Gateway and downstream backends. Requests above the allowed rate can receive HTTP 429 Too Many Requests.
14. What are usage plans?
Answer: REST API usage plans associate API keys with API stages and optionally configure client-specific request throttling and quotas.
15. What is API Gateway caching?
Answer: REST API caching stores integration responses at the API Gateway stage/method layer. A cache hit can return a response without invoking the backend, reducing latency and backend load.
16. What is CORS?
Answer: CORS is a browser security mechanism controlling whether frontend JavaScript from one origin can call an API at another origin. API Gateway must return appropriate Access-Control-* headers and sometimes handle OPTIONS preflight requests.
17. Why can an API work in Postman but fail in the browser?
Answer: A common reason is CORS. Browsers enforce CORS rules while tools such as Postman usually do not.
18. What is a VPC Link?
Answer: A VPC Link lets API Gateway integrate with private resources inside a VPC, commonly through an ALB or NLB, so the backend does not need to be publicly accessible.
19. Private API vs private integration?
Answer: A private REST API controls how clients privately reach API Gateway. A private integration controls how API Gateway reaches a private backend through a VPC Link. They solve opposite sides of the request path.
20. What is a custom domain in API Gateway?
Answer: It replaces the generated execute-api URL with a friendly hostname such as api.example.com. You typically use an ACM certificate and DNS mapping, then map the domain/path to an API stage.
21. API Gateway vs ALB?
Answer: API Gateway is an API-management service with routes, authorizers, stages, usage plans, and API publishing features. ALB is primarily a Layer-7 load balancer for distributing HTTP/HTTPS traffic across application targets.
22. API Gateway vs Lambda Function URL?
Answer: A Lambda Function URL is a simpler direct HTTP endpoint for one Lambda function. API Gateway supports richer routing, authorization choices, API lifecycle, custom domains, multiple backends, throttling, and API-management features.
23. When would you use a WebSocket API?
Answer: Use it when you need persistent two-way communication or server push, such as chat, notifications, live dashboards, collaboration, or multiplayer applications.
24. How would you secure a public API?
Answer: Use HTTPS, appropriate authorization (IAM/JWT/Cognito/Lambda authorizer), least-privilege backend IAM permissions, throttling, validation, logging/monitoring, and where applicable AWS WAF. Do not rely on API keys alone for security.
25. What would you check if API Gateway returns 502?
Answer: Check the backend/integration response format, Lambda errors, permissions, integration configuration, and logs. For Lambda proxy integration, malformed Lambda responses are a common cause.
26. What would you check for 504 Gateway Timeout?
Answer: Check backend response time, integration timeout behavior, downstream dependencies, database calls, network paths, and whether the operation should be asynchronous instead of a long synchronous API request.
27. How can API Gateway call a private ECS service?
Answer: Create a private integration using a VPC Link, connect the API route to an ALB/NLB or supported service-discovery target, then route traffic to the private ECS tasks.
28. Can API Gateway directly perform business logic?
Answer: It can perform API-level routing, transformations, validation, authorization, and some direct AWS service integrations, but complex business logic should normally live in Lambda or application services.
29. How do you monitor API Gateway?
Answer: Use CloudWatch metrics and alarms, access logs, REST API execution logs where appropriate, backend logs such as Lambda CloudWatch Logs, and tracing such as X-Ray where supported.
30. Which API type should I choose in a normal serverless interview scenario?
Answer: If the requirements are a simple Lambda/HTTP RESTful API, HTTP API is a strong starting point. Choose REST API when requirements explicitly need its advanced features. Choose WebSocket API for persistent bidirectional communication.
30. Final API Gateway Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword

	Simpler/lower-cost RESTful API product
	REST API
	Feature-rich API management
	WebSocket API

	Route

	Integration

	Lambda Proxy

	Deployment
