If you are short on time, revise these first
What API Gateway is -> HTTP vs REST vs WebSocket -> route/resource/method -> integrations -> Lambda proxy -> authorization -> API keys are not auth -> throttling -> CORS -> stages/deployments -> VPC Link -> API Gateway vs ALB -> interview Q&A.
	31. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * HTTP APIs are intentionally simpler and lower-feature than REST APIs.
                  * REST APIs support API keys/usage plans, request validation, caching, AWS WAF integration, and private API endpoints.
                  * HTTP APIs support built-in CORS configuration, JWT/OIDC authorization, and automatic deployments.
                  * Modern VPC Link V2 private integrations can connect HTTP and REST APIs to private ALB/NLB resources; HTTP APIs also support AWS Cloud Map integrations.
                  * API keys should not be used as the sole authentication/authorization mechanism.
32. AWS Documentation References
                  * Amazon API Gateway Developer Guide - What is Amazon API Gateway?
                  * Choose between REST APIs and HTTP APIs
                  * API Gateway HTTP APIs
                  * API Gateway REST APIs
                  * API Gateway WebSocket APIs
                  * Lambda integrations / Lambda proxy integrations
                  * Usage plans and API keys for REST APIs
                  * Throttle requests to REST APIs
                  * CORS for HTTP APIs and REST APIs
                  * Stages and deployments
                  * Custom domain names and API mappings
                  * Private integrations and VPC links


AWS ECS - Interview Study Notes
AWS ECS - Interview Study Notes
Beginner to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand how Amazon ECS runs containers, how task definitions/services/clusters fit together, Fargate vs EC2, networking, IAM, scaling, deployments, logging, and common production interview scenarios.
	1. What is Amazon ECS?
Amazon Elastic Container Service (ECS) is AWS's managed container orchestration service. You provide container images and configuration; ECS schedules, runs, monitors, replaces, and scales container tasks on supported compute capacity.
                  * ECS is an orchestrator. Docker builds/runs containers; ECS coordinates containers across compute infrastructure.
                  * Common workloads: REST APIs, Spring Boot/Node services, microservices, workers, scheduled jobs, and background consumers.
                  * ECS integrates with ECR, IAM, VPC, ALB/NLB, CloudWatch, Auto Scaling, Secrets Manager, and other AWS services.
Simple interview definition
ECS is AWS's managed container orchestration service for deploying and operating Docker/container workloads without managing your own Kubernetes control plane.
	  

A typical ECS flow from container image to running service tasks.
2. ECS Core Building Blocks
  

Cluster, Task Definition, Task, and Service are the four concepts you must distinguish clearly.
Concept
	What to Remember
	Cluster
	Logical grouping/boundary for services, tasks and capacity.
	Task Definition
	Versioned blueprint describing one or more containers.
	Task
	A running instantiation of a task definition.
	Service
	Maintains a desired number of tasks and handles deployments/replacements.
	Standalone Task
	Run-to-completion or one-off task not continuously maintained by a service.
	Memorize this
Task Definition = blueprint. Task = running copy. Service = keeps N copies running.
	3. Container Image and Amazon ECR
                  * Your application is packaged as a container image.
                  * Amazon ECR is AWS's managed container image registry and is commonly used with ECS.
                  * Typical CI/CD flow: build image -> tag image -> push to ECR -> register new task definition revision -> deploy ECS service.
                  * Use immutable image tags or image digests in disciplined production pipelines to make deployments reproducible.
4. ECS Compute Options
  

ECS can run tasks on multiple compute models. Fargate vs EC2 is the core interview comparison.
Feature
	Fargate
	ECS on EC2
	ECS Managed Instances
	Infrastructure
	AWS manages servers
	You manage EC2 fleet
	AWS manages EC2-backed infrastructure
	Control
	Less host-level control
	Maximum host control
	EC2 variety with less infrastructure work
	Billing model
	Task CPU/memory/runtime
	EC2 instance capacity
	Managed compute + EC2-style capabilities
	Good for
	Most standard container services
	Specialized hosts, GPUs, custom tuning, high utilization
	Need specialized EC2 capabilities but less fleet management
	Networking
	awsvpc required
	awsvpc/bridge/host/none (OS-dependent)
	awsvpc/host depending on configuration
	Interview shortcut
Fargate = less infrastructure management. ECS on EC2 = more control and potentially more tuning/cost optimization responsibility.
	5. Task Definition
A task definition is a versioned JSON blueprint for the containers in an ECS task.
  

The task definition contains runtime, networking, security, logging, and container configuration.
                  * Family + revision: e.g. orders-api:17.
                  * Container image URI, CPU/memory, command/entry point, environment variables and port mappings.
                  * Task role and task execution role.
                  * Logging configuration such as awslogs to CloudWatch Logs.
                  * Secrets from Secrets Manager or Systems Manager Parameter Store.
                  * Health checks and volumes where needed.
Important
Registering a new task definition creates a new revision. Existing running tasks do not magically change until you deploy/run the new revision.
	6. ECS Service
An ECS service is used for long-running applications. The ECS service scheduler maintains the desired number of healthy tasks.
                  * Desired count = how many task replicas you want running.
                  * If a task crashes, the service launches a replacement.
                  * A service can integrate with ALB/NLB and service discovery.
                  * Updating the service to a new task definition revision starts a deployment.
Example
Desired count = 3. One task crashes. ECS service detects the loss and launches another task to return to 3.
	7. Fargate Networking and awsvpc
  

A common production pattern: public ALB, private ECS tasks, security-group-to-security-group access.
                  * Fargate tasks always use awsvpc network mode.
                  * With awsvpc, each task gets its own elastic network interface (ENI), private IP, and security groups.
                  * For private-subnet tasks that need internet access, use NAT or suitable VPC endpoints depending on the dependency.
                  * For ECR image pulls without general internet access, VPC endpoints can be used for relevant AWS services.
Interview architecture
Internet -> ALB in public subnets -> ECS tasks in private subnets -> RDS/ElastiCache in private subnets.
	8. ECS on EC2 Networking Modes
Mode
	How It Works
	Interview Note
	awsvpc
	Task gets its own ENI/IP
	Recommended unless you specifically need another mode.
	bridge
	Docker bridge network on EC2 host
	Supports dynamic host port mapping; common older EC2 pattern.
	host
	Container uses host network stack
	High performance but port conflicts limit placement.
	none
	No external networking
	Rare/specialized use.
	9. Load Balancer Integration
                  * Application Load Balancer is common for HTTP/HTTPS microservices.
                  * ECS registers healthy service tasks into an ALB target group.
                  * ALB health checks determine whether a task should receive traffic.
                  * Path/host rules can route different URLs/domains to different ECS services.
                  * Network Load Balancer is useful for Layer-4/TCP/UDP/TLS style workloads.
Example
/users/* -> User ECS service target group; /orders/* -> Order ECS service target group.
	10. Health Checks
                  * Container health check: command defined in the task definition runs inside the container.
                  * Load balancer health check: ALB/NLB probes the registered target.
                  * ECS uses service/task health signals to replace unhealthy tasks.
                  * Health endpoints should verify the application is ready to serve traffic, not merely that the process exists.
11. ECS IAM Roles
  

The task role and task execution role solve different permission problems.
Role
	Used By
	Typical Permissions
	Task Role
	Application code inside container
	S3, DynamoDB, SQS, SNS, Secrets APIs as required
	Task Execution Role
	ECS agent/platform on task's behalf
	Pull private ECR image, write logs, fetch referenced secrets
	Container Instance Role
	EC2 hosts when using ECS on EC2
	Register EC2 host with cluster and agent operations
	Most asked IAM question
Task execution role = ECS/platform actions. Task role = your application's AWS API permissions.
	12. Secrets and Configuration
                  * Non-sensitive configuration can be environment variables.
                  * Sensitive values should come from Secrets Manager or Systems Manager Parameter Store rather than baked into the image.
                  * The task execution role may need permission to retrieve secrets referenced by the task definition.
                  * Application-specific runtime AWS access should use the task role.
13. ECS Service Auto Scaling
  

Two different scaling problems: number of tasks vs amount of EC2 host capacity.
                  * Service Auto Scaling changes the ECS service desired task count.
                  * It uses Application Auto Scaling and CloudWatch metrics.
                  * Target tracking is usually the simplest option: maintain CPU, memory, or another supported metric around a target value.
                  * Step scaling and scheduled scaling are also available; predictive scaling exists for suitable patterns.
                  * Example: average CPU > target -> add tasks. Demand falls -> remove tasks within configured min/max limits.
14. ECS Cluster Auto Scaling (EC2)
When ECS runs on EC2, adding more tasks does not help if the cluster lacks CPU/memory capacity. Capacity providers can integrate ECS with an EC2 Auto Scaling Group.
                  * Service Auto Scaling: changes task count.
                  * Cluster Auto Scaling: changes EC2 container-instance count.
                  * Capacity provider managed scaling can add/remove EC2 instances based on task demand and capacity reservation.
Classic interview trap
Task scaling and host scaling are separate concerns on ECS/EC2. Fargate removes most host-capacity management from you.
	15. Capacity Providers
Capacity providers let ECS decide which compute capacity should run tasks and can manage scaling strategies.
                  * Fargate and Fargate Spot can be used through capacity-provider strategies.
                  * For ECS on EC2, a capacity provider can be associated with an EC2 Auto Scaling Group.
                  * Strategies can use base and weight values to distribute tasks among capacity providers.
16. Fargate Spot
                  * Runs eligible Fargate tasks on spare AWS capacity for a lower price.
                  * Tasks can be interrupted, so workloads must tolerate replacement.
                  * Good for stateless workers, batch processing, asynchronous consumers, and fault-tolerant services.
                  * Use normal Fargate for baseline capacity and Fargate Spot for cost-optimized extra capacity when appropriate.
17. ECS Deployment Strategies
  

Rolling and blue/green are the main interview topics; ECS also supports newer native traffic-shifting strategies.
Strategy
	Behavior
	Use
	Rolling
	Gradually replaces old tasks with new tasks
	Default/common balance of safety and resource efficiency
	Blue/Green
	Runs old and new revisions separately, then switches traffic
	Validation before production and quick rollback
	Canary
	Shifts a small percentage first, then the remainder
	Reduce risk by testing with limited production traffic
	Linear
	Moves traffic in equal increments over time
	Gradual controlled rollout
	Interview focus
Be strongest on rolling vs blue/green. Know canary and linear as current ECS-native options.
	18. Rolling Deployment Controls
                  * minimumHealthyPercent controls how many existing healthy tasks must remain during a rolling deployment.
                  * maximumPercent controls how many total tasks may temporarily run above desired count during deployment.
                  * New tasks typically start receiving load balancer traffic after they become healthy.
                  * Deployment circuit breaker/rollback features can help recover from failed deployments.
19. Service Discovery and Service Connect
                  * Not every service-to-service call should go through a public load balancer.
                  * AWS Cloud Map can provide service discovery using DNS.
                  * ECS Service Connect provides managed service-to-service connectivity, discovery and traffic features for ECS services.
                  * For a beginner/intermediate full-stack interview, know the purpose; deep configuration is usually unnecessary.
20. Persistent Storage
Storage
	Use
	Notes
	Container / ephemeral storage
	Temporary files, caches, scratch space
	Do not keep irreplaceable state only inside the task.
	Amazon EFS
	Shared POSIX filesystem
	Useful when multiple tasks need shared persistent files.
	Amazon EBS
	Block volume attached to supported ECS tasks
	Useful for stateful/specialized workloads; requires appropriate ECS infrastructure configuration.
	S3
	Object storage outside container
	Best for files/objects rather than mounted POSIX disk semantics.
	Design principle
Treat ECS tasks as replaceable. Put durable state in services such as RDS, DynamoDB, S3, EFS, or other appropriate storage.
	21. Logging and Monitoring
                  * awslogs log driver commonly sends container stdout/stderr to CloudWatch Logs.
                  * CloudWatch metrics can monitor CPU, memory, running/pending task count and service-level health.
                  * Container Insights provides deeper ECS/container observability when enabled.
                  * Use CloudWatch alarms for service availability and scaling signals.
                  * Use CloudTrail to audit ECS API changes and deployments.
22. ECS Exec
ECS Exec lets authorized operators run commands inside a running container for debugging without opening SSH to the task.
                  * It integrates with AWS Systems Manager Session Manager mechanisms and IAM authorization.
                  * It must be enabled/configured correctly on the service/task and requires appropriate permissions.
                  * Use it for controlled debugging, not as a substitute for proper logs, metrics, or immutable deployments.
23. ECS vs EKS vs EC2 vs Lambda
Service
	Model
	Best When
	You Manage
	ECS
	AWS-native container orchestration
	You want containers without Kubernetes complexity
	Task/service config; compute depends on Fargate vs EC2
	EKS
	Managed Kubernetes
	Kubernetes APIs/ecosystem are required
	Kubernetes workload/config and possibly worker capacity
	EC2
	Virtual machines
	Need VM/OS-level control
	OS, runtime, app and scaling architecture
	Lambda
	Functions/serverless
	Short event-driven functions with serverless execution
	Function code/config, not servers
	24. ECS vs Docker
                  * Docker/container runtime builds and runs containers.
                  * ECS orchestrates containers: placement, desired count, health replacement, deployments, networking integration, IAM integration and scaling.
Simple answer
Docker runs a container. ECS operates containerized applications at service/cluster scale.
	25. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Task keeps stopping immediately.
Check stopped reason -> application exit code/logs -> task definition CPU/memory -> environment/secrets -> image/command -> IAM/network dependencies.
Scenario: ALB returns 503 for ECS service.
Check service has running tasks -> target group registrations -> health-check path/port -> security groups -> application listens on correct container port.
Scenario: Fargate task in private subnet cannot pull ECR image.
Check route to NAT or required ECR/S3 VPC endpoints, DNS, security/NACL rules and task execution role permissions.
Scenario: App inside task gets AccessDenied to S3.
Check task role, not task execution role, and verify least-privilege S3 permissions/resource ARN.
Scenario: Service CPU is high.
Use ECS Service Auto Scaling to increase desired tasks; also investigate app bottleneck and downstream limits.
Scenario: ECS on EC2 has pending tasks despite service scaling.
Cluster may lack host capacity; inspect task placement/resource requirements and capacity provider/EC2 Auto Scaling.
Scenario: Need zero/very low downtime deployment with easy rollback.
Use blue/green or another controlled traffic-shifting strategy with health validation.
Scenario: Need shell access to debug a Fargate container.
Use ECS Exec with appropriate IAM/SSM configuration instead of SSH.
26. Troubleshooting a Pending or Stopped Task
                  1. Check ECS service events and the task stopped reason.
                  2. Confirm image exists and task execution role can pull it.
                  3. Check CPU/memory values and available capacity (especially ECS on EC2).
                  4. Check subnet, route table, security group, DNS and required endpoints/NAT.
                  5. Check secrets/configuration permissions.
                  6. Check application command, environment and logs.
                  7. Check load balancer target health and container port mapping if service is behind ALB/NLB.
27. Must-Know ECS Interview Questions
                  * What is ECS?
                  * ECS vs Docker?
                  * Cluster vs Task Definition vs Task vs Service?
                  * What is a desired count?
                  * Fargate vs ECS on EC2?
                  * What is ECS Managed Instances?
                  * What is a task definition revision?
                  * Task role vs task execution role?
                  * How does ECS integrate with ECR?
                  * Why does Fargate require awsvpc?
                  * How does an ALB route to ECS tasks?
                  * How do health checks work?
                  * Service Auto Scaling vs Cluster Auto Scaling?
                  * What is a capacity provider?
                  * What is Fargate Spot?
                  * Rolling vs blue/green deployment?
                  * What is minimumHealthyPercent / maximumPercent?
                  * How should ECS containers store secrets?
                  * How do ECS tasks get persistent storage?
                  * What is ECS Exec?
                  * ECS vs EKS?
                  * How would you troubleshoot a task that keeps stopping?
28. Final ECS Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	ECS
	AWS managed container orchestration
	Cluster
	Logical group/boundary for ECS workloads and capacity
	Task Definition
	Versioned container blueprint
	Task
	Running copy of a task definition
	Service
	Keeps desired task count running
	ECR
	AWS container image registry
	Fargate
	Serverless compute for ECS containers
	ECS on EC2
	ECS tasks running on your EC2 fleet
	awsvpc
	Task gets ENI/IP/security groups
	Task Role
	AWS permissions used by app code
	Execution Role
	Permissions ECS needs to start/run task
	Desired Count
	Number of service task replicas
	ALB Target Group
	Healthy ECS tasks that receive traffic
	Service Auto Scaling
	Changes number of tasks
	Cluster Auto Scaling
	Changes EC2 capacity
	Capacity Provider
	How ECS selects/manages compute capacity
	Fargate Spot
	Interruptible discounted Fargate capacity
	Rolling
	Gradually replace tasks
	Blue/Green
	Run old/new and switch traffic
	EFS
	Shared persistent filesystem
	ECS Exec
	Run debug commands in a container without SSH
	

If you are short on time, revise these first
ECS basics -> Cluster/Task Definition/Task/Service -> Fargate vs EC2 -> task definition -> awsvpc/networking -> ALB -> task role vs execution role -> service scaling vs cluster scaling -> rolling vs blue/green -> logging/troubleshooting.
	29. Current AWS Notes Worth Recognizing
                  * Fargate tasks require awsvpc networking.
                  * AWS recommends awsvpc for ECS on EC2 unless another network mode is specifically needed.
                  * ECS Managed Instances is a newer managed compute option that keeps access to many EC2 capabilities while AWS manages more of the infrastructure lifecycle.
                  * ECS Service Auto Scaling supports target tracking, step, scheduled, and predictive scaling options.
                  * ECS supports native deployment strategies including rolling, blue/green, linear, and canary; rolling remains the standard default/common interview baseline.


AWS EKS - Interview Study Notes
AWS EKS - Interview Study Notes
Beginner to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand Kubernetes fundamentals and how Amazon EKS manages the Kubernetes control plane while integrating worker compute, networking, IAM, load balancing, storage, scaling, and observability with AWS.
	1. What is Amazon EKS?
Amazon EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service) is AWS's managed Kubernetes service. It runs a conformant Kubernetes control plane for you while your applications run as Kubernetes workloads on compute such as EC2 nodes, AWS Fargate, or EKS Auto Mode.
                  * AWS manages the Kubernetes control plane infrastructure, availability, scaling, patching, and replacement of unhealthy control-plane components.
                  * You still use standard Kubernetes APIs and tools such as kubectl, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets, and Ingress.
                  * EKS integrates Kubernetes with AWS services such as VPC, IAM, ELB, EBS, EFS, CloudWatch, and Route 53.
Simple interview definition
EKS is managed Kubernetes on AWS: AWS manages the control plane, while you deploy containers as Kubernetes workloads on AWS compute.
	  

EKS separates the AWS-managed Kubernetes control plane from your worker/data plane.
2. EKS vs Self-Managed Kubernetes
Area
	Self-Managed Kubernetes
	Amazon EKS
	Control plane
	You deploy and operate API server, etcd, controllers
	AWS manages it
	High availability
	You design and maintain it
	Built into EKS control-plane architecture
	Patching/upgrades
	You manage control plane
	EKS manages control-plane infrastructure; you choose supported version upgrades
	Worker nodes
	You manage
	You choose Auto Mode, managed/self-managed EC2 nodes, or Fargate
	Kubernetes API
	Standard Kubernetes
	Standard conformant Kubernetes
	3. Kubernetes Building Blocks You Must Know
  

These Kubernetes resources appear constantly in EKS interviews.
Resource
	Interview Meaning
	Pod
	Smallest Kubernetes execution unit; contains one or more tightly related containers.
	Deployment
	Manages stateless Pods, replica count, rolling updates, and rollback.
	ReplicaSet
	Ensures the desired number of Pod replicas; normally managed by a Deployment.
	Service
	Stable network endpoint/load balancing for a set of Pods.
	Ingress
	HTTP/HTTPS routing rules from outside the cluster to Services.
	ConfigMap
	Non-sensitive application configuration.
	Secret
	Sensitive configuration object; protect access and consider external secret stores/encryption.
	Namespace
	Logical partition for resources and policies.
	DaemonSet
	Runs a Pod on each matching node; common for agents.
	StatefulSet
	Stable identity and storage behavior for stateful applications.
	4. Control Plane vs Data Plane
Plane
	Contains
	Who Manages It in EKS
	Control Plane
	API server, scheduler, controller manager, etcd
	AWS
	Data Plane
	Worker compute running kubelet, container runtime, and Pods
	AWS/user depending on compute option
	Interview point
EKS does not mean AWS automatically manages every EC2 worker node. That depends on whether you choose Auto Mode, managed node groups, self-managed nodes, or Fargate.
	5. EKS Control Plane High Availability
                  * Each EKS cluster has its own managed control plane.
                  * EKS distributes control-plane components across multiple Availability Zones.
                  * AWS documentation describes at least two API server instances and three etcd instances across three Availability Zones.
                  * EKS monitors and replaces unhealthy control-plane instances and scales the control plane as needed.
                  * The Kubernetes API endpoint can be configured for public access, private access, or both.
What you manage
You choose when to upgrade Kubernetes versions and must ensure workloads/add-ons remain compatible, but you do not SSH into or administer EKS control-plane servers.
	6. Compute Options
  

Current EKS compute choices. Most interviews focus on managed node groups and Fargate; Auto Mode is increasingly relevant.
Option
	Server Management
	Flexibility
	Best Fit
	EKS Auto Mode
	AWS handles much of node/infrastructure lifecycle
	High, but opinionated managed model
	Reduce Kubernetes infrastructure operations
	Managed Node Groups
	EKS manages EC2 node-group lifecycle
	High
	Most EC2-based EKS workloads
	Self-Managed Nodes
	You manage EC2 lifecycle
	Maximum
	Special AMIs/kernel/bootstrap/customization
	Fargate
	No EC2 nodes to manage
	More constraints
	Serverless Pod compute
	7. Managed Node Groups
                  * Managed node groups provision EC2 worker nodes as part of an EC2 Auto Scaling group.
                  * EKS automates node provisioning, updates, termination, and draining during managed operations.
                  * Node groups can use On-Demand or Spot capacity.
                  * Use labels and taints to influence which workloads are scheduled to which nodes.
                  * Choose self-managed nodes only when you need deeper EC2/AMI/bootstrap control than managed node groups provide.
Interview shortcut
Managed node group = Kubernetes worker nodes are still EC2 instances, but EKS manages much of their lifecycle.
	8. EKS on Fargate
                  * Fargate runs eligible Kubernetes Pods without you provisioning EC2 worker nodes.
                  * Fargate profiles determine which Pods/namespaces are scheduled onto Fargate.
                  * Each Fargate Pod has its own compute boundary.
                  * Fargate supports ALB/NLB integration using IP targets.
                  * Fargate has important limitations such as no DaemonSets, privileged containers, GPUs, HostNetwork, or HostPort.
                  * EKS Fargate Pods run in private subnets.
When to use
Use Fargate when you prefer serverless Pod compute and your workload fits its restrictions. Use EC2 nodes for broader Kubernetes flexibility, specialized hardware, or daemon/privileged workloads.
	9. EKS Auto Mode
EKS Auto Mode extends AWS management beyond the Kubernetes control plane to much of the infrastructure required to run workloads.
                  * Automatically provisions and consolidates compute capacity.
                  * Includes managed capabilities for Pod/service networking, DNS, load balancing, block storage, node monitoring, and Pod Identity integration.
                  * Builds its compute autoscaling capabilities on Karpenter concepts.
                  * Useful to know as a modern option, but interviews may still focus heavily on traditional managed node groups + add-ons.
Do not confuse
EKS Auto Mode is still Kubernetes/EKS. It reduces infrastructure management; it does not replace Kubernetes objects such as Pods, Deployments, and Services.
	10. EKS Networking and Amazon VPC CNI
  

Traditional EC2-backed EKS clusters commonly use Amazon VPC CNI so Pods receive VPC addresses.
                  * An EKS cluster is associated with a VPC and subnets.
                  * For EC2 worker nodes, Amazon VPC CNI provides Pod networking and can assign VPC IP addresses to Pods.
                  * This gives Pods direct VPC connectivity but makes subnet/IP-address planning important.
                  * Security groups protect nodes and, for supported configurations, can also be applied to Pods.
                  * CoreDNS provides Kubernetes DNS inside the cluster.
Common issue
If Pods cannot be scheduled or created at scale, insufficient subnet IP addresses can be one possible cause in VPC-CNI-based clusters.
	11. Service Types
Service Type
	Purpose
	Typical Use
	ClusterIP
	Internal virtual IP inside cluster
	Default internal service-to-service communication
	NodePort
	Opens a port on every node
	Usually an implementation building block; less common directly in production
	LoadBalancer
	Requests an external/cloud load balancer
	Expose TCP/UDP/HTTP services through AWS load balancing
	ExternalName
	DNS alias to an external name
	Reference an external service through Kubernetes DNS
	12. Ingress and AWS Load Balancer Controller
  

AWS Load Balancer Controller reconciles Kubernetes resources into AWS ALB/NLB infrastructure.
                  * Ingress provides Layer-7 HTTP/HTTPS host/path routing rules.
                  * AWS recommends the AWS Load Balancer Controller for EKS load-balancer integration.
                  * Ingress commonly provisions an Application Load Balancer (ALB).
                  * Service type LoadBalancer can provision a Network Load Balancer (NLB).
                  * With IP target mode, traffic can be sent directly to Pod IPs instead of node ports.
                  * EKS Auto Mode includes load-balancing capability without installing the traditional controller yourself.
13. Kubernetes Deployment and Rolling Update
                  * A Deployment defines the desired image, Pod template, and number of replicas.
                  * When the image/template changes, Kubernetes can gradually create new Pods and remove old Pods.
                  * Readiness probes prevent a Pod from receiving Service traffic before it is ready.
                  * Liveness probes help detect a stuck/broken application and trigger container restart.
                  * Rollback can return a Deployment to an earlier revision.
Interview distinction
Readiness = should this Pod receive traffic? Liveness = should Kubernetes restart this container?
	14. ConfigMap vs Secret
Resource
	Stores
	Interview Guidance
	ConfigMap
	Non-sensitive configuration
	Environment variables, config files, feature flags
	Secret
	Sensitive values
	Passwords/tokens/certs; restrict RBAC and consider KMS/external secrets
	Important
Kubernetes Secret data is not magically safe merely because it is called a Secret. Protect etcd/encryption, RBAC, access paths, and consider AWS Secrets Manager/Parameter Store integrations.
	15. IAM Permissions for Pods
  

Do not give every Pod the worker node's broad IAM permissions.
                  * Applications inside Pods often need S3, DynamoDB, SQS, Secrets Manager, or other AWS APIs.
                  * EKS Pod Identity maps an IAM role to a Kubernetes service account using an EKS association.
                  * IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) is the older widely used OIDC-based pattern and remains supported.
                  * AWS currently recommends EKS Pod Identity when possible for EKS workloads.
                  * Both approaches support fine-grained IAM permissions per workload/service account.
Interview answer
Use a dedicated Kubernetes service account mapped to a least-privilege IAM role. Do not hardcode AWS keys and do not rely on the broad EC2 node IAM role.
	16. Human / kubectl Access to the Cluster
  

IAM authenticates the caller; Kubernetes/EKS policies determine what the caller can do.
                  * kubectl ultimately communicates with the Kubernetes API server.
                  * EKS access entries are the recommended AWS-native mechanism for granting IAM principals cluster access.
                  * An access entry can associate an AWS-managed EKS access policy or map to Kubernetes groups/RBAC.
                  * The legacy aws-auth ConfigMap is deprecated for managing cluster access, though older clusters/interview questions may still mention it.
Two layers
AWS IAM identifies/authenticates the principal. Kubernetes authorization (RBAC or EKS access policies) determines allowed Kubernetes actions.
	17. Kubernetes RBAC
Object
	Purpose
	Role
	Permissions inside one namespace.
	ClusterRole
	Cluster-wide permissions or reusable permission set.
	RoleBinding
	Assigns a Role/ClusterRole to subjects within a namespace.
	ClusterRoleBinding
	Assigns a ClusterRole across the cluster.
	                  * Apply least privilege; avoid casually granting cluster-admin.
                  * Do not confuse Kubernetes RBAC permissions with AWS IAM permissions to S3/EC2/etc.
18. Autoscaling: HPA vs Node Scaling
  

Application replicas and cluster compute capacity are separate scaling layers.
Scaler
	What It Scales
	Trigger / Purpose
	Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA)
	Pod replica count
	CPU, memory, or custom/external metrics
	Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA)
	Pod resource requests/recommendations
	Right-size CPU/memory; use carefully with HPA
	Cluster Autoscaler
	Node group size
	Pending Pods that need more node capacity
	Karpenter
	EC2 node capacity/type
	Provisions right-sized nodes from workload requirements
	EKS Auto Mode
	Cluster compute automatically
	AWS-managed provisioning/consolidation based on workload needs
	Most important distinction
HPA adds/removes Pods. Karpenter/Cluster Autoscaler/Auto Mode add or remove worker compute when Pods cannot fit or capacity can be consolidated.
	19. Taints, Tolerations, Labels, and Node Selectors
                  * Labels attach metadata to Kubernetes objects such as nodes and Pods.
                  * nodeSelector / node affinity lets Pods prefer or require particular nodes.
                  * Taints repel Pods from nodes.
                  * Tolerations let matching Pods be scheduled onto tainted nodes.
                  * Useful for GPU nodes, Spot nodes, dedicated workloads, or isolation by workload type.
Example
Taint GPU nodes with workload=gpu:NoSchedule, then give only GPU workloads a matching toleration.
	20. Persistent Storage: EBS and EFS
AWS Storage
	Kubernetes Integration
	Typical Use
	Amazon EBS
	EBS CSI driver + PersistentVolume/PersistentVolumeClaim
	Block storage for one workload/node-AZ attachment patterns
	Amazon EFS
	EFS CSI driver
	Shared POSIX file storage across multiple Pods/nodes/AZs
	                  * PersistentVolume (PV) represents storage.
                  * PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) is a workload's request for storage.
                  * StorageClass can dynamically provision storage.
Stateful interview point
Containers and Pods are replaceable. Persistent data should live outside the container's ephemeral filesystem.
	21. Observability
                  * CloudWatch can collect control-plane logs, application logs, metrics, and alarms depending on configuration.
                  * Container Insights provides container/cluster observability.
                  * Prometheus-compatible metrics are common for Kubernetes applications.
                  * kubectl logs, kubectl describe, events, Pod status, and Deployment status are first-line troubleshooting tools.
                  * Distributed tracing can be added through OpenTelemetry/X-Ray-compatible tooling.
Useful commands
kubectl get pods -A | kubectl describe pod <name> | kubectl logs <pod> | kubectl get events | kubectl get svc/ingress/deploy
	22. Common Pod States / Failures
State / Error
	Typical Meaning
	Pending
	No suitable node/capacity, PVC issue, Fargate profile mismatch, scheduling constraints
	CrashLoopBackOff
	Container starts and repeatedly crashes
	ImagePullBackOff
	Image cannot be pulled: name/tag/auth/network/ECR permission issue
	ErrImagePull
	Immediate image pull failure
	OOMKilled
	Container exceeded memory limit
	Evicted
	Node pressure or scheduling/resource condition caused eviction
	Running but NotReady
	Container runs but readiness probe is failing
	23. Typical Troubleshooting Flow
                  1. kubectl get pods -o wide - determine status, node, restart count.
                  2. kubectl describe pod - read Events and scheduling/probe/image errors.
                  3. kubectl logs - inspect application/container output.
                  4. Check Deployment/ReplicaSet desired vs available replicas.
                  5. Check Service selectors and Endpoints/EndpointSlices.
                  6. Check Ingress / AWS Load Balancer Controller and target health.
                  7. Check security groups, subnets, route tables, DNS, and network policies.
                  8. For AWS API failures, verify Pod Identity/IRSA and IAM permissions.
                  9. For Pending Pods, inspect requests/limits, taints, affinity, node capacity, and autoscaler events.
24. EKS Security Areas
                  * Use private worker subnets where possible and carefully control the Kubernetes API endpoint.
                  * Restrict security groups and Kubernetes network policies.
                  * Use least-privilege IAM roles for Pods.
                  * Use RBAC/access entries for human/CI access.
                  * Keep cluster version, nodes, add-ons, and images patched.
                  * Use image scanning and trusted registries such as ECR.
                  * Protect Secrets and consider KMS/external secret-management solutions.
                  * Use Pod Security Standards/admission controls appropriate to your organization.
                  * Audit using Kubernetes/EKS control-plane logs, CloudTrail, and runtime/security tooling.
25. EKS vs ECS
Area
	EKS
	ECS
	Orchestrator
	Kubernetes
	AWS-native ECS scheduler/orchestrator
	Portability/ecosystem
	Large Kubernetes ecosystem; portable APIs
	Deep AWS simplicity/integration
	Operational complexity
	Higher; Kubernetes concepts/control
	Usually simpler
	Core unit
	Pod
	Task
	Desired app state
	Deployment/StatefulSet/etc.
	ECS Service
	Networking/load balancing
	Kubernetes + AWS controllers
	ECS native AWS integrations
	Best fit
	Teams needing Kubernetes ecosystem/standards
	Teams wanting simpler AWS-native containers
	Interview answer
Choose EKS when Kubernetes itself is a requirement or ecosystem advantage. Choose ECS when you want AWS-native container orchestration with less Kubernetes operational complexity.
	26. EKS vs Fargate - Do Not Confuse the Terms
EKS is the Kubernetes control/orchestration service. Fargate is one possible compute option for running Pods.
                  * EKS + managed node groups = Kubernetes Pods on EC2 worker nodes.
                  * EKS + Fargate = Kubernetes Pods on serverless Fargate compute.
                  * EKS Auto Mode = managed EKS model that automates much of cluster infrastructure.
Shortcut
EKS tells you how containers are orchestrated. Fargate tells you who manages the underlying compute.
	27. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Pods are Pending after a traffic spike.
Check resource requests, node capacity, taints/affinity, subnet IPs, then verify Karpenter/Cluster Autoscaler/Auto Mode can add appropriate nodes.
Scenario: One Pod needs S3 but other Pods must not have S3 access.
Use a dedicated Kubernetes service account mapped to a least-privilege role with EKS Pod Identity or IRSA.
Scenario: Expose /api and /admin through one ALB.
Use Ingress host/path rules and AWS Load Balancer Controller.
Scenario: Need to run a logging agent on every EC2 worker node.
Use a DaemonSet. Fargate does not support DaemonSets.
Scenario: Application Pods are running but ALB says unhealthy.
Check readiness/health path, Service selector/ports, Ingress annotations, target type, security groups and application listener.
Scenario: Need cheaper fault-tolerant worker compute.
Use Spot managed node groups or appropriate Karpenter/Auto Mode capacity strategy; design Pods to tolerate interruption.
Scenario: Developer can authenticate to AWS but kubectl is forbidden.
Check EKS access entry/RBAC permissions; AWS authentication alone does not imply Kubernetes authorization.
28. Must-Know EKS Interview Questions
                  * What is EKS?
                  * What does AWS manage in EKS?
                  * Control plane vs data plane?
                  * Pod vs Deployment vs Service?
                  * What is Ingress?
                  * ClusterIP vs NodePort vs LoadBalancer?
                  * EKS managed node group vs self-managed nodes?
                  * EKS on Fargate vs EC2 nodes?
                  * What is EKS Auto Mode?
                  * How does Pod networking work with VPC CNI?
                  * What does AWS Load Balancer Controller do?
                  * Readiness vs liveness probe?
                  * ConfigMap vs Secret?
                  * How should a Pod access S3 securely?
                  * EKS Pod Identity vs IRSA?
                  * How do developers get kubectl access?
                  * What are EKS access entries?
                  * What is Kubernetes RBAC?
                  * HPA vs Cluster Autoscaler vs Karpenter?
                  * What are taints and tolerations?
                  * How do EBS/EFS integrate with EKS?
                  * Why is a Pod stuck in Pending?
                  * CrashLoopBackOff vs ImagePullBackOff?
                  * EKS vs ECS?
29. Final EKS Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	EKS
	Managed Kubernetes on AWS
	Control Plane
	API server + scheduler/controllers + etcd; AWS-managed
	Data Plane
	Worker compute that runs Pods
	Pod
	Smallest Kubernetes workload unit
	Deployment
	Manages stateless Pod replicas and updates
	Service
	Stable networking for a set of Pods
	Ingress
	HTTP/HTTPS routing to Services
	Managed Node Group

	Fargate
	Serverless compute for eligible Pods
	EKS Auto Mode
	AWS-managed Kubernetes infrastructure automation
	VPC CNI
	AWS Pod networking integration
	AWS Load Balancer Controller
	Creates/manages ALB/NLB from Kubernetes resources
	EKS Pod Identity

If you are short on time, revise these first
EKS basics -> control plane vs worker nodes -> Pod/Deployment/Service/Ingress -> managed node groups vs Fargate -> VPC CNI -> ALB/NLB integration -> Pod Identity -> access entries/RBAC -> HPA vs node autoscaling -> probes -> EKS vs ECS.
	30. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * EKS runs the managed control plane across multiple Availability Zones for high availability.
                  * EKS access entries are recommended for IAM-principal access; the legacy aws-auth ConfigMap approach is deprecated.
                  * AWS recommends EKS Pod Identity for Pod-to-AWS IAM permissions when possible; IRSA remains supported.
                  * EKS Auto Mode can manage compute autoscaling, networking, load balancing, storage, DNS, and other infrastructure functions.
                  * AWS Load Balancer Controller is the recommended traditional controller for reconciling Kubernetes Service/Ingress resources with AWS load balancers.
                  * Managed node groups automate EC2 node lifecycle and draining; Fargate removes EC2 node management but has Kubernetes feature limitations.
