AWS ElastiCache - Interview Study Notes
AWS ElastiCache - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand ElastiCache from first principles: why caching helps, cache-aside, Valkey/Redis OSS vs Memcached, Serverless vs node-based, replication, Multi-AZ, sharding, TTL/eviction, cache consistency, security, monitoring, and common interview scenarios.
	1. What is Amazon ElastiCache?
Amazon ElastiCache is AWS's fully managed in-memory cache/data-store service. It makes it easier to run Valkey, Redis OSS, or Memcached without managing the underlying cache servers yourself.
                  * Stores frequently accessed data in memory for very low latency.
                  * Reduces repeated reads to RDS, DynamoDB, external APIs, or expensive computations.
                  * Supports Serverless and node-based deployment models.
                  * AWS handles much of provisioning, node replacement, monitoring integration, and software maintenance.
                  * Common uses: sessions, database query caching, API results, counters, leaderboards, rate limits, temporary state.
Interview one-liner
ElastiCache is a managed in-memory caching service that reduces latency and backend load by keeping hot data close to the application.
	2. Why Use a Cache?
                  * Reduce database load.
                  * Return hot data faster.
                  * Absorb read spikes.
                  * Avoid repeating expensive computations.
                  * Store short-lived session or token state.
                  * Implement counters, rate limits, sorted rankings, locks, and temporary coordination.
Important
A cache is normally an optimization layer. If cache loss would permanently lose critical business data, either keep a durable system of record elsewhere or deliberately use newer durability features with a design that understands their tradeoffs.
	3. Cache-Aside / Lazy Loading
  

Cache-aside is the most common application-managed caching pattern.
                  * Application first reads the cache.
                  * If key exists, return it: cache hit.
                  * If key does not exist, read the database: cache miss.
                  * Put the result in the cache, usually with a TTL.
                  * Return the result to the client.
Pseudo-flow
GET cache:user:123 -> miss -> SELECT user 123 from RDS -> SET cache:user:123 EX 300 -> return user.
	4. Cache Hit vs Cache Miss
Term
	Meaning
	Effect
	Cache hit
	Requested key exists and is usable
	Fast response; backend avoided
	Cache miss
	Key absent/expired/evicted
	Read origin/DB and repopulate
	Hit ratio
	Hits / total lookups
	Higher is usually better for cacheable workloads
	5. Valkey / Redis OSS vs Memcached
  

Valkey/Redis OSS are feature-rich; Memcached is a simpler distributed key-value cache.
Area
	Valkey / Redis OSS
	Memcached
	Data model
	Rich data structures
	Simple key-value
	Replication
	Yes
	No replica/failover model
	Multi-AZ failover
	Yes with replicas
	No equivalent primary/replica HA model
	Persistence/backups
	Snapshots and newer durability options
	No durable persistence model
	Pub/Sub
	Yes
	No
	Transactions/scripts
	Supported engine features
	No equivalent rich feature set
	Horizontal scale
	Cluster mode/shards
	Add nodes and client key distribution
	Best fit
	Most modern feature-rich caching
	Very simple high-throughput ephemeral cache
	6. What is Valkey?
Valkey is an open-source in-memory data store compatible with the Redis OSS API ecosystem. AWS now actively supports Valkey as a first-class ElastiCache engine.
                  * Supports common Redis-style commands and data structures.
                  * ElastiCache currently supports Valkey Serverless and node-based clusters.
                  * Newer Valkey releases receive features beyond Redis OSS 7.1 in ElastiCache.
Interview advice
If the interviewer says 'Redis', explain the Redis-style concepts normally. If discussing current AWS options, mention that ElastiCache supports Valkey, Redis OSS, and Memcached.
	7. Serverless vs Node-Based
  

Serverless abstracts cluster capacity; node-based gives more topology control.
Area
	Serverless
	Node-Based
	Capacity planning
	Automatic
	You choose node/shard/replica design
	Scaling
	Automatic vertical + horizontal
	You manage scaling configuration
	Endpoint
	Simple endpoint experience
	Primary/configuration/reader endpoints depending topology
	Availability
	Automatic multi-AZ design
	You configure replicas/Multi-AZ
	Pricing
	Data stored + ECPU usage
	Per node-hour plus related usage
	Global Datastore
	No
	Yes for supported Valkey/Redis OSS
	Durability
	No
	Valkey 9.0+ supported configurations
	When to choose
Serverless: new/unpredictable workloads and minimal operational planning. Node-based: predictable workload, topology control, Global Datastore, specific node types, or advanced durability/data-tiering requirements.
	8. ElastiCache Endpoints
                  * Cluster mode disabled Valkey/Redis OSS commonly exposes a primary endpoint for writes and reader endpoint for replicas.
                  * Cluster mode enabled uses a configuration endpoint so cluster-aware clients discover shards/nodes.
                  * Serverless provides a simple endpoint experience.
                  * Do not hardcode individual node IP addresses.
Interview point
Use the endpoint designed for the topology. A cluster-aware client is required for sharded/cluster-mode deployments.
	9. Replication and Read Replicas
  

Valkey/Redis OSS can maintain replicas for read scale and failover.
                  * Writes go to the primary node of a shard.
                  * Replicas receive updates asynchronously in normal non-durable configurations.
                  * Reads can be directed to replicas to scale read-heavy workloads.
                  * Replica reads can be eventually consistent.
                  * Replication lag means a recent write might not immediately appear on a replica.
Read-after-write
If a request must immediately see its own latest write, read from the primary rather than an eventually consistent replica.
	10. Multi-AZ and Automatic Failover
                  * Multi-AZ places replicas in different Availability Zones and enables automatic failover.
                  * If the primary fails, ElastiCache promotes the replica with the least replication lag.
                  * Writes typically resume within seconds.
                  * ElastiCache then provisions replacement replica capacity.
                  * For standard asynchronous replication, a small amount of recent data may be lost during failover.
High availability rule
For node-based Valkey/Redis OSS production workloads, replicas in another AZ + Multi-AZ are the normal HA pattern.
	11. Cluster Mode Disabled vs Enabled
Area
	Cluster Mode Disabled
	Cluster Mode Enabled
	Shards
	1 shard
	Multiple shards possible
	Write primary
	One primary
	One primary per shard
	Horizontal data scaling
	Limited
	Yes
	Client awareness
	Normal primary/reader endpoint model
	Cluster-aware client required
	Key placement
	Single shard
	Hash slots distributed across shards
	Best fit
	Smaller/simple workloads
	Larger throughput/memory and horizontal scaling
	12. Sharding / Cluster Mode
  

Shards divide key space so memory and throughput can scale horizontally.
                  * Valkey/Redis OSS cluster mode divides the key space into hash slots.
                  * Each shard owns a subset of slots.
                  * Each shard has one primary and can have replicas.
                  * Adding/removing shards redistributes slots/data.
                  * A cluster-aware client follows redirections/configuration to the correct shard.
Hot key warning
Sharding distributes many keys, but one extremely hot key still belongs to one slot/shard and can become a bottleneck.
	13. TTL / Expiration
  

TTL controls data lifetime; eviction handles memory pressure.
                  * TTL automatically expires a key after a configured time.
                  * Use TTL for sessions, temporary API results, tokens, product data, and other data that naturally becomes stale.
                  * TTL prevents old cache entries from surviving forever.
                  * Pick TTL based on acceptable staleness and backend load.
Tradeoff
Long TTL = higher hit ratio but potentially staler data. Short TTL = fresher data but more backend/database reads.
	14. Eviction Policies
When the cache reaches its memory limit, the engine may evict keys according to its maxmemory policy.
                  * volatile-lru: evict least-recently-used keys that have TTLs.
                  * allkeys-lru: consider all keys for LRU eviction.
                  * volatile-ttl: favor keys that expire sooner.
                  * noeviction: reject writes instead of evicting keys when memory is exhausted.
                  * Exact supported/default behavior depends on engine/deployment configuration.
Important
Eviction is not a replacement for correct TTL and capacity planning. If evictions suddenly rise, your working set may no longer fit in memory.
	15. Cache Invalidation Strategies
                  * TTL expiration: simplest; accept bounded staleness.
                  * Delete-on-write: application updates DB then deletes related cache key.
                  * Write-through: application writes cache and DB as part of the update flow.
                  * Versioned keys: include entity/version in key when appropriate.
                  * Event-driven invalidation: publish DB/domain changes and invalidate/update cache.
Common interview answer
For a normal cache-aside design, after a successful DB update I usually delete the cache key rather than trying to perfectly update every cached representation.
	16. ElastiCache + RDS
  

RDS remains durable source of truth; ElastiCache serves hot reads.
                  * Cache frequent SELECT results.
                  * Cache reference data that changes infrequently.
                  * Store session state outside stateless app servers.
                  * Reduce repeated DB work during traffic spikes.
                  * Do not assume ElastiCache automatically tracks RDS changes.
Key point
ElastiCache and RDS are separate systems. Your application owns cache consistency.
	17. Cache Stampede / Thundering Herd
A cache stampede happens when a popular key expires and many requests simultaneously miss the cache and hit the backend.
                  * Use jitter/randomized TTLs so many keys do not expire at exactly the same time.
                  * Use request coalescing/single-flight locks so only one worker reloads a hot key.
                  * Refresh important entries before expiry.
                  * Use stale-while-revalidate style logic where appropriate.
                  * Protect the origin with rate limits/back-pressure.
Interview example
10,000 users request the same product at 10:00:00, and its cache key expires at 10:00:00. Without protection, all 10,000 requests may hit RDS.
	18. Hot Keys
                  * A hot key receives extremely high request volume compared with other keys.
                  * One hot key can overload a shard even when the overall cluster has spare capacity.
                  * Use local/application caching for ultra-hot immutable data where appropriate.
                  * Split/replicate workload when semantics permit.
                  * Read from replicas for read-heavy hot data.
                  * Inspect per-shard/node metrics and command/key patterns.
19. Sessions
                  * ElastiCache is commonly used as a distributed session store.
                  * All application instances can read the same session key.
                  * TTL naturally removes expired sessions.
                  * Do not keep critical long-term user data only in a normal ephemeral cache.
ASG connection
With EC2/ECS Auto Scaling, storing session only in one instance's memory breaks when traffic moves to another instance. A shared cache avoids that coupling.
	20. Data Structures in Valkey / Redis OSS
Type
	Typical Use
	String
	Simple cached value, counter, token
	Hash
	User/session/object fields
	List
	Queues/deques/simple ordered collections
	Set
	Unique membership
	Sorted Set
	Leaderboard/ranking/time-ordered score
	Streams
	Event/log-style stream processing
	Pub/Sub
	Ephemeral real-time message broadcasting
	21. Security
  

ElastiCache should normally be reachable only from trusted application networks.
                  * Run ElastiCache inside a VPC; it is not designed as a public internet cache endpoint.
                  * Use security groups to allow only trusted application SGs/subnets.
                  * Enable in-transit encryption (TLS) for supported engines/configurations.
                  * Enable encryption at rest where appropriate.
                  * Valkey/Redis OSS support RBAC and password/IAM authentication depending on version/configuration.
                  * Use IAM for AWS control-plane permissions separately from cache data-plane permissions.
Serverless
Serverless Valkey/Redis OSS uses RBAC for access control, and Serverless connections require TLS.
	22. IAM Authentication and RBAC
                  * For supported Valkey/Redis OSS versions, IAM authentication uses a short-lived SigV4-generated token.
                  * IAM authentication requires TLS.
                  * The IAM principal needs elasticache:Connect permission to the cache/user.
                  * RBAC controls which commands and key patterns a cache user can access.
                  * IAM authentication and RBAC solve data-plane cache access; normal IAM policies still govern AWS API/console management actions.
Interview distinction
IAM role permission to manage ElastiCache resources is not automatically the same thing as permission to run GET/SET inside the cache.
	23. Backups and Snapshots
                  * Valkey/Redis OSS node-based clusters support backups/snapshots.
                  * Snapshots can be used for restore/migration scenarios.
                  * Serverless Valkey/Redis OSS can restore from supported backups and node-based backups can be moved between deployment options.
                  * Memcached does not provide the same persistence/backup model.
Cache vs database
Snapshots improve recoverability, but traditional cache designs should still tolerate cache loss and rebuild from the system of record.
	24. Global Datastore
                  * Global Datastore is available for node-based Valkey/Redis OSS clusters.
                  * One primary Region accepts writes.
                  * Secondary Regions replicate asynchronously and serve reads.
                  * Useful for global low-latency reads and disaster recovery.
                  * A secondary Region can be promoted during regional failure/recovery planning.
Important
Global Datastore is not multi-Region active-active writes. The normal model is single-Region writes with cross-Region read replicas.
	25. ElastiCache Serverless
                  * Create a cache without choosing node sizes or shard counts.
                  * Continuously scales memory, compute, and network bandwidth.
                  * Stores data redundantly across multiple Availability Zones.
                  * Supports Valkey, Redis OSS, and Memcached.
                  * Valkey/Redis OSS Serverless operates in cluster mode enabled.
                  * Clients must support TLS; cluster-mode-capable clients are required for Valkey/Redis OSS Serverless.
                  * Billing is based on stored data and ElastiCache Processing Units (ECPUs).
When it shines
Great when traffic is new, unpredictable, or you want the simplest operational model.
	26. Valkey 9.0+ Durability - Advanced / Current Topic
Newer node-based ElastiCache for Valkey can optionally persist writes to a Multi-AZ transactional log, allowing cache-like latency while improving durability.
                  * Supported for Valkey 9.0+ in supported node-based cluster configurations.
                  * Synchronous durability: acknowledged writes are persisted before returning; designed for zero acknowledged-write loss during failures.
                  * Asynchronous durability: keeps microsecond-style write latency but up to roughly 10 seconds of acknowledged writes can be at risk during failure.
                  * Replicas recover from the transactional log rather than depending entirely on primary-to-replica streaming.
                  * Durability is not supported for ElastiCache Serverless.
Interview priority
Know this exists, but for most full-stack interviews still explain ElastiCache primarily as a cache. Durable Valkey is an advanced/current capability.
	27. Monitoring
Metric / Signal
	What It Tells You
	CacheHits / CacheMisses
	Whether caching is effective
	Evictions
	Memory pressure / working set too large
	CurrConnections
	Client connection pressure
	EngineCPUUtilization / CPUUtilization
	Engine/host CPU load
	DatabaseMemoryUsagePercentage
	Memory usage pressure for Valkey/Redis
	ReplicationLag
	Replica staleness
	NetworkBytesIn/Out
	Network load
	FreeableMemory
	Host memory headroom on node-based clusters
	First metrics to remember
Cache hit ratio, evictions, memory usage, CPU, connections, and replica lag.
	28. ElastiCache vs DAX
Area
	ElastiCache
	DAX
	Backend
	Any application/data source
	DynamoDB-specific
	API
	Valkey/Redis/Memcached client APIs
	DynamoDB-compatible client
	Data structures
	Rich with Valkey/Redis
	DynamoDB item cache
	Use
	General caching/sessions/counters/rankings
	Accelerate DynamoDB reads
	29. ElastiCache vs DynamoDB
Area
	ElastiCache
	DynamoDB
	Primary role
	In-memory cache / fast data structure store
	Durable NoSQL database
	Latency
	Microsecond/sub-millisecond style cache access
	Single-digit millisecond typical database access
	Durability
	Traditional cache semantics; optional advanced Valkey durability
	Durable system of record
	Query model
	Key/engine commands
	Table/item/key/index APIs
	Typical use
	Hot data, sessions, rate limits
	Persistent application data
	30. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: RDS CPU is high because the same product rows are read thousands of times.
Use cache-aside: cache hot product data with a TTL and invalidate/delete when product data changes.
Scenario: User session should survive moving between EC2 instances.
Store session in shared ElastiCache instead of local process memory.
Scenario: Need leaderboard rankings.
Valkey/Redis sorted sets are a natural fit.
Scenario: Cache contains stale profile data after update.
Delete/update the cache entry after the successful database write and use TTL as a safety net.
Scenario: Thousands of requests hit RDS when one popular key expires.
This is a cache stampede; use TTL jitter, single-flight locking, prewarming, or stale-while-revalidate patterns.
Scenario: Need strict HA for node-based Valkey/Redis.
Use replicas in different AZs, Multi-AZ, and automatic failover.
Scenario: Read workload is too high on primary.
Use replicas for read scaling if eventual consistency is acceptable.
Scenario: Cache is constantly evicting keys.
Working set may not fit; inspect memory, TTLs, eviction policy, node size/shards, and unnecessary cached data.
Scenario: Need global low-latency reads with one write Region.
Use node-based Global Datastore for supported Valkey/Redis OSS.
Scenario: Traffic is unpredictable and team does not want shard/node planning.
Use ElastiCache Serverless.
31. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Cannot connect: VPC/subnet path -> security groups -> endpoint/port -> TLS -> RBAC/IAM/password -> DNS.
2.  High latency: CPU -> connections -> network -> hot key -> slow commands -> client pooling -> cross-AZ/network path.
3.  Low hit ratio: TTL too short -> cache key design -> data not reused -> cache never populated -> frequent invalidation.
4.  High evictions: memory usage -> working set -> TTL -> eviction policy -> node/shard capacity.
5.  Replica stale: replication lag -> write volume -> replica capacity -> read from primary when necessary.
6.  Failover errors: client retry/reconnect -> endpoint discovery -> Multi-AZ -> library topology awareness.
7.  Cluster-mode MOVED/ASK errors: client is not cluster-aware or topology refresh is broken.
8.  Connection spikes: reuse long-lived connections, use pooling, inspect client limits and deployment architecture.
32. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is Amazon ElastiCache?
Answer: A fully managed AWS in-memory cache/data-store service supporting Valkey, Redis OSS, and Memcached. It reduces latency and backend load by serving hot data from memory.
2. Why use ElastiCache in front of RDS?
Answer: To avoid repeatedly running the same expensive queries. The application reads cache first and falls back to RDS on a miss, reducing database load and latency.
3. What is cache-aside?
Answer: The application checks cache first; on a miss it reads the database, stores the result in cache with a TTL, and returns it.
4. What is a cache hit?
Answer: The requested value already exists in cache and can be returned without calling the backend.
5. What is a cache miss?
Answer: The requested value is not usable/present, so the application must fetch it from the system of record and usually repopulate the cache.
6. Valkey/Redis OSS vs Memcached?
Answer: Valkey/Redis provide rich data structures, replication, Multi-AZ failover, snapshots, Pub/Sub and other features. Memcached is a simpler distributed key-value cache without the same replication/persistence model.
7. What is Valkey?
Answer: An open-source Redis-compatible in-memory data store. ElastiCache supports Valkey as a first-class engine alongside Redis OSS and Memcached.
8. Serverless vs node-based ElastiCache?
Answer: Serverless automatically handles capacity and scaling. Node-based lets you choose node types, shards, replicas, AZ placement, and supports features such as Global Datastore and newer Valkey durability.
9. What is a primary node?
Answer: The writable node for a Valkey/Redis shard. Replicas copy its data and can serve reads.
10. What is a read replica?
Answer: A replica that asynchronously follows the primary and can serve read traffic and act as a failover target.
11. Are replica reads strongly consistent?
Answer: No. Replica reads are typically eventually consistent because replication can lag behind the primary.
12. What is Multi-AZ in ElastiCache?
Answer: A high-availability configuration placing replicas in other Availability Zones and enabling automatic failover.
13. What happens when the primary fails?
Answer: With Multi-AZ/automatic failover, ElastiCache promotes the replica with the least lag to primary and provisions replacement replica capacity.
14. Can data be lost during failover?
Answer: With normal asynchronous replication, a small amount of recent data can be lost. Newer durable Valkey configurations can improve this guarantee.
15. What is cluster mode?
Answer: A Valkey/Redis sharding mode where the key space is split across multiple shards using hash slots.
16. Cluster mode disabled vs enabled?
Answer: Disabled uses one shard. Enabled supports multiple shards for horizontal memory and throughput scaling and requires a cluster-aware client.
17. What is a shard?
Answer: A subset of the key space, normally containing one primary and optional replicas.
18. What is TTL?
Answer: An expiration time attached to a key. After TTL expires, the key is removed and the next request may reload it from the backend.
19. TTL vs eviction?
Answer: TTL is intentional expiration based on age. Eviction is removal caused by memory pressure according to the eviction policy.
20. What is LRU eviction?
Answer: Least Recently Used style eviction prefers removing keys that have not been accessed recently.
21. What is a cache stampede?
Answer: Many requests simultaneously miss the same popular key and all hit the backend, often just after the key expires.
22. How do you prevent cache stampede?
Answer: Use TTL jitter, locking/single-flight, prewarming, refresh-ahead, stale-while-revalidate, and backend protection.
23. What is a hot key?
Answer: A key that receives disproportionately high traffic and can overload one shard/node even if overall cluster capacity is available.
24. How do you keep RDS and ElastiCache consistent?
Answer: Your application manages it: delete/update cache after DB writes, use TTLs, versioned keys, or event-driven invalidation.
25. Does ElastiCache automatically sync with RDS?
Answer: No. ElastiCache and RDS are independent services; application logic owns cache population and invalidation.
26. What are common Valkey/Redis data structures?
Answer: Strings, hashes, lists, sets, sorted sets, streams, and Pub/Sub channels.
27. What is a common session-store pattern?
Answer: Store session ID as key and session data as value with a TTL, so all stateless application instances can access the same session.
28. How do you secure ElastiCache?
Answer: Keep it private in a VPC, restrict security groups, enable TLS and at-rest encryption where supported, and use RBAC/password/IAM authentication for Valkey/Redis.
29. What is IAM authentication for ElastiCache?
Answer: For supported Valkey/Redis versions, clients use short-lived SigV4-generated IAM tokens to authenticate, with elasticache:Connect permissions and TLS.
30. What is Global Datastore?
Answer: A node-based Valkey/Redis feature that asynchronously replicates a primary cluster to secondary clusters in other Regions for local reads and disaster recovery.
31. Is Global Datastore active-active writes?
Answer: No. Normally the primary Region accepts writes and secondary Regions are read-only until promoted.
32. What metrics should you monitor?
Answer: Cache hits/misses, evictions, memory usage, CPU, connections, replication lag, and network traffic.
33. What is ElastiCache Serverless?
Answer: A deployment option where AWS automatically manages scaling and capacity. It supports Valkey, Redis OSS, and Memcached and provides a simple endpoint experience.
34. What is Valkey durability in ElastiCache?
Answer: For supported Valkey 9.0+ node-based clusters, writes can be persisted to a Multi-AZ transactional log using synchronous or asynchronous durability modes.
35. ElastiCache vs DAX?
Answer: ElastiCache is a general-purpose in-memory cache/data structure service. DAX is specifically a DynamoDB-compatible in-memory accelerator.
33. Final ElastiCache Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	ElastiCache
	Managed in-memory cache/data store
	Valkey / Redis OSS
	Rich data structures + replication/HA
	Memcached
	Simple distributed key-value cache
	Cache-Aside
	Read cache -> miss -> DB -> populate cache
	Hit
	Served from cache
	Miss
	Fetch backend and populate
	TTL
	Intentional key expiration
	Eviction
	Memory-pressure key removal
	Primary
	Writable Valkey/Redis node
	Replica
	Read/failover copy
	Multi-AZ
	Cross-AZ replicas + automatic failover
	Cluster Mode
	Sharded Valkey/Redis
	Shard
	Subset of key space
	Hot Key
	One key gets too much traffic
	Stampede
	Many simultaneous misses hit backend
	Serverless
	Automatic capacity/scaling
	Node-Based
	Fine-grained node/shard control
	RBAC
	Fine-grained Valkey/Redis command/key access
	IAM Auth
	Short-lived IAM token cache authentication
	Global Datastore
	Cross-Region read replicas / DR
	Durability
	Valkey 9.0+ transactional log option
	Hit Ratio
	Hits / lookups
	Evictions Metric
	Signals memory pressure
	

If you are short on time, revise these first
What ElastiCache is -> cache-aside -> Valkey/Redis vs Memcached -> Serverless vs node-based -> primary/replica -> Multi-AZ -> cluster mode/shards -> TTL vs eviction -> invalidation -> cache stampede -> security -> monitoring -> interview Q&A.
	34. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * ElastiCache currently supports Valkey, Redis OSS, and Memcached.
                  * Serverless supports all three engines, automatically scales capacity, uses multi-AZ redundancy, and requires TLS.
                  * Cluster mode enabled is the recommended Valkey/Redis scaling model for larger workloads; Serverless Valkey/Redis operates in cluster mode enabled.
                  * Multi-AZ is the recommended high-availability configuration for node-based Valkey/Redis.
                  * Global Datastore is currently node-based only and uses cross-Region asynchronous replication.
                  * IAM authentication is available for supported Valkey/Redis versions and requires TLS.
                  * Valkey 9.0+ node-based clusters can optionally use a Multi-AZ transactional log for synchronous or asynchronous durability.
35. AWS Documentation References
AWS docs used for current details: What is Amazon ElastiCache; deployment options; Valkey/Redis replication and Multi-AZ; cluster mode/sharding; security, RBAC and IAM authentication; Global Datastore; Valkey durability; CloudWatch monitoring.


AWS CloudWatch - Interview Study Notes
AWS CloudWatch - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand CloudWatch from first principles: metrics, namespaces/dimensions, alarms, logs, Logs Insights, dashboards, EC2 monitoring, CloudWatch Agent, custom metrics, metric math, anomaly detection, composite alarms, Container Insights, Application Signals, Synthetics/RUM, and practical troubleshooting.
	1. What is Amazon CloudWatch?
Amazon CloudWatch is AWS's monitoring and observability service. It collects metrics, logs, events, traces/application signals, and other telemetry so you can visualize system health, investigate problems, and automate responses.
                  * Metrics: numeric time-series such as CPU utilization or request count.
                  * Logs: application/system/service log events.
                  * Alarms: evaluate metrics/expressions and react to threshold/state changes.
                  * Dashboards: visualize metrics, alarms, logs, and operational views.
                  * Application observability: Container Insights, Application Signals, Synthetics, RUM, and integrations with tracing.
  

CloudWatch turns telemetry into visibility, alerts, and operational actions.
2. Metrics vs Logs vs Alarms vs Dashboards
Feature
	What It Is
	Example
	Metric
	Numeric value over time
	EC2 CPUUtilization = 72%
	Log
	Timestamped event/record
	ERROR payment failed
	Alarm
	Rule evaluating metric/expression
	CPU > 80% for 5 minutes
	Dashboard
	Visualization of telemetry
	CPU + 5xx + latency graphs
	Interview shortcut
Metric tells you HOW MUCH; log often tells you WHAT HAPPENED; alarm tells you WHEN TO REACT; dashboard gives you the overview.
	3. CloudWatch Metrics
                  * Many AWS services publish metrics automatically.
                  * Metrics are time-ordered data points.
                  * A metric has a namespace, name, dimensions, timestamps, values, and units/statistics.
                  * AWS service metrics are standard-resolution by default.
                  * Custom metrics can be standard resolution or high resolution.
  

Namespace + metric name + dimensions define a CloudWatch time series.
4. Namespaces and Dimensions
                  * Namespace groups related metrics, for example AWS/EC2, AWS/Lambda, AWS/ApplicationELB.
                  * Custom metrics should use your own namespace such as MyCompany/Checkout.
                  * Dimensions are name/value pairs that identify a specific resource or perspective.
                  * Example: CPUUtilization with InstanceId=i-123.
                  * Different dimension combinations create different metric time series.
Common interview issue
Do not create an unbounded/high-cardinality dimension such as RequestId for every request. That can create huge numbers of custom metrics and cost.
	5. Statistics and Periods
Concept
	Meaning
	Period
	Time window used to aggregate metric points, e.g. 60 seconds.
	Average
	Mean value during the period.
	Minimum / Maximum
	Lowest/highest value during the period.
	Sum
	Total of values during the period.
	SampleCount
	Number of data points included.
	Percentiles
	p90/p95/p99-style distribution statistics where supported.
	Latency tip
For user-facing latency, average alone can hide slow requests. p95/p99 often reveal tail latency more clearly.
	6. Standard vs High-Resolution Custom Metrics
                  * Standard-resolution custom metrics have 1-minute granularity.
                  * High-resolution custom metrics can use 1-second granularity.
                  * High-resolution alarms can use 10-second or 30-second periods.
                  * Higher-resolution data and alarms can cost more.
                  * Use high resolution only when sub-minute reaction materially matters.
Example
A trading/real-time system may need sub-minute signals; a nightly batch job usually does not.
	7. EC2 Basic vs Detailed Monitoring
  

EC2 built-in metrics and CloudWatch Agent metrics solve different needs.
Mode
	Frequency
	Typical Cost
	Basic monitoring
	Most EC2 metrics at 5-minute periods
	Included
	Detailed monitoring
	EC2 metrics at 1-minute periods
	Additional CloudWatch metric charges
	                  * EC2 status-check metrics are available at 1-minute periods even with basic monitoring.
                  * CPUUtilization and network metrics are published automatically.
                  * Memory utilization and filesystem usage are not standard EC2 metrics.
Very common interview question
To monitor EC2 memory or disk filesystem usage, install/configure the CloudWatch Agent or another telemetry agent.
	8. CloudWatch Agent
                  * Collect OS-level metrics such as memory, swap, disk/filesystem, process, StatsD, and collectd data.
                  * Collect application/system log files and send them to CloudWatch Logs.
                  * Can run on EC2 and on-premises servers.
                  * Requires IAM permissions to publish metrics/logs.
                  * Use configuration files/Systems Manager tooling to manage agent setup at scale.
Spring Boot example
Use the agent for host memory/disk and log files; use application metrics/OpenTelemetry or PutMetricData/EMF for business/application metrics.
	9. Custom Metrics
                  * Publish with PutMetricData.
                  * Or emit structured logs using Embedded Metric Format (EMF), which CloudWatch can extract into metrics.
                  * Examples: OrdersCreated, CheckoutFailures, QueueBacklogPerWorker, ActiveUsers.
                  * Choose meaningful dimensions such as Environment, Service, Region, or API name.
                  * Avoid dimensions with extremely high cardinality.
Business monitoring
Infrastructure can look healthy while the business is broken. A custom metric such as SuccessfulPayments can detect failures that CPU graphs cannot.
	10. CloudWatch Alarms
  

Alarms evaluate a metric/expression over periods and move among OK, ALARM, and INSUFFICIENT_DATA.
                  * Set threshold, comparison operator, period, evaluation periods, and datapoints-to-alarm.
                  * Choose how missing data should be treated.
                  * Alarms generally act on state changes, not continuously just because they remain in ALARM.
                  * Common actions include SNS notifications, EC2 actions, and Auto Scaling policies.
                  * Alarm state changes are available as EventBridge events.
Example
Alarm if 3 of the last 5 one-minute CPU datapoints exceed 80%. This is more robust than alarming on one brief spike.
	11. Alarm States
State
	Meaning
	OK
	Metric/expression is not breaching the configured alarm condition.
	ALARM
	Configured threshold/evaluation condition is breaching.
	INSUFFICIENT_DATA
	Not enough data to determine OK vs ALARM.
	12. Missing Data
When expected metric data is missing, an alarm can be configured to treat missing values as:
                  * breaching
                  * notBreaching
                  * ignore
                  * missing
Why it matters
For a 'heartbeat' metric, missing data may mean failure and should be breaching. For a metric that is emitted only when errors happen, missing may mean there are no errors.
	13. Composite Alarms
  

Composite alarms combine other alarm states and reduce duplicate/noisy notifications.
                  * A composite alarm evaluates Boolean logic over child alarm states.
                  * Example: CPUHigh AND ErrorRateHigh.
                  * Can suppress actions until multiple related symptoms occur.
                  * Useful for one application-level incident notification instead of 20 component alerts.
                  * Composite alarms do not directly monitor raw metrics.
Noise reduction
Use individual metric alarms for diagnosis and a composite alarm for the page/ticket that wakes someone up.
	14. Metric Math
                  * CloudWatch can calculate new time series from existing metrics.
                  * Examples: error percentage = 5xx / request count * 100.
                  * Backlog per instance = SQS visible messages / ASG instance count.
                  * Can use metric-math expressions in dashboards and alarms.
                  * Lets you alarm on meaningful ratios rather than raw counts.
System-design example
Scale worker fleet using queue backlog per worker instead of total queue depth.
	15. Anomaly Detection
                  * CloudWatch builds an expected-value band from historical metric behavior.
                  * Can account for trends, seasonality, and changing patterns.
                  * Alarm when actual values move outside the expected band.
                  * Useful when a fixed threshold such as 'CPU > 80%' is too simplistic.
                  * Anomaly detection can also work on supported metric-math expressions.
Example
Traffic normally drops every night and rises every morning. Anomaly detection learns that pattern instead of alerting on every normal daily change.
	16. CloudWatch Logs Concepts
  

A log group contains one or more log streams.
Term
	Meaning
	Log event
	Timestamp + message/data.
	Log stream
	Sequence of events from one source, such as one instance/container.
	Log group
	Collection of related streams sharing retention/access settings.
	Retention
	How long log events are kept.
	                  * By default, CloudWatch Logs retains log data indefinitely until you configure a retention period.
                  * Set explicit retention in production to control compliance and cost.
                  * Log groups can also use deletion protection for critical logs.
17. CloudWatch Logs Insights
Logs Insights is the interactive query engine for CloudWatch Logs.
                  * Filter by fields, text, JSON attributes, time range, and patterns.
                  * Aggregate counts, averages, percentiles, and grouped results.
                  * Sort and limit to find top errors or slowest requests.
                  * Create charts from query results.
                  * Current CloudWatch Logs also supports OpenSearch PPL and SQL in addition to Logs Insights QL.
Sample query
fields @timestamp, @message | filter @message like /ERROR/ | sort @timestamp desc | limit 20
	18. Metric Filters vs Subscription Filters
Feature
	Purpose
	Example
	Metric Filter
	Turn matching log events into a CloudWatch metric
	Count 'ERROR' lines -> AppErrors metric
	Subscription Filter
	Continuously stream matching log events to another destination
	Logs -> Lambda / Kinesis / Firehose
	Memory trick
Metric filter = logs become numbers. Subscription filter = logs are forwarded somewhere else.
	19. Live Tail
                  * CloudWatch Logs Live Tail shows matching log events as they are ingested.
                  * Useful for short interactive troubleshooting sessions.
                  * Do not leave it running unnecessarily; it is an interactive paid feature.
                  * For historical investigation, Logs Insights is usually better.
20. Dashboards
                  * Create custom dashboards containing metric graphs, alarms, logs/query widgets, and text.
                  * CloudWatch dashboards are global rather than Region-specific.
                  * Dashboards can show metrics from multiple Regions and, with observability sharing/configuration, multiple accounts.
                  * Use dashboards for application/service views rather than dumping every infrastructure metric onto one page.
Good dashboard
Start with user/business health: request rate, error rate, latency, saturation/capacity, then drill down to infrastructure.
	21. Container Insights
  

Higher-level CloudWatch features summarize container and application telemetry.
                  * Container Insights collects and summarizes metrics/logs for ECS, EKS, Kubernetes, and supported Fargate workloads.
                  * Helps view cluster, node, pod/task, container, CPU, memory, network, and restart/failure signals.
                  * Useful when raw EC2 metrics are too low-level for container platforms.
                  * For EKS, current AWS guidance includes OTel Container Insights as the recommended approach.
22. Application Signals
                  * Application-centric observability for services and dependencies.
                  * Automatically surfaces call volume, latency, faults, errors, and service maps when instrumented/supported.
                  * Create and monitor Service Level Objectives (SLOs).
                  * Correlates with X-Ray traces and can integrate RUM/Synthetics views.
Interview priority
For beginner interviews, know metrics/logs/alarms first. Application Signals is the newer higher-level observability layer worth recognizing.
	23. CloudWatch Synthetics
                  * Synthetics canaries are scripts that continuously test endpoints/APIs from the outside.
                  * Can run as frequently as once per minute.
                  * Check availability, latency, functional flows, certificates, and UI behavior.
                  * Can collect screenshots, HAR files, logs, metrics, and optional tracing.
                  * Current runtimes include Node.js, Python, and Java with supported browser/framework combinations.
Real monitoring difference
Server CPU can be normal while login is broken. A canary can actually run the login/API journey and detect the user-visible failure.
	24. CloudWatch RUM
                  * Real User Monitoring captures telemetry from actual browser sessions.
                  * Measures client-side performance and errors experienced by real users.
                  * Useful for page-load performance, JavaScript errors, browser/device/geography analysis.
                  * Different from Synthetics: RUM observes real users; Synthetics generates controlled test traffic.
25. Synthetics vs RUM
Area
	Synthetics
	RUM
	Traffic
	Synthetic scripted requests
	Actual end-user sessions
	Goal
	Proactive availability/journey checks
	Real-user experience visibility
	Runs when no users exist
	Yes
	No
	Examples
	Login every minute, API check
	Page load, JS errors, client latency
	26. CloudWatch vs CloudTrail
Area
	CloudWatch
	CloudTrail
	Primary purpose
	Monitoring/observability
	AWS API activity/audit trail
	Question answered
	Is the app/resource healthy?
	Who changed/called what AWS API?
	Data
	Metrics, logs, alarms, telemetry
	AWS management/data events
	Example
	CPU 95%, API 5xx spike
	User deleted security group at 10:03
	Interview shortcut
CloudWatch = monitor operation. CloudTrail = audit AWS API activity.
	27. CloudWatch vs EventBridge
Area
	CloudWatch
	EventBridge
	Main role
	Observability/monitoring
	Event routing and event bus
	Triggers
	Alarms based on metrics/expressions
	Events/rules/schedules
	Historical note
	CloudWatch Events was an older name/feature area
	Amazon EventBridge is the current event service
	Integration
	Alarm state changes emit EventBridge events
	Can route those events to targets
	28. Typical Production Monitoring Strategy
                  * Infrastructure metrics: CPU, memory, disk, network, connections.
                  * Golden application signals: request rate, errors, latency, saturation.
                  * Business metrics: orders, payments, signups, failed checkouts.
                  * Logs: structured JSON with request/correlation IDs.
                  * Alarms: actionable symptoms, not every possible metric.
                  * Dashboards: service-level health first.
                  * Synthetics/RUM/Application Signals for user/application perspective.
                  * Centralize/cross-account observability for larger environments.
Most important principle
Monitor outcomes users care about, not only server resources.
	29. Troubleshooting Workflow
  

Correlate telemetry rather than looking at one graph in isolation.
1.  Start from user symptom or alarm and note exact time range.
2.  Check request rate, errors, latency, saturation/capacity metrics.
3.  Use Logs Insights around the same time range.
4.  Search request ID/correlation ID and error stack/context.
5.  Use traces/Application Signals to locate slow/failing downstream dependency.
6.  Fix the issue, then verify metrics/logs/canaries return to normal.
30. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: EC2 CPU is visible but memory is missing.
Install/configure CloudWatch Agent because memory is an OS-level metric not published by standard EC2 monitoring.
Scenario: Need alert if CPU is high for 3 of the last 5 minutes.
Create a metric alarm with 1-minute period, evaluation periods=5, datapoints-to-alarm=3, threshold > desired value.
Scenario: Need Auto Scaling when ALB load increases.
Create/consume a CloudWatch metric such as ALBRequestCountPerTarget and use an Auto Scaling target-tracking policy.
Scenario: Need to alert on ERROR lines in application logs.
Create a CloudWatch Logs metric filter that produces an error-count metric, then alarm on that metric.
Scenario: Need real-time streaming of logs to a processing pipeline.
Use a CloudWatch Logs subscription filter to Lambda/Kinesis/Firehose-supported destination.
Scenario: Need one page only when CPU AND 5xx are bad.
Use child metric alarms plus a composite alarm.
Scenario: Fixed threshold creates alerts every normal morning spike.
Consider anomaly detection or a better dynamic/business threshold.
Scenario: Users report checkout is down but infrastructure graphs look healthy.
Use business metrics, Synthetics, RUM, Logs Insights, and Application Signals/traces rather than only EC2 CPU.
Scenario: Need to find top 10 slowest requests in logs.
Use CloudWatch Logs Insights with parse/filter/sort/stats depending on structured log format.
Scenario: Need audit of who changed an AWS security group.
Use CloudTrail, not CloudWatch metrics.
31. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is Amazon CloudWatch?
Answer: CloudWatch is AWS's monitoring and observability service for metrics, logs, alarms, dashboards, and application telemetry.
2. What is a CloudWatch metric?
Answer: A numeric time-series data point such as CPUUtilization, request count, latency, or a custom business value.
3. What is a namespace?
Answer: A container/category for metrics, such as AWS/EC2 or a custom namespace like MyCompany/Payments.
4. What is a dimension?
Answer: A name/value pair that identifies a specific metric time series, for example InstanceId=i-123.
5. What are common metric statistics?
Answer: Average, Minimum, Maximum, Sum, SampleCount, and supported percentiles such as p95/p99.
6. What is a period?
Answer: The time interval over which CloudWatch aggregates/evaluates metric data, such as 60 seconds.
7. Basic vs detailed EC2 monitoring?
Answer: Basic monitoring publishes most EC2 metrics at 5-minute periods; detailed monitoring provides 1-minute metric periods and incurs additional metric charges.
8. Does EC2 automatically publish memory utilization?
Answer: No. Install CloudWatch Agent or another telemetry agent to collect memory/filesystem metrics.
9. What is CloudWatch Agent?
Answer: An agent that can collect OS metrics such as memory/disk/process data and send application/system logs to CloudWatch.
10. What is a custom metric?
Answer: An application/user-defined metric published to CloudWatch using PutMetricData, EMF, OpenTelemetry/instrumentation, or supported integrations.
11. Standard vs high-resolution metric?
Answer: Standard resolution uses 1-minute granularity; a high-resolution custom metric can use 1-second granularity.
12. What is a CloudWatch alarm?
Answer: A rule that evaluates a metric or expression against a condition over time and changes between OK, ALARM, and INSUFFICIENT_DATA.
13. What are the three alarm states?
Answer: OK, ALARM, and INSUFFICIENT_DATA.
14. What is Datapoints to Alarm?
Answer: How many breaching data points within the evaluation window are required to put the alarm into ALARM.
15. What actions can an alarm trigger?
Answer: Common actions include SNS notifications, Auto Scaling policies, and supported EC2 actions; state changes are also available through EventBridge.
16. What is a composite alarm?
Answer: An alarm whose state is calculated from the states of other alarms using Boolean logic, useful for reducing alert noise.
17. What is metric math?
Answer: Calculations across CloudWatch metrics, such as error percentage or backlog per worker, which can be graphed or alarmed on.
18. What is anomaly detection?
Answer: CloudWatch learns an expected metric band from historical behavior and can alert when values move outside that band.
19. What is a log group?
Answer: A container for related log streams that share retention, access-control, and monitoring settings.
20. What is a log stream?
Answer: A sequence of log events from a common source, such as one application instance or container.
21. What is Logs Insights?
Answer: CloudWatch's interactive log-query feature for searching, filtering, aggregating, sorting, and visualizing log data.
22. What is a metric filter?
Answer: A CloudWatch Logs filter that converts matching log events into metric data points.
23. Metric filter vs subscription filter?
Answer: Metric filter turns logs into numbers/metrics. Subscription filter forwards matching log events to another service for processing/delivery.
24. How long are CloudWatch Logs retained by default?
Answer: Indefinitely, unless you configure a retention period on the log group.
25. What is Live Tail?
Answer: An interactive CloudWatch Logs feature that shows matching log events as they are ingested in near real time.
26. What is a CloudWatch dashboard?
Answer: A customizable visual page showing metrics, alarms, logs/query widgets, and operational information. Dashboards are global, not Region-specific.
27. What is Container Insights?
Answer: CloudWatch observability for container platforms such as ECS/EKS/Kubernetes, providing aggregated metrics/logs and container-level diagnostics.
28. What is Application Signals?
Answer: A higher-level CloudWatch application-observability feature that surfaces service health, latency, errors/faults, dependency maps, traces, and SLOs.
29. What is CloudWatch Synthetics?
Answer: Scripted canaries that repeatedly test endpoints or user flows from the outside to measure availability/latency and detect failures.
30. Synthetics vs RUM?
Answer: Synthetics runs controlled test traffic; RUM records telemetry from actual users' browser sessions.
31. CloudWatch vs CloudTrail?
Answer: CloudWatch monitors performance/health; CloudTrail records AWS API activity for audit and governance.
32. CloudWatch vs EventBridge?
Answer: CloudWatch focuses on observability; EventBridge routes events. CloudWatch alarm state changes can become EventBridge events.
33. How would you alarm on application ERROR logs?
Answer: Create a metric filter that counts matching ERROR events, then create a CloudWatch alarm on the generated metric.
34. What is the most important CloudWatch interview mistake to avoid?
Answer: Do not say CloudWatch automatically gives EC2 memory/filesystem metrics. Standard EC2 monitoring does not; use the CloudWatch Agent or another collector.
35. What should a good production monitoring setup include?
Answer: Infrastructure metrics, application/business metrics, structured logs, actionable alarms, dashboards, and user/application-level signals such as Synthetics/RUM/traces when appropriate.
32. Final CloudWatch Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword

If you are short on time, revise these first
CloudWatch basics -> metrics vs logs vs alarms -> namespace/dimensions -> EC2 basic vs detailed -> CloudWatch Agent -> alarms/states/datapoints -> Logs/Logs Insights -> metric vs subscription filters -> dashboards -> CloudWatch vs CloudTrail/EventBridge -> interview Q&A.
	33. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * EC2 basic monitoring publishes most metrics at 5-minute periods; detailed monitoring uses 1-minute periods.
                  * Custom CloudWatch metrics can be high resolution with 1-second granularity; high-resolution alarms can evaluate at 10- or 30-second periods.
                  * CloudWatch Logs keeps log data indefinitely by default unless a log-group retention policy is configured.
                  * CloudWatch Logs Insights now supports Logs Insights QL plus OpenSearch PPL and SQL query languages.
                  * Application Signals provides service maps, core service health metrics, trace correlation, and SLO monitoring.
                  * Container Insights supports ECS, EKS, Kubernetes, and Fargate workloads; OTel Container Insights is current recommended guidance for EKS.
                  * CloudWatch Synthetics supports modern Node.js, Python, and Java runtimes/frameworks, and canaries can run as frequently as once per minute.
                  * CloudWatch dashboards are global and can visualize telemetry across Regions.

AWS Route 53 - Interview Study Notes
AWS Route 53 - Interview Study Notes
Beginner-Friendly to Intermediate | Full-Stack / Backend Interview Focus
Goal of these notes
Understand Route 53 from first principles: DNS resolution, domain registration, hosted zones, record types, Alias vs CNAME, TTL, routing policies, health checks/failover, AWS-resource aliases, private DNS, Resolver/hybrid DNS, DNSSEC, and common interview scenarios.
	1. What is Amazon Route 53?
Amazon Route 53 is AWS's highly available and scalable Domain Name System (DNS) service. It can register domain names, host authoritative DNS records, perform DNS health checks and traffic routing, and provide DNS resolution inside VPC/hybrid networks.
                  * Domain registration: buy/manage domain names such as example.com.
                  * Authoritative DNS: answer DNS queries for your public hosted zones.
                  * Private DNS: resolve internal names inside associated VPCs.
                  * Health checking and DNS failover.
                  * Advanced routing policies such as weighted, latency, geolocation, and geoproximity.
                  * Route 53 Resolver for VPC and hybrid DNS.
  

Route 53 often acts as the authoritative DNS service for your application's domain.
2. Why is it called Route 53?
DNS traditionally uses port 53. Route 53 combines the idea of routing users to resources with the DNS port number.
Interview one-liner
Route 53 is AWS's managed DNS service for domain registration, authoritative DNS, health-aware traffic routing, and VPC/hybrid DNS resolution.
	3. Authoritative DNS vs Recursive Resolver
Role
	What It Does
	Example
	Authoritative DNS
	Owns the final DNS records for a domain/zone
	Route 53 public hosted zone
	Recursive Resolver
	Looks up answers on behalf of clients and caches them
	ISP resolver, corporate resolver, Route 53 Resolver
	Simple flow
Browser asks recursive resolver -> resolver asks DNS hierarchy/Route 53 authoritative servers -> answer is cached for the record's TTL.
	4. Domain Registration vs Hosted Zone
Concept
	Purpose
	Can Be Separate?
	Domain registration
	Ownership/registration of example.com
	Yes
	Hosted zone
	DNS records for a namespace such as example.com
	Yes
	                  * You can register a domain with Route 53 and host DNS elsewhere.
                  * You can register a domain elsewhere and host DNS in Route 53.
                  * For public DNS, update the registrar to use the Route 53 hosted zone's assigned name servers.
Common confusion
Buying example.com and creating DNS records for example.com are related but distinct tasks.
	5. Public vs Private Hosted Zones
  

Public hosted zones answer internet DNS; private hosted zones provide DNS inside associated VPCs/hybrid paths.
                  * Public hosted zone: DNS records are visible through public authoritative DNS.
                  * Private hosted zone: records are only resolved from associated VPCs or connected hybrid DNS using Route 53 Resolver.
                  * Private hosted zones are useful for names such as db.internal.example.com or service.corp.
                  * One private hosted zone can be associated with multiple VPCs, including supported cross-account configurations.
6. Name Servers (NS) and Delegation
                  * When you create a public hosted zone, Route 53 assigns authoritative name servers.
                  * Route 53 public hosted zones normally receive four authoritative name servers.
                  * The domain registrar's NS/delegation settings must point to those name servers.
                  * You can delegate a subdomain to a separate hosted zone by creating NS records in the parent zone.
Example
example.com hosted in one zone; dev.example.com can be delegated to a different Route 53 hosted zone/account using NS records.
	7. Important DNS Record Types
Record
	Meaning
	Example Use
	A
	Name -> IPv4 address
	app.example.com -> 203.0.113.10
	AAAA
	Name -> IPv6 address
	IPv6 application endpoint
	CNAME
	Name -> another DNS name
	www.example.com -> vendor.example.net
	MX
	Mail servers + priority
	Google Workspace / mail provider
	TXT
	Text metadata
	SPF, DKIM verification, domain validation
	NS
	Authoritative name servers
	Zone/subdomain delegation
	SOA
	Zone authority/metadata
	Automatically created zone metadata
	CAA
	Which CAs may issue certs
	Restrict certificate authorities
	SRV
	Service discovery metadata
	Service/port records
	PTR
	Reverse DNS: IP -> name
	Reverse lookup
	HTTPS / SVCB
	Modern service-binding metadata
	HTTP protocol/endpoint hints
	8. A Record vs CNAME
Area
	A / AAAA
	CNAME
	Points to
	IP address
	Another DNS name
	IPv4/IPv6
	A / AAAA respectively
	Target name resolves onward
	Zone apex
	Allowed
	CNAME not allowed
	Typical use
	Fixed IP or Route 53 Alias record type A/AAAA
	Subdomain mapped to external hostname
	9. Alias Record vs CNAME
  

Alias solves important AWS DNS cases that a normal CNAME cannot.
                  * Alias is a Route 53 extension, not a standalone DNS record type.
                  * You normally create an A or AAAA record and choose Alias = Yes.
                  * Alias can target selected AWS resources such as CloudFront, ELB, API Gateway, S3 website endpoints, and another Route 53 record.
                  * Alias can be created at the zone apex, for example example.com.
                  * A CNAME cannot be created at the zone apex.
                  * For an Alias to an AWS resource, you do not set the TTL yourself; Route 53 uses the resource's TTL behavior.
Interview answer
example.com -> ALB should normally use Route 53 Alias A/AAAA. www.example.com -> external-vendor.example.net can use CNAME.
	10. TTL (Time to Live)
TTL tells recursive DNS resolvers how long they may cache a DNS answer before asking Route 53 again.
                  * High TTL: fewer DNS queries and more caching, but changes propagate more slowly.
                  * Low TTL: faster reaction to record changes, but more DNS query traffic.
                  * Before a planned migration, temporarily lowering TTL can help clients refresh sooner.
                  * You cannot force every resolver/client to discard an answer that it already cached until its TTL expires.
Example
TTL 300 = resolver may cache the record for 5 minutes.
	11. Route 53 Routing Policies
  

Current Route 53 documentation lists eight routing policies.
Policy
	When to Use
	Simple
	One normal resource / basic DNS.
	Weighted
	Split DNS answers by relative weight.
	Latency
	Choose the AWS Region with the best latency for the user.
	Failover
	Active-passive primary/secondary.
	Geolocation
	Route based on user's geographic location.
	Geoproximity
	Route based on user/resource location and optionally shift boundaries with bias.
	IP-based
	Route based on user/client CIDR mappings you define.
	Multivalue Answer
	Return up to 8 healthy records for DNS-level load sharing.
	12. Simple Routing
                  * Use when one resource performs the function for the DNS name.
                  * You can place multiple values in a simple non-alias record, but Route 53 does not health-check those values as individual simple-routing endpoints.
                  * If multiple values are returned, client/resolver behavior determines which address gets used.
Use case
A small website has one public server IP: www.example.com -> 203.0.113.10.
	13. Weighted Routing
                  * Create multiple records with the same name/type and assign relative weights.
                  * Traffic share is approximately weight / total weight.
                  * Useful for canary releases, migrations, active-active traffic split, or gradually moving traffic.
                  * Weight 0 can be useful in controlled routing setups.
                  * Because DNS answers are cached, observed traffic percentages are approximate.
  

Weighted controls proportions; latency optimizes response location; geolocation enforces geographic mapping.
Example
v1 weight 90, v2 weight 10 -> Route 53 returns v1 roughly 90% and v2 roughly 10% across DNS answers, not exactly every 10 requests.
	14. Latency-Based Routing
                  * Create records for resources in multiple AWS Regions.
                  * Route 53 chooses the Region that provides the lowest AWS-measured latency for the DNS querier.
                  * Useful for globally distributed applications.
                  * The user is not necessarily routed to the geographically closest Region; latency and geography are different concepts.
Interview distinction
Latency routing = performance. Geolocation routing = explicit geography policy.
	15. Geolocation Routing
                  * Route based on the geographic origin of the DNS query.
                  * Can specify continent, country, and U.S. state.
                  * More-specific matching location takes precedence over broader overlapping location.
                  * Create a default record for locations Route 53 cannot identify or that do not match configured locations.
                  * Useful for localization, content rights, regulatory/business rules, or predictable regional routing.
16. Geoproximity Routing
                  * Routes based on location of users and resources.
                  * AWS resources can be specified by AWS Region or supported Local Zone location.
                  * Non-AWS resources can use latitude/longitude.
                  * Bias lets you expand or shrink the geographic area routed to a resource.
Geolocation vs geoproximity
Geolocation says 'users from this location go here.' Geoproximity says 'send users toward the nearby resource, and optionally shift the boundary with bias.'
	17. IP-Based Routing
                  * Create CIDR collections mapping known client IP ranges to specific endpoints.
                  * Useful when you know your customer/ISP network ranges and want routing optimized for cost/performance.
                  * Can override more general geographic assumptions with your own network knowledge.
Example
A video provider knows users from ISP-A CIDR ranges perform better through endpoint X, so it maps those CIDRs to X.
	18. Multivalue Answer Routing
                  * Create multiple records for the same name, often one per web server/IP.
                  * Associate health checks with records.
                  * Route 53 returns up to eight healthy records in a response.
                  * It is DNS-level load sharing, but it is not a replacement for an ALB/NLB.
                  * If the resolver caches several returned IPs, the client can try another when one endpoint fails.
Interview line
Multivalue can return several healthy addresses; ALB is a real connection/request load balancer with much richer traffic management.
	19. Failover Routing
  

Failover routing is the standard active-passive Route 53 pattern.
                  * Create PRIMARY and SECONDARY records for the same name/type.
                  * Associate a health check with primary or use Evaluate Target Health for supported Alias targets.
                  * When primary is unhealthy, Route 53 returns the secondary record.
                  * DNS TTL/cache means failover is not instantaneous for every client.
Disaster recovery example
Primary: ALB in Mumbai. Secondary: ALB/static recovery site in another Region. Route 53 switches DNS answers when primary health fails.
	20. Health Checks
                  * Route 53 can monitor public endpoints using HTTP, HTTPS, or TCP health checks.
                  * Endpoint checks can run every 30 seconds or optionally every 10 seconds.
                  * HTTP/HTTPS health checks can optionally search the response for a specific string.
                  * Calculated health checks combine multiple child health checks.
                  * CloudWatch-alarm health checks let Route 53 base health on CloudWatch metrics.
                  * Health-check status can be monitored/alarmed in CloudWatch.
Private endpoint caveat
Route 53's external health checkers cannot directly reach a private-only endpoint. Use an appropriate CloudWatch-alarm-based health check or another internal health signal instead.
	21. Evaluate Target Health
Alias records to selected AWS resources can use Evaluate Target Health instead of a separate Route 53 endpoint health check.
                  * Common with Elastic Load Balancing aliases.
                  * Route 53 considers the health of the AWS target resource when selecting DNS answers.
                  * For ALB/NLB, target-group health contributes to whether the load balancer alias is considered healthy.
Interview pattern
Route 53 Alias -> ALB + Evaluate Target Health is simpler than pointing a manual health check at every EC2 target.
	22. Route 53 + ALB / CloudFront / API Gateway / S3
  

Alias records give your application a friendly DNS name without hardcoding AWS-generated hostnames.
                  * example.com -> CloudFront distribution Alias.
                  * api.example.com -> Application Load Balancer Alias.
                  * service.example.com -> API Gateway custom domain Alias.
                  * example.com -> S3 website endpoint Alias when using S3 static website hosting.
                  * AWS-managed endpoint IPs can change; Alias tracks supported AWS resources automatically.
ALB best practice
Point DNS to the ALB DNS name using a Route 53 Alias. Never copy one current ALB IP into an A record.
	23. Route 53 vs CloudFront vs ALB
Service
	Primary Job
	Layer
	Typical Flow
	Route 53
	DNS name resolution / traffic policy
	DNS
	example.com -> CloudFront/ALB
	CloudFront
	Global CDN, caching, edge security
	HTTP edge/CDN
	CloudFront -> origin
	ALB
	Regional HTTP application load balancing
	Layer 7
	ALB -> EC2/ECS targets
	Common stack
Route 53 -> CloudFront -> ALB -> Auto Scaling/ECS -> application.
	24. Route 53 Resolver
Route 53 Resolver provides recursive DNS resolution for VPCs and enables hybrid DNS between AWS and on-premises networks.
                  * VPC workloads normally use the Amazon-provided Route 53 Resolver automatically.
                  * Resolver rules can forward selected domains to other DNS servers.
                  * Resolver query logging can record DNS queries for troubleshooting/security.
                  * Resolver DNS Firewall can filter outbound DNS queries using domain lists/rules.
                  * Route 53 Profiles can package/share DNS configurations such as private hosted zones, Resolver rules, and DNS Firewall rule groups across VPCs/accounts.
  

Inbound and outbound Resolver endpoints are key hybrid-DNS interview concepts.
25. Inbound vs Outbound Resolver Endpoints
Endpoint
	Direction
	Use
	Inbound
	On-prem/network -> AWS Resolver
	Resolve private hosted-zone/AWS names from external network
	Outbound
	AWS VPC Resolver -> on-prem DNS
	Forward selected corporate/internal domains from AWS to on-prem
	Memory trick
Inbound means DNS queries come IN to AWS. Outbound means DNS queries go OUT from AWS to your network.
	26. Split-Horizon / Split-View DNS
You can use the same domain name in a public hosted zone and a private hosted zone so internal and internet clients receive different answers.
                  * Public: api.example.com -> public CloudFront/ALB.
                  * Private: api.example.com -> internal ALB/private endpoint.
                  * VPC-associated clients resolve the private hosted-zone answer.
Use case
Internal users access a private route while external users access the public edge, both using the same hostname.
	27. DNSSEC
                  * Route 53 supports DNSSEC signing for public hosted zones.
                  * DNSSEC uses cryptographic signatures to let validating resolvers verify that DNS responses are authentic and unmodified.
                  * Route 53 Resolver can perform DNSSEC validation for recursive queries.
                  * DNSSEC does not encrypt DNS traffic; it provides authenticity/integrity.
DNSSEC vs HTTPS
DNSSEC protects DNS-answer integrity. HTTPS/TLS protects the application connection and data in transit. They solve different problems.
	28. Common Interview Scenarios
Scenario: Need example.com to point to an ALB.
Create a Route 53 Alias A/AAAA record to the ALB. Do not hardcode ALB IPs.
Scenario: Need www.example.com to point to an external SaaS hostname.
Use a CNAME for the subdomain if the provider requires a hostname target.
Scenario: Need 90% traffic to v1 and 10% to v2.
Use Weighted routing with relative weights 90 and 10.
Scenario: Need users routed to the AWS Region with best network latency.
Use Latency-based routing.
Scenario: Need users in India to one endpoint and Europe to another.
Use Geolocation routing.
Scenario: Need active-passive disaster recovery.
Use Failover records plus health checks/Evaluate Target Health.
Scenario: Need private db.internal.example.com inside a VPC.
Create a Private Hosted Zone associated with the VPC.
Scenario: On-prem DNS must resolve AWS private hostnames.
Use a Route 53 Resolver inbound endpoint.
Scenario: EC2 inside AWS must resolve corp.internal hosted on-prem.
Use a Resolver outbound endpoint plus forwarding rule.
Scenario: DNS changed but some users still reach old endpoint.
Check TTL/resolver/client caching and propagation/delegation.
29. Troubleshooting Checklist
1.  Check the domain registrar NS records point to the correct public hosted-zone name servers.
2.  Use dig/nslookup to inspect the exact DNS response and record type.
3.  Check whether the record is in the correct public or private hosted zone.
4.  Check record name carefully: apex vs www/subdomain.
5.  Check Alias target or CNAME value.
6.  Check TTL and local/recursive resolver caches after a DNS change.
7.  For routing policies, check record identifiers, weights/regions/locations, health-check associations, and record type/name consistency.
8.  For failover, check health-check status and whether cached old answers remain.
9.  For private DNS, check VPC association, DNS support/hostnames, Resolver rules, endpoints, and network reachability.
10.  For hybrid Resolver, check endpoint SGs, routing, port 53 TCP/UDP, and forwarding rules.
30. Interview Questions with Answers
1. What is Amazon Route 53?
Answer: Route 53 is AWS's managed DNS service. It provides domain registration, authoritative public/private DNS, health-aware traffic routing, and Route 53 Resolver for VPC/hybrid DNS.
2. Why is it called Route 53?
Answer: DNS uses port 53, and the service routes users to application resources.
3. What is a hosted zone?
Answer: A container for DNS records for a domain or DNS namespace, such as example.com.
4. Public hosted zone vs private hosted zone?
Answer: A public hosted zone answers DNS on the internet. A private hosted zone answers only from associated VPCs or connected hybrid DNS through Route 53 Resolver.
5. Domain registration vs hosted zone?
Answer: Registration establishes ownership of the domain; the hosted zone stores DNS records. They can be managed by different providers.
6. What is an A record?
Answer: A DNS record mapping a name to an IPv4 address.
7. What is an AAAA record?
Answer: A DNS record mapping a name to an IPv6 address.
8. What is a CNAME?
Answer: A standard DNS record that maps one DNS name to another DNS name.
9. Why can't CNAME be used at the zone apex?
Answer: DNS standards do not allow a CNAME at the top of a DNS namespace because the apex also needs records such as NS and SOA.
10. What is a Route 53 Alias?
Answer: A Route 53 extension that lets A/AAAA records point to selected AWS resources or another Route 53 record. It can be used at the zone apex.
11. Alias vs CNAME?
Answer: Alias can be used at the zone apex and can point to supported AWS resources; CNAME is standard DNS, works for non-apex names, and points to another hostname.
12. What is TTL?
Answer: The number of seconds a recursive DNS resolver may cache a DNS record before asking the authoritative DNS server again.
13. High TTL vs low TTL?
Answer: High TTL reduces DNS queries but makes changes take longer to be seen. Low TTL speeds future refreshes but creates more DNS queries.
14. What routing policies does Route 53 support?
Answer: Simple, Weighted, Latency, Failover, Geolocation, Geoproximity, IP-based, and Multivalue Answer.
15. What is Weighted routing?
Answer: It returns different resources based on relative weights, useful for canary deployment or gradually shifting traffic.
16. Is 90/10 weighted routing exactly 9 out of every 10 HTTP requests?
Answer: No. Route 53 controls DNS answers, which are cached by resolvers, so actual application request percentages are approximate.
17. What is Latency routing?
Answer: Route users to the configured AWS Region that Route 53 expects to provide the lowest latency.
18. Latency vs geolocation routing?
Answer: Latency optimizes performance. Geolocation follows explicit user geography rules.
19. What is Geolocation routing?
Answer: Route DNS queries based on the geographic location they originate from, such as continent, country, or US state.
20. What is Geoproximity routing?
Answer: Route based on proximity of users and resources, with optional bias to expand or shrink a resource's traffic region.
21. What is IP-based routing?
Answer: Route based on client CIDR ranges that you define, useful when you have your own knowledge of customer/ISP networks.
22. What is Multivalue routing?
Answer: Route 53 can return up to eight healthy records for a DNS name. It provides simple DNS-level load sharing but is not a replacement for a load balancer.
23. What is Failover routing?
Answer: An active-passive model with primary and secondary records; Route 53 returns the secondary when the primary is considered unhealthy.
24. What is a Route 53 health check?
Answer: A health monitor used by Route 53 to determine whether an endpoint or calculated/CloudWatch-based condition is healthy.
25. Can Route 53 directly health-check a private-only EC2 endpoint?
Answer: Public Route 53 health checkers cannot directly reach private-only endpoints. Use a suitable CloudWatch-alarm-based health check or another reachable health signal.
26. What is Evaluate Target Health?
Answer: An Alias option where Route 53 uses the health of the selected AWS target, commonly an ELB load balancer, when choosing DNS answers.
27. Route 53 vs ALB?
Answer: Route 53 is DNS and returns endpoints/addresses. ALB receives actual HTTP connections and load-balances requests across targets.
28. Route 53 vs CloudFront?
Answer: Route 53 resolves the domain name. CloudFront is a global CDN/edge proxy that serves HTTP content and can be the DNS target.
29. How should example.com point to an ALB?
Answer: Use a Route 53 Alias A/AAAA record to the ALB DNS name.
30. What is Route 53 Resolver?
Answer: AWS's recursive DNS resolver for VPCs, with features for private DNS, hybrid forwarding, query logs, DNS Firewall, and Resolver endpoints.
31. Inbound vs outbound Resolver endpoints?
Answer: Inbound lets external/on-prem DNS query AWS Resolver. Outbound lets AWS Resolver forward selected DNS queries to on-prem/external DNS servers.
32. What is split-horizon DNS?
Answer: Using the same hostname with different public and private DNS answers depending on where the query originates.
33. What is DNSSEC?
Answer: A DNS security mechanism that cryptographically signs DNS data so validating resolvers can verify authenticity/integrity. It does not encrypt DNS.
34. Why might a DNS change not appear immediately?
Answer: Recursive resolver, OS, browser, or application caches may still hold the old answer until TTL expires.
35. What is the most important Route 53 interview rule?
Answer: Remember that Route 53 is DNS, not a proxy/load balancer. Routing policies choose DNS answers, and DNS caching affects how quickly traffic changes take effect.
31. Final Route 53 Interview Cheat Sheet
Keyword
	One-Line Meaning
	Route 53
	Managed AWS DNS + domain registration + Resolver
	Hosted Zone

	Public Zone
	Internet-authoritative DNS
	Private Zone

	A

	AAAA
	Name -> IPv6
	CNAME
	Name -> another DNS name
	Alias

	TTL

	Normal single-resource routing

	Approximate traffic split
	Latency

	Failover

If you are short on time, revise these first
Route 53 basics -> hosted zones -> A/AAAA/CNAME -> Alias vs CNAME -> TTL -> all routing policies -> Weighted vs Latency vs Geolocation -> Failover + health checks -> Route 53 + ALB/CloudFront -> Resolver inbound/outbound -> interview Q&A.
	32. Current AWS Notes Worth Remembering
                  * Current Route 53 docs list eight routing policies: Simple, Weighted, Latency, Failover, Geolocation, Geoproximity, IP-based, and Multivalue Answer.
                  * Alias records remain the standard Route 53 way to map a zone apex to supported AWS resources such as ALB or CloudFront.
                  * Route 53 now supports newer DNS record types including HTTPS/SVCB in addition to traditional records.
                  * Route 53 Resolver supports inbound/outbound hybrid DNS endpoints, DNS Firewall, query logging, and reusable Route 53 Profiles.
                  * Public hosted zones can use DNSSEC signing, and Route 53 Resolver can perform DNSSEC validation.
                  * Multivalue routing returns up to eight healthy records but is not a replacement for Elastic Load Balancing.

Topics
In interviews, the most popular AWS services to know are:
                  * EC2 — virtual servers
                  * S3 — object/file storage
                  * RDS — managed relational databases
                  * IAM — users, roles, permissions
                  * VPC — private networking
                  * ELB / ALB — load balancing
                  * Auto Scaling — automatically scale EC2 instances
                  * CloudWatch — logs, metrics, alarms
                  * Lambda — serverless functions
                  * API Gateway — expose/manage APIs
                  * SQS — message queue
                  * SNS — pub/sub notifications
                  * DynamoDB — managed NoSQL database
                  * ElastiCache — Redis/Memcached caching
                  * Route 53 — DNS
                  * CloudFront — CDN
                  * ECS / EKS — container orchestration
                  * Secrets Manager — store secrets/passwords
                  * KMS — encryption keys
                  * CodePipeline / CodeBuild / CodeDeploy — CI/CD


For your full-stack interview, prioritize these first:
EC2, S3, RDS, IAM, VPC, ALB, Auto Scaling, CloudWatch, Lambda, SQS, SNS, DynamoDB, ElastiCache.




Very important: EC2, S3, IAM, VPC, RDS
Important: ELB, Auto Scaling, CloudWatch, Route 53
Good to know: Lambda, SQS, SNS, CloudFront, EBS


q/a
Q ) “We have a Spring Boot application with millions of users. How would you deploy it on AWS?”
A solid answer would be:
I would put the application behind an Application Load Balancer. The Spring Boot services could run on EC2 instances or ECS inside private subnets. An Auto Scaling Group would add or remove instances according to traffic. I would use RDS for relational data and possibly ElastiCache/Redis for caching. Static files would go to S3. IAM roles would provide service-to-service permissions without storing credentials. CloudWatch would handle logs, metrics and alerts. For asynchronous operations such as sending emails or processing jobs, I could use SQS.
